The transition between cooperative and defective regimes is sharper with migration, but only group competition and fitness-based migration expand the parameter space where cooperation and punishment are stable. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of strategies, not efficiency or group payoff, but higher frequencies of cooperation and punishment imply higher group payoffs relative to defection-dominated regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task: in PGG-like environments with punishment, the presence of group competition or fitness-based migration mechanisms can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation and punishment, and thus are likely to increase efficiency or group payoff compared to control games without punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so the evidence is based on the frequencies of cooperative and punishing strategies. The results suggest that, for the same game design dimensions, enabling punishment in a context with group competition or fitness-based migration will likely lead to higher efficiency than in well-mixed or constant migration settings. The findings are most relevant for models with large group sizes, high mutation and migration rates, and community structure.

- source: Guo, P., Wang, X. N., & Zhang, D. (2023). Optimization of supervision mechanism for public cooperation considering limited supervision ability and heterogeneous preferences. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that targeted punishment via the HPS mechanism increases the fraction of cooperators compared to random supervision, especially at higher synergy factors and with well-chosen exemption list parameters. Increasing supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list capacity generally increases cooperation rates. Moderately long observation periods and short removal times also promote cooperation. The results are robust across different network structures. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that targeted punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, and that design parameters such as supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list management are important moderators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results may be useful for predicting the direction of change in cooperation rates under different punishment regimes, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Du, K., Fan, R. G., Xue, H., Wang, Y. T., & Bao, X. G. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game based on heterogenous social preferences and dynamic punishment. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) heterogeneous social preferences (especially altruism) promote higher cooperation rates in spatial PGGs; (2) dynamic punishment mechanisms are more effective than static punishment in increasing cooperation and addressing second-order free-riding; (3) the enhancement factor (r_e, analogous to MPCR) is a key moderator—when it is low, cooperation is hard to sustain even with punishment, but when it is high, cooperation is high even without punishment; (4) inequality aversion slightly inhibits cooperation, and reciprocity has a mixed effect depending on r_e. All findings are based on simulation of agent-based models, with the main outcome being the proportion of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that enabling punishment (especially dynamic punishment) increases cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, particularly when the enhancement factor is moderate. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment and social preference heterogeneity affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings. The model's focus on spatial structure and dynamic punishment is relevant for games with similar features.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2012). Leaving the loners alone: Evolution of cooperation in the presence of antisocial punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary success of cooperation and punishment in public goods games with voluntary participation depends critically on whether loners (abstainers) can be punished or can punish others. If loners are truly isolated (neither punish nor are punished), cooperators who punish defectors become dominant in the population, even when antisocial punishment is allowed. If loners can be punished, cooperation and punishment do not evolve, and the population is split among cooperators, defectors, and loners, with no strategy dominating. The results are robust across different strategy sets and selection intensities. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the abundance of strategies as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the ability to punish or be punished, especially for abstainers (loners), is a critical moderator of whether punishment can support cooperation in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that if the game design allows abstainers to be punished, punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or cooperation. If abstainers are truly isolated, punishment can support the dominance of cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding which game design features (especially the treatment of abstainers) are likely to enable or undermine the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation.

- source: Brandts, J., & Fatas, E. (2012). THE PUZZLE OF SOCIAL PREFERENCES. *Revista Internacional De Sociologia*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper reviews experimental evidence showing that in public goods games, the introduction of punishment mechanisms typically increases contributions and cooperation. However, it also highlights that punishment is costly and not always optimal from a collective (efficiency) perspective, especially when antisocial punishment or counter-punishment occurs. The effectiveness of punishment is robust to parameter changes (e.g., punishment cost/effectiveness), but can be undermined by incomplete information or network structure. Communication and group selection also enhance cooperation. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral regularities and mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that enabling punishment in public goods games generally increases cooperation and contributions, but warns that the net effect on efficiency depends on the costs of punishment and the prevalence of antisocial punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when punishment is targeted at free riders, costs are not excessive, and antisocial punishment is rare. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value is mainly in contextualizing and interpreting empirical findings from other sources.

- source: Akdeniz, A., & van Veelen, M. (2021). The evolution of morality and the role of commitment. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that commitment is a central mechanism for the evolution of cooperation, punishment, and other moral behaviors in humans. It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that commitment to punishment can sustain cooperation in public goods games and prisoners' dilemmas, even when punishment is costly. The authors contend that standard models based on population structure or repetition do not fully explain observed human behavior, and that commitment (and the ability to recognize commitment in others) is necessary for the evolution of costly punishment and conditional cooperation. The paper discusses the implications for efficiency and group welfare in a theoretical sense but does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical results on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment can sustain cooperation in public goods games and similar environments, emphasizing the role of commitment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that the presence of punishment (and the ability to commit to punishment) can change the strategic environment and potentially increase cooperation, but it does not allow for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of commitment and recognizability of commitment for punishment to be effective.

- source: Barrett, J. (2020). Punishment and disagreement in the state of nature. *Economics and Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that experimental public goods games with punishment show that punishment can stabilize cooperation when individuals agree on norms and punishment costs are low. However, when there is moral disagreement or opportunities for counter-punishment, cooperation breaks down and conflict can escalate. The analysis is theoretical and interpretive, using published experimental results as illustrative evidence. The paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public goods games, especially when punishment is low-cost and there is agreement on norms. However, it also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment is undermined by moral disagreement and counter-punishment, which can lead to conflict and breakdown of cooperation. For the prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment is likely to increase cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) in standard public goods games under conditions of agreement and low punishment cost, but that this effect is fragile to disagreement and retaliation dynamics. The paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value is mainly in highlighting moderators and mechanisms.

- source: Rosas, A. (2010). Evolutionary game theory meets social science: Is there a unifying rule for human cooperation?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that cooperative strategies in public goods games and related social dilemmas are governed by a common conditional rule: cooperate with those of good reputation and enforce (by withholding benefits or costly punishment) against those of bad reputation. The choice between withholding benefits and costly punishment depends on the structure of the game—costly punishment is more relevant in public goods games where defectors cannot be targeted directly. The paper reviews experimental evidence showing that combining costly punishment with indirect reciprocity can increase cooperation at lower cost than punishment alone, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes. Theoretical and experimental evidence suggests that the same reputation rules (e.g., SCORING, SIMPLE STANDING, STERN JUDGING) operate across frameworks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation (and potentially efficiency) in public goods games depends on whether withholding benefits is a viable enforcement mechanism. It suggests that costly punishment is most effective and necessary in public goods games where direct targeting of defectors is not possible. However, the paper does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. It is most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be effective as an enforcement mechanism, and for mapping the conceptual relationships between different enforcement strategies.

- source: Karakostas, A., Kocher, M. G., Matzat, D., Rau, H. A., & Riewe, G. (2023). The team allocator game: Allocation power in public goods games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper experimentally compares standard public goods games (VCM) to versions with a team allocator (TAG) who can redistribute the public good among team members. Three production technologies are tested: linear, weakest-link, and best-shot. In the linear case, the team allocator increases both contributions and group efficiency (total profit), as the allocator uses allocation power to reward contributors and sanction free-riders without explicit punishment costs. In the best-shot case, the allocator increases contributions but causes over-contribution and reduces efficiency due to coordination problems. In the weakest-link case, the allocator has no significant effect on contributions or efficiency. Thus, the effect of hierarchical allocation power as an implicit sanction/reward mechanism depends crucially on the production technology.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of introducing a sanctioning/reward mechanism (via a team allocator) on efficiency depends on the public goods production function. In linear PGGs, such mechanisms can increase efficiency, but in best-shot games, they may reduce efficiency due to over-contribution. In weakest-link games, the effect is negligible. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the production technology (linear, best-shot, weakest-link), the presence of an allocator with discretionary redistribution power (as a proxy for punishment/reward), and the absence of explicit punishment costs. Control efficiency (VCM) is a strong predictor, but the treatment effect varies by technology. The findings suggest that the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms is positive in linear games, negative in best-shot, and neutral in weakest-link games, even when the mechanism is costless to the allocator.

- source: Duong, M. H., Durbac, C. M., & Han, T. A. (2024). Cost Optimisation of Individual-Based Institutional Reward Incentives for Promoting Cooperation in Finite Populations. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the expected total cost to the institution for achieving a target cooperation level in finite-population PGGs depends strongly on the threshold for incentive application (t), the strength of selection (β), mutation rate (μ), and the type of incentive (reward, punishment, hybrid). For small thresholds (t=1), the cost function is always non-decreasing in the per-capita incentive cost, and explicit formulas are derived. For larger thresholds, the cost function can exhibit non-monotonic behavior and phase transitions, especially at high selection strength. Numerical results show that both reward and punishment incentives can be optimized for cost, but the optimal cost and its behavior depend on the game parameters and incentive scheme. The paper provides a framework for predicting the institutional cost required to achieve a desired cooperation level, given the game design and population parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence on how the cost of institutional incentives (reward, punishment, hybrid) required to achieve a target cooperation level in PGG-like environments depends on game design dimensions such as player count, group size, marginal per-capita return, and the structure of the incentive scheme (threshold t, cost per unit, effectiveness). While the main outcome is the institutional cost rather than group efficiency, the results are directly relevant for predicting how changes in punishment or reward design (e.g., cost, threshold, effectiveness) will affect the efficiency of institutional intervention. The paper is most informative for settings where the goal is to optimize the cost of achieving cooperation, rather than to maximize group payoff per se. It provides explicit formulas and numerical results that can be used to inform predictions about the efficiency of punishment or reward interventions, conditional on game design and baseline cooperation levels.

- source: Lippert, S., & Tremewan, J. (2021). Pledge-and-review in the laboratory. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares a standard two-player public goods game (PG) to three pledge-and-review (P&R) variants: fixed disagreement payoffs (F), heterogeneous/certainty (C), and uncertainty (U). In all P&R treatments, after pledges are made, both players must vote to accept the pledges for them to be implemented; otherwise, both receive a disagreement payoff. Efficiency (average payoffs as a fraction of the full cooperation benchmark) is significantly higher in all P&R treatments than in the control PG game. The efficiency gain is persistent in the fixed disagreement payoff treatment, with no decline over 20 rounds, while some decline is observed in the other P&R treatments. There is no significant difference in efficiency between the three P&R variants. The mechanism allows conditional cooperators to avoid the typical decline in cooperation seen in repeated public goods games. The P&R institution functions as a form of second-party punishment (via voting down low pledges), but with endogenous costs and without explicit costly punishment tokens.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adding a pledge-and-review (P&R) mechanism—where players can veto low contributions after seeing pledges—substantially increases efficiency in a two-player, 20-round public goods game with continuous contributions and no communication. The effect is robust to variations in disagreement payoffs (fixed, heterogeneous, uncertain). For prediction, if the control (PG) efficiency is known, enabling a P&R-style review stage is likely to increase efficiency by about 10% of the full cooperation benchmark, with the effect persisting over repeated rounds (at least in the fixed disagreement payoff case). The P&R mechanism is not identical to standard costly punishment (no explicit punishment tokens), but functions similarly by allowing players to block low contributions at a cost. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, continuous-contribution games without chat, with full feedback, and a review/veto stage after pledges.

- source: Gintis, H., Smith, E. A., & Bowles, S. (2001). Costly signaling and cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player public goods game, honest signaling of quality by providing a public good (or by punishing defectors) can be an evolutionarily stable strategy, provided that the cost of signaling is lower for high-quality individuals and that the benefits of alliances or partnerships are sufficiently high. The signaling equilibrium yields higher average group payoffs than the non-signaling equilibrium. The model also shows that punishment can function as a costly signal, and that under certain conditions, prosocial signals (including punishment) are more likely to evolve than neutral or antisocial signals. The model provides explicit conditions for when signaling equilibria exist and are stable, and analyzes the evolutionary dynamics leading to these equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling costly signaling (including punishment) in public goods games can increase group efficiency and average payoff, even in the absence of repeated interactions or reciprocity. The model is directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where punishment is interpreted as a costly signal of quality. The findings suggest that, when the cost structure and benefit parameters are appropriate, introducing punishment (as a form of signaling) should increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of the cost differential between high- and low-quality individuals, the size of the group, and the benefits of alliances in determining the stability and efficiency of the signaling equilibrium. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct experimental evidence.

- source: Micheli, L., Stallen, M., & Sanfey, A. G. (2021). The Effect of Centralized Financial and Social Incentives on Cooperative Behavior and Its Underlying Neural Mechanisms. *Brain Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: Across two lab experiments (one with fMRI), the prospect of centralized, probabilistic financial or social incentives (either rewards or punishments) substantially increases the percentage of tokens contributed to the group pot in a public goods game, compared to a no-incentive baseline. Financial incentives have a stronger effect than social incentives, but both are effective. There is no significant difference between the effect of rewards and punishments on contribution rates. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, so the findings are most directly about contribution behavior rather than payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing centralized, probabilistic punishment (and/or reward) mechanisms increases contribution rates in a standard public goods game, with financial incentives being more effective than social incentives. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency effects. In typical PGGs, higher contribution rates are associated with higher efficiency, so the results suggest that enabling punishment (or reward) would likely increase efficiency relative to a no-incentive baseline, especially when the incentives are financial and administered centrally. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, probabilistic centralized punishment, no chat, continuous contribution) are well specified, making the study informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar lab PGGs, but the lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation.

- source: Galbiati, R., & Vertova, P. (2008). Obligations and cooperative behaviour in public good games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that, for a given structure of low, fixed marginal incentives (probabilistic monitoring, penalty, and reward), increasing the minimum required contribution (the 'obligation') leads to higher average contributions in a repeated public good game. This effect is significant only when the obligation is set high (80% of endowment); a low obligation (40%) does not significantly increase contributions relative to no obligation. Contributions decline over time in all conditions, but the rate of decline is slower with higher obligations. Unexpected increases in the minimum contribution obligation cause a temporary jump in contributions, while decreases do not reverse the downward trend. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public good games with low-probability, low-magnitude punishment and reward, increasing the minimum required contribution (obligation) can increase average contributions, especially when the obligation is set high. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that, under similar game parameters (6 players, 10 rounds, continuous contribution, no chat, fixed MPCR, probabilistic monitoring, anonymous play), introducing or raising a minimum contribution obligation (with fixed marginal incentives) will likely increase contributions, but may not sustain them over time. The effect on efficiency would depend on the cost of monitoring and the penalty/reward structure, which are not analyzed in terms of group payoff. The findings are most relevant for predicting contribution behavior, not efficiency.

- source: Li, S. L., Du, C. P., Li, X. X., Shen, C., & Shi, L. (2024). Antisocial peer exclusion does not eliminate the effectiveness of prosocial peer exclusion in structured populations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in structured populations, prosocial peer exclusion can uphold cooperation even in the presence of antisocial peer exclusion, except when exclusion costs are low. Antisocial peer exclusion alone suppresses cooperation, but when both types coexist, prosocial exclusion remains effective at sustaining cooperation through complex coexistence and cyclic dominance patterns. The results are reported in terms of cooperation rates and strategy frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are robust across different network sizes and parameter sweeps, but are based on theoretical simulations rather than empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that prosocial peer exclusion can maintain high levels of cooperation in spatial public goods games, even when antisocial exclusion is possible, except at low exclusion costs. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation rates and not as efficiency or group payoff, the direct quantitative effect of exclusion on efficiency cannot be extracted. The results suggest that, in structured populations, enabling prosocial exclusion (a form of targeted sanctioning) is likely to increase cooperation rates, and that the negative impact of antisocial exclusion does not fully undermine this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect support: higher cooperation rates likely correspond to higher efficiency, but the mapping is not explicit. The findings are most relevant for settings with spatial structure, peer-based exclusion, and variable exclusion costs.

- source: Faillo, M., Grieco, D., & Zarri, L. (2020). The impact of peer ratings on cooperation: The role of information and cost of rating. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with four players and 20 rounds, adding a mandatory, costless peer rating stage (where players rate each other's contributions on a 0-10 scale, with no monetary consequences) increases group contributions and efficiency relative to a baseline with no rating. This effect is robust to whether rating information is private (only the recipient sees their ratings) or public (all see everyone's ratings). When a small cost (1 token per peer rated) is introduced for assigning ratings, most players stop rating, and the effect on contributions and efficiency vanishes. The study provides direct evidence that non-monetary, payoff-irrelevant peer approval can foster cooperation and efficiency in public goods games, but only if it is costless to provide.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costless, non-monetary peer approval mechanism (rating) in a standard repeated public goods game increases efficiency, even when ratings have no direct payoff consequences and are anonymous. The effect is robust to whether rating information is private or public. However, even a small cost to rating eliminates the effect. For prediction, the presence of a costless, mandatory, non-monetary peer approval mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. If rating is costly, no efficiency gain should be expected. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the only sanction/reward is social approval, not monetary punishment or reward.

- source: Greiff, M. (2013). Rewards and the private provision of public goods on dynamic networks. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with rewards (where giving rewards is costly but beneficial to the receiver), the emergence of a social institution prescribing prosocial behavior (contribute and reward contributors) can lead to efficient public good provision, provided that rewards are only given to contributors and the network structure evolves quickly enough to allow exclusion of non-contributors. If rewards are also given to defectors, or if players update strategies too quickly, the system converges to low contribution and low efficiency. The model does not analyze punishment directly, but exclusion from rewards via network dynamics acts as an endogenous sanction. The findings are theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward mechanisms, especially in dynamic network settings. It provides strong theoretical evidence that reward-based institutions can achieve high efficiency if rewards are targeted only at contributors and if the network allows for exclusion of non-contributors. However, it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it compare reward to punishment directly. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that endogenous exclusion (a form of sanction) can substitute for explicit punishment, but does not quantify the effect of punishment per se. The model's results are most applicable to games with dynamic partner selection and reward, not to standard fixed-group punishment treatments.

- source: Falvey, R., Lane, T., & Luckraz, S. (2025). On a mechanism that improves efficiency and reduces inequality in voluntary contribution games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The Galbraith Mechanism (GM) leads to higher efficiency (measured by average group contributions and payoffs) and lower inequality (measured by Gini coefficients) than the standard equal-sharing mechanism in voluntary contribution games with heterogeneous endowments. The GM's effect is robust across different levels of endowment inequality and marginal per-capita return (beta), though the effect is stronger when beta is higher. The mechanism works by allowing players to allocate the public good among each other based on observed contributions, incentivizing higher contributions without explicit punishment or reward costs. The GM outperforms the control in both efficiency and equity, especially benefiting the less-endowed players.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing endogenous allocation of the public good based on observed contributions (like the GM) can substantially increase efficiency compared to equal-sharing controls, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment or reward. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that introducing a mechanism where players can allocate payoffs based on contributions (even without explicit punishment) can yield efficiency gains similar to or greater than those seen with standard punishment mechanisms, especially when the marginal per-capita return is sufficiently high. The results are most directly relevant for games with 3 players, 15 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and full information about others' contributions and allocations. The GM is not a punishment mechanism per se, but its allocation stage can serve a similar incentive function. The paper does not test standard punishment (costly reduction of others' payoffs), so direct quantitative transfer to punishment-enabled PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative lesson is that endogenous, contribution-based allocation can strongly boost efficiency.

- source: Kurokawa, S., & Ihara, Y. (2017). Evolution of group-wise cooperation: Is direct reciprocity insufficient?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper shows that in n-player repeated prisoners' dilemma games with non-rivalrous public goods, direct reciprocity alone can support the evolution of cooperation (and thus high group efficiency), especially as group size increases. This holds even when individuals sometimes make mistakes or have imperfect information, provided they use sufficiently optimistic conditional strategies. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is the condition under which cooperative strategies are selectively favored over defection, which is directly tied to group payoff and efficiency. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated efficiency values, but gives analytic thresholds for when cooperation is favored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with non-rivalrous benefits, increasing group size can make cooperation (and thus high efficiency) more likely, even in the presence of mistakes or imperfect information, as long as players use optimistic conditional strategies. However, the paper does not model punishment, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, information, and error, but not for the effect of punishment. The paper is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated public goods games with direct reciprocity, but not for predicting the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Unemi, T. (2011). Replicator dynamics in public goods games with reward funds. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a compulsory public goods game with a pool-rewarding mechanism, cooperation and high efficiency can be maintained if the net benefit from the reward fund exceeds the cost of contributing to the public good. Specifically, if c2*(r2-1) > sigma (where sigma is the net cost of contributing to the public good), the population can converge to all rewarders (full efficiency) or exhibit stable cycles with high average payoff. If this condition is not met, defectors dominate and efficiency collapses. The model also shows that introducing second-order sanctions (reducing rewards to second-order free riders) can stabilize full cooperation. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGG-like games with reward mechanisms, but only indirectly inform about punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with reward funds, the efficiency outcome depends critically on the relative effectiveness and cost of the reward mechanism compared to the cost of cooperation. The explicit condition c2*(r2-1) > sigma can be used to predict when enabling a reward mechanism will increase efficiency relative to the control (no reward) case. However, the paper does not analyze punishment, so its direct value for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency is limited. The model structure and results may be informative by analogy for understanding how the cost and effectiveness of sanctions (punishment or reward) interact with game parameters (group size, MPCR, etc.) to determine efficiency outcomes.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2018). Seven rules to avoid the tragedy of the commons. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games with three players and implementation error, it is possible to construct deterministic memory-3 strategies that achieve mutual cooperation (efficiency) and defensibility (no player can be exploited), as well as distinguishability (outperforming unconditional cooperators). For n-person games, memory length at least n is required for such strategies. The strategies do not involve explicit punishment actions or costs, but use conditional cooperation and defection rules that can serve similar functions. The findings are theoretical and do not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment mechanisms on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated public goods games, it is possible to achieve high efficiency through carefully designed deterministic strategies with sufficient memory, even in the presence of errors. However, it does not study the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (such as costly punishment or sanctions) on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare games with and without punishment. Therefore, while the results are highly relevant for understanding the potential for cooperation and efficiency in repeated PGGs, they do not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in experimental or empirical settings. The paper is most useful for understanding the theoretical limits of strategy design rather than the causal effect of punishment interventions.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2021). Coevolution of actions, personal norms and beliefs about others in social dilemmas. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, in repeated public goods and related games, the long-term average group action and payoff converge to the Nash equilibrium based on material payoffs when there is no external authority. Social and cognitive factors (such as conformity, cognitive dissonance, and beliefs about others) affect the distribution and variance of actions and beliefs, but do not shift the group average away from the material-payoff optimum in the absence of authority. When an external authority promotes a particular action, group behavior can shift toward the promoted standard, sometimes increasing or decreasing efficiency depending on the alignment of the promoted action with the material optimum. The model does not include explicit peer punishment or sanctioning as a treatment variable, so it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical baseline for predicting efficiency in public goods and related games without explicit punishment mechanisms. It shows that, absent external authority or explicit sanctions, efficiency is determined by material payoffs, and social/cognitive factors mainly affect variance and convergence speed. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, unless punishment is modeled as a material cost for non-conformity (as in the 'disapproval by peers' term), enabling punishment would require additional modeling or empirical evidence. The model is most useful for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency and for understanding the role of soft normative pressures, but does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling explicit punishment on efficiency.

- source: Hauert, C. (2010). Replicator dynamics of reward & reputation in public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the opportunity to reward contributors (with or without reputation) can encourage the emergence of cooperation if rewards are sufficiently appealing and visible (via reputation). However, reward alone cannot stabilize cooperation, as second-order free-riders (who cooperate but do not reward) undermine the system, leading to cycles of cooperation and defection. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and explicit payoff calculations for different strategy types. The paper contrasts these findings with the literature on punishment, noting that punishment can stabilize cooperation but is less effective at establishing it. Efficiency (average population payoff) is analyzed as a function of the strategic composition of the population, but the paper does not provide direct results for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the mechanisms by which reward and reputation affect efficiency and cooperation in public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but its discussion of the contrasting roles of reward and punishment can inform predictions: reward can increase cooperation and thus efficiency in the short term, but cannot maintain high efficiency due to second-order free-riding. Punishment, as discussed from prior literature, can stabilize high efficiency once cooperation is established. The explicit payoff equations and analysis of group size, reward cost/benefit, and reputation can inform parameterization of models, but direct quantitative predictions for punishment effects are not provided.

- source: Suzuki, S., & Akiyama, E. (2007). Evolution of indirect reciprocity in groups of various sizes and comparison with direct reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-person public goods games with indirect reciprocity, both cooperative (discriminating) and defective strategies can be evolutionarily stable, but the threshold for cooperation increases sharply with group size. As group size increases, it becomes much harder for cooperation to evolve and persist, leading to lower expected group efficiency/payoff. The reputation mechanism (standing vs. image scoring) is a strong moderator: standing allows for cooperation to be stable under a wider range of parameters. The presence of implementation errors (failures to cooperate) further undermines the stability of cooperation. When unconditional cooperators are present, cooperation is only stable for group sizes greater than two and under certain parameter ranges. The results are derived analytically from payoff and replicator dynamics equations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing group size in public goods games with indirect reciprocity (and no explicit punishment mechanism) makes it much harder for cooperation and high efficiency to evolve. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in large groups, the baseline efficiency is likely to be low, and the marginal effect of punishment may depend on whether it can overcome the group size barrier. The paper also highlights the importance of the reputation mechanism: if the punishment or sanctioning system is more like 'standing' (justified punishment does not harm reputation), cooperation is more likely to be stable. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, error rates, and reputation mechanisms, but do not directly model explicit costly punishment as a game option.

- source: Kristensen, N. P., Chisholm, R. A., & Ohtsuki, H. (2025). Many-strategy games in groups with relatives and the evolution of coordinated cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that kin selection (homophily) can facilitate the initial invasion of coordinated cooperation in public goods games with nonlinear (sigmoidal) benefits, even when such cooperation cannot invade in a well-mixed population due to cognitive or coordination costs. Once established, coordinated cooperation can persist as homophily declines, provided that the benefit function is sufficiently nonlinear and the switching gain from non-contribution to contribution at the coordination point exceeds the cost. If this condition is not met, some level of homophily must be maintained to prevent the invasion of defectors or 'liars' (who commit to contribute but do not follow through). The framework provides analytical and numerical tools for predicting evolutionary outcomes (including efficiency and group payoff) as a function of group size, relatedness, benefit function nonlinearity, and strategy set. The results are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but do not address punishment or sanctions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a general theoretical framework for predicting efficiency and group payoff in public goods games with arbitrary group size, number of strategies, and non-additive (e.g., sigmoidal) benefit functions, under varying levels of kin structure (homophily). It is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR (via the benefit function), and group structure. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The framework could, in principle, be extended to include punishment as a discrete strategy, but this is not done in the present paper. Therefore, the paper is highly informative for predicting baseline efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but provides only indirect or contextual support for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). Evolution of commitment and level of participation in public goods games. *Autonomous Agents and Multi-agent Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a commitment mechanism—where players can propose binding agreements before the PGG, with compensation for defection—substantially increases both cooperation and average payoffs, provided the cost of arranging commitment is not too high. The effect is robust across group sizes and compensation levels, as long as compensation is above a threshold. The optimal participation threshold (number of committers required) depends on the severity of the dilemma and the cost of commitment. The model shows that commitment mechanisms can transform the efficiency of the PGG, making group payoffs much closer to the fully cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling credible commitment mechanisms in PGG-like environments can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of arranging commitment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a commitment mechanism (even if not a classic punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency, with the effect size moderated by the cost of commitment, the mpcr, and the participation threshold. The results are robust across group sizes and compensation levels (above a threshold). However, the model does not directly analyze classic punishment (costly peer punishment), so direct transfer to punishment-enabled PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Arenas, A., Camacho, J., Cuesta, J. A., & Requejo, R. J. (2011). The joker effect: Cooperation driven by destructive agents. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing destructive agents ('jokers') into a public goods game leads to robust cycles in which cooperation periodically surges. The mechanism is that jokers harm defectors more than cooperators, creating a dynamic where cooperators can invade jokers, jokers invade defectors, and defectors invade cooperators. The cycles are robust to mutation and population size. The model provides explicit payoff expressions for each strategy, but the main focus is on the evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how destructive threats can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions per se. Instead, it analyzes the effect of introducing a destructive agent type that harms all non-jokers. The model is a theoretical extension of the PGG and provides insight into how external threats or destructive forces can promote cooperation through population dynamics. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze standard punishment mechanisms. Its main value is in highlighting that destructive forces can, under certain conditions, increase cooperation, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Yang, R., Chen, T., & Chen, Q. (2020). Promoting cooperation by reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism in public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The reputation-based payoff transfer mechanism, which redistributes payoffs from low- to high-reputation players, can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. The effect depends on the parameters controlling the proportion of players who lose or gain payoffs (transfer ratios) and the strength of the transfer. When both the proportion of punished (transferred) and rewarded (accepted) players are moderate and balanced, cooperation can quickly dominate the population. The mechanism is most effective when all players are either punished or rewarded (transfer ratio = 1). However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms combining endogenous punishment and reward based on reputation can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when the redistribution is balanced and covers the whole population. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings may be useful for predicting cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Du, C. P., Jia, D. Y., Jin, L. B., & Shi, L. (2018). The impact of neutral reward on cooperation in public good game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a neutral reward mechanism, where the majority strategy in a group is rewarded by the minority, promotes the evolution of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. As the reward factor increases, cooperation becomes more prevalent, and for sufficiently high reward, the system can reach full cooperation. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperators and the spatial distribution of strategies. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reward mechanisms (even when not perfectly targeted) can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the outcomes are limited to cooperation rates and do not include efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The game design dimensions are described in the model, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment or reward. The results may suggest that reward mechanisms can increase efficiency if cooperation translates to higher group payoff, but this is not demonstrated in the paper.

- source: Martinsson, P., & Persson, E. (2019). Public Goods and Minimum Provision Levels: Does the Institutional Formation Affect Cooperation?. *Scandinavian Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Imposing a minimum contribution level (5 tokens out of 20) in a linear public goods game increases average contributions compared to a standard voluntary contribution mechanism. This effect is robust to whether the minimum is imposed exogenously or chosen endogenously by group vote. The increase in contributions is mainly due to free riders being forced to contribute at least the minimum; there is little evidence of crowding out among those who previously contributed more than the minimum. The mode of institutional formation (democracy vs. authority) does not significantly affect the outcome. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, but the results suggest that minimum contribution rules can increase public good provision in settings where punishment is not feasible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of minimum contribution rules in public goods games, especially in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that institutional interventions (minimum levels) can increase contributions, mainly by raising the floor for low contributors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that minimum contribution rules can be effective in increasing contributions, but does not inform about the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, no chat, continuous contributions, no punishment/reward) are clearly specified, which helps in mapping to other studies. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction.

- source: Alenaizi, R., Contu, D., Elshareif, E., Kaya, O., & Kaya, I. (2024). Assumed similarity in personality traits has a short-lived positive effect on cooperation in absence of reciprocity. *Applied Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are told they are matched with others who have similar personality traits, their contributions to the public good increase in the short term (notably in round 7 compared to round 4). When told they are matched with dissimilar personalities, contributions decrease. However, the effect of similarity framing is short-lived and diminishes quickly. The game includes a penalty for non-contribution, but this is a fixed, automatic penalty rather than a peer-administered punishment. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The penalty mechanism is automatic and not peer-administered, so it does not map directly to the punishment dimension of interest. The findings are relevant for understanding how framing and perceived similarity affect contributions, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment mechanisms. The game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, and penalty structure are described, but the absence of a peer punishment treatment means this paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Greiff, M., & Paetzel, F. (2016). Second-order beliefs in reputation systems with endogenous evaluations - an experimental study. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a repeated two-player public good game, providing information about both one's own and the partner's evaluation (reputation) increases contributions compared to control and to the treatment where only the partner's evaluation is shown. When only the partner's evaluation is available, contributions do not increase significantly relative to control. The effect is explained by the role of second-order beliefs in conditional cooperation. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the BOTH-INFO treatment imply higher group payoffs. There is no punishment or costly sanctioning; evaluations are costless, non-binding signals.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of reputation-based information (not costly punishment) on contribution rates in repeated two-player public good games. It suggests that simply providing information about others' evaluations is insufficient to increase cooperation, but providing both own and partner's evaluation can increase contributions. However, because the study does not include a costly punishment mechanism, its direct relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social image or reputation feedback (not material punishment) is the main intervention. For prediction tasks involving costly punishment, this paper provides only adjacent evidence.

- source: Adams, Z., Ludwiczak, A., Sharma, D., & Osman, M. (2022). Verbal interaction in a social dilemma. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the quality of communication—specifically, consensus-building language and alignment in chat—significantly predicts increases in cooperative behavior in a repeated public goods game. Groups with higher consensus scores in chat showed larger increases in contributions to the group pot in the next round. The analysis also shows that certain registers (gameshow, familial, socialist) are associated with more successful cooperation, while business and political registers are associated with less success. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that allowing chat/communication between rounds in a public goods game can increase cooperation, but the effect depends on the quality and style of the communication. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the importance of communication (chat) as a moderator of cooperation, but does not address punishment or efficiency directly. It is most useful for understanding the role of chat/communication as a game design dimension.

- source: Liu, X. L., Li, J. B., Wang, G. R., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., & Fu, X. G. (2020). Transcranial direct current stimulation of the rLPFC shifts normative judgments in voluntary cooperation. *Neuroscience Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that modulating rLPFC activity via tDCS causally affects both participants' normative judgments about appropriate contributions and their actual contributions in a one-shot, linear, asymmetric public goods game. Anodal stimulation (increasing rLPFC excitability) leads to higher normative judgments and higher contributions, while cathodal stimulation (decreasing rLPFC excitability) leads to lower judgments and contributions, relative to sham. The effect is robust across both 'rich' and 'poor' player types. No punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms are present in the game, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant to understanding the neural basis of normative judgment and voluntary cooperation, but not to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, number of rounds) may be useful for context, but the absence of punishment means this study should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Chen, T., Wu, Z. H., & Wang, L. (2018). Disseminators or silencers: The effect of information diffusion intensity on cooperation in public goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and type of information dissemination strongly affect the speed at which full cooperation is achieved in a spatial public goods game with reputation and conformity. Negative disseminators (who spread information about defectors) most often lead to the fastest convergence to full cooperation, followed by neutral and positive disseminators. Silencers (no dissemination) are least effective. The effect of dissemination type is most pronounced when conformity synergy, link-neighbor degree, population size, or environmental noise are high, or when the synergy factor is low. However, all models eventually reach full cooperation, so there is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare differences between models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of information diffusion and reputation in accelerating the achievement of cooperation. The findings suggest that mechanisms which amplify reputational consequences for defectors (akin to negative gossip or public shaming) can speed up the transition to full cooperation, especially in certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address whether the final payoffs or efficiency differ between conditions.

- source: Wang, Z., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Rewarding evolutionary fitness with links between populations promotes cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that rewarding players whose utility exceeds a threshold by granting them external links to another population can significantly promote cooperation in both public goods and prisoner's dilemma games on structured networks. The effect is maximized at intermediate threshold values, where rewarded players are rare but able to percolate, acting as catalysts for cooperation. If the threshold is too low or too high, the effect diminishes. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. The results are robust across network types and game details. Punishment is not modeled, only discussed as background.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a reward mechanism (external links for high-fitness players) and analyzes its effect on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or payoff. While it discusses the relative merits of reward and punishment in the introduction, all results pertain to reward. Therefore, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, except as context for how reward mechanisms can promote cooperation under certain network and threshold conditions.

- source: Liu, Q., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2018). Evolutionary Public Goods Game on Evolving Random Networks. *Journal of the Korean Physical Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing informed cooperators to rewire their network connections at a cost can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in public goods games on random networks. Higher rates of network rewiring (by informed cooperators) lower the threshold for cooperation to emerge, especially under adverse conditions (low synergy factor). The system exhibits distinct phases (full cooperation, full defection, coexistence, and segregated sub-networks), and the transition between these phases depends on the synergy factor and rewiring rate. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not implement or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Instead, it studies the effect of costly information and network rewiring on cooperation rates. While the findings suggest that network adaptation can promote cooperation, they do not provide evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effect of punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency, though it may be contextually relevant for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Morsky, B., & Akçay, E. (2021). False beliefs can bootstrap cooperative communities through social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that inaccurate, overly optimistic beliefs about the level of cooperation can bootstrap and sustain high cooperation in communities governed by conditional cooperation norms, even in anonymous settings. The model shows that the dynamics of joining, learning, and leaving the community can create bistability, abrupt transitions, or cycles in cooperation rates and community size. High cooperation can be achieved and maintained if naive (optimistic) individuals are present in sufficient numbers, and if learning the true (lower) level of cooperation is slow or if disillusioned individuals can easily leave. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but discusses how mechanisms that increase transparency or allow for punishment may interact with these dynamics. The findings are relevant for understanding how social norms and belief management can foster cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how belief management and social norm dynamics can affect cooperation rates in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. It suggests that mechanisms which slow learning about true cooperation levels or allow for easy exit can paradoxically increase cooperation by maintaining a pool of optimistic, conditionally cooperative individuals. However, because the model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual and highlights possible trade-offs between transparency (which may reduce cooperation by revealing low compliance) and incentive mechanisms (which may increase or decrease cooperation depending on beliefs). For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding the role of belief dynamics and social norms as moderators, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Muñoz-Herrera, M., & Nikiforakis, N. (2019). James Andreoni and the quest for others in our utility functions. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews the development of experimental economics in the context of public goods and prosocial behavior, highlighting Andreoni's work on impure altruism, crowding out, and the rationality of giving. It discusses how early experiments showed voluntary contributions to public goods, the decline of contributions over repeated rounds, and the theoretical and empirical challenges to the 'free rider' hypothesis. The review emphasizes the importance of motives (e.g., warm-glow giving) and the incomplete crowding out of voluntary contributions by government intervention. It also highlights the rationality of prosocial choices as demonstrated by Andreoni and Miller (2002). However, the paper does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or data for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding the historical context and theoretical motivations behind public goods experiments, especially regarding voluntary contributions and prosocial motives. However, it does not analyze or report on punishment mechanisms, their design dimensions, or their effects on efficiency or payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers only background context and no actionable evidence.

- source: Tan, L. J., & Wei, L. J. (2014). VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTION MECHANISM PLAYED OVER AN INFINITE HORIZON. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, average contributions in infinitely repeated VCM games do not monotonically decline as in finitely repeated games. Instead, they show non-monotonic patterns, often sustained by the presence of 'contribution makers' who lead reciprocators to increase their contributions. There is a strong restart effect in finitely repeated games but not in infinitely repeated games. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that groups with contribution makers achieve higher average contributions and, by implication, higher payoffs than groups without them.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
