It does, however, provide evidence on baseline contribution dynamics in standard VCMs with continuous contribution choices, fixed groups of 5, MPCR of 0.5, and no communication or punishment. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (proxied by contribution rates) is sustained at moderate levels in infinitely repeated games, especially when 'contribution makers' are present. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as a control/baseline for VCMs without punishment, not for estimating the effect of punishment.

- source: Oechssler, J., Reischmann, A., & Sofianos, A. (2022). The conditional contribution mechanism for repeated public goods-The general case. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The CCM leads to much higher efficiency (as measured by average group contributions and the share of Pareto optimal outcomes) than the standard VCM in repeated public goods games with complete information. The CCM achieves about 78% Pareto optimal outcomes, compared to 24% for the VCM. The SCCM (one-message version) and VCMC (VCM with non-binding communication) perform between the two, with SCCM catching up to CCM in later periods. Under incomplete information, both CCM and VCM achieve similar, moderate efficiency, and the difference is not significant. The risk of being a 'sucker' (earning less than the endowment) is much lower in CCM than in VCM. The study provides strong evidence that the CCM can sustain high efficiency in repeated public goods games without requiring knowledge of others' preferences, but does not test any punishment or sanctioning treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated public goods games with mechanisms that allow conditional contributions, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. The experimental design and outcome measures are directly relevant to efficiency prediction, but since no punishment is present in any treatment, the paper cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. It does, however, provide a strong benchmark for efficiency in control games without punishment, especially for games with 5 players, 60 rounds, continuous contributions, and no communication or punishment. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper can only inform the baseline (control) efficiency, not the treatment effect of punishment.

- source: English, W. (2012). Demystifying trust: Experimental evidence from Cambodia and Thailand. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot, four-player public goods games in rural Thailand and Cambodia, standard survey measures of trust (from the World Values Survey) do not predict contributions or group efficiency. Instead, expectations about others' contributions are the key driver of cooperative behavior and group payoff. When expectations are experimentally lowered (by revealing low contributions from a previous group), both contributions and group efficiency drop sharply. The distribution of player types (reciprocators, free riders, altruists) is similar to previous studies, with reciprocators being the largest group. The results suggest that interventions that shift expectations can rapidly change group efficiency, while survey-based trust is not a reliable predictor of economic cooperation in this context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, group efficiency in public goods games is highly sensitive to participants' expectations about others' behavior, but not to standard survey measures of trust. For the downstream prediction task, this means that, for games with similar design (one-shot, no punishment, no communication, fixed group size, known MPCR), efficiency is likely to be determined by expectation management rather than by trust survey scores. The absence of punishment means the paper does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of expectation-shaping interventions. The findings are most relevant for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in PGGs without punishment, and for understanding that expectation manipulation (e.g., via feedback or communication) can have large effects on efficiency.

- source: Hajikhameneh, A., & Iannaccone, L. R. (2023). God games: An experimental study of uncertainty, superstition, and cooperation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that adding random or pseudo-supernatural earnings shocks to a standard public goods game (without punishment or reward) substantially increases group contributions and group earnings, relative to the baseline. Communication (chat) further boosts contributions and efficiency in most conditions, but in the 'watchful' (quasi-monotheistic) condition, chat can foster counterproductive superstitions, leading to suboptimal group strategies and lower efficiency. The study provides strong evidence that even irrelevant risk or uncertainty can increase cooperation and group welfare in PGGs, but does not test or report on punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. However, it does show that introducing random or pseudo-supernatural shocks (even when not contingent on behavior) can increase efficiency, and that communication can further enhance or sometimes reduce efficiency depending on framing. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight that other forms of uncertainty or narrative framing can have large effects on group efficiency. The game design dimensions are well-documented, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot be used to estimate the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Ball, S., Sarangi, S., & Upadhyay, S. (2025). Coalitions Improve the Coordination and Provision of Public Goods: Theory and Experimental Evidence. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to form coalitions before making contribution decisions in a binary public goods game leads to higher contributions and higher group payoffs. Coalition members are much more likely to contribute than in the standard VCM, and the decline in contributions over rounds is mitigated. Higher MPCR increases both coalition size and the likelihood of contribution, resulting in higher payoffs for all group members, including the least well-off. The coalition mechanism works by sorting players with stronger social preferences into the coalition, who then coordinate to contribute. The experiment directly compares the standard VCM (control) to the coalition treatment, showing clear increases in group payoff and efficiency when coalitions are allowed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in binary (all-or-nothing) public goods games with induced social preferences, adding a coalition formation stage (where coalition members vote and are bound by majority rule) increases group payoff and efficiency relative to the standard VCM. The effect is stronger at higher MPCR. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without coalition mechanisms, but not for punishment-based interventions, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. The findings suggest that coordination mechanisms (like coalitions) can substitute for punishment in increasing efficiency, especially when MPCR is high and social preferences are present.

- source: Reeves, T., Ohtsuki, H., & Fukui, S. (2017). Asymmetric public goods game cooperation through pest control. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric public goods games (modeled as pest control among farmers), the Nash equilibrium investment and resulting efficiency depend strongly on the discount factor and the number of players. There are threshold effects: only farmers close to the risk (frontier) contribute when the discount factor is low, while more distant farmers contribute only if the discount factor is high enough. Increasing the number of players does not always increase efficiency; in some parameter regions, adding more players can paradoxically reduce total investment and group welfare. Asymmetry leads to a disproportionate burden on those at greatest risk, making the system more vulnerable to shocks. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how efficiency in asymmetric public goods games depends on player count, discount factor, and the structure of risk, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is highly informative for baseline (control) efficiency in asymmetric, no-punishment settings, especially regarding the effects of group size and discounting. However, it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no such mechanism is present or analyzed. Its findings suggest that efficiency can be non-monotonic in group size and is sensitive to asymmetry and time preferences, which should be considered when predicting control efficiency, but it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment.

- source: Wang, S., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2018). Non-Fixed Investment in Voluntary Public Goods Games. *Journal of the Korean Physical Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing non-fixed investment in voluntary PGGs leads to complex rock-scissors-paper dynamics among cooperators, defectors, and loners. Increasing the maximum possible investment ratio can initially promote cooperation and increase average payoff, but further increases may reduce cooperation due to increased temptation to defect. Higher interest rates (MPCR) and lower loner payoffs promote both cooperation and higher average payoffs. The memory length (sensitivity to past payoffs) also moderates outcomes: moderate memory promotes higher average payoffs and cooperation, while very short or very long memory is detrimental. The system always converges to a stable equilibrium regardless of initial conditions. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or discussed as interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the model or analysis. It is therefore not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how other design dimensions (investment flexibility, MPCR, loner payoff, memory) affect efficiency and cooperation in voluntary PGGs without punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant.

- source: Takezawa, M., & Price, M. E. (2010). Revisiting The Evolution of Reciprocity in Sizable Groups: Continuous reciprocity in the repeated n-person prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated n-person public goods games, continuous reciprocity (where players match the average contribution of others) is much more robust than discrete reciprocity (all-or-nothing) in supporting the evolution of cooperation, especially as the marginal per capita return (mpcr) increases. The basin of attraction for continuous reciprocity grows almost linearly with efficiency, allowing cooperation to invade from low initial frequencies when mpcr is high. Assortative interaction (preferential grouping of reciprocators) further enhances the viability of continuous reciprocity, even at lower efficiency. However, both forms of reciprocity struggle to invade when efficiency is low or group size is large, unless assortativity is present. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency of cooperation (mpcr) is a critical moderator of the success of reciprocity-based cooperation in public goods games. In the absence of punishment, high mpcr (approaching 1) is necessary for cooperation to be stable or to invade. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the mpcr dimension should be weighted heavily when estimating efficiency outcomes in control (no-punishment) games. However, because the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight that in high-mpcr environments, cooperation can be stable even without punishment, while in low-mpcr environments, cooperation is unlikely to persist without additional mechanisms.

- source: Hilbe, C., Wu, B., Traulsen, A., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Evolutionary performance of zero-determinant strategies in multiplayer games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that repeated interactions (direct reciprocity) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in small groups, but not in large groups. As group size increases, the evolutionary dynamics favor defectors and extortioners, leading to a sharp drop in average group payoff. The results are robust across both restricted (ZD strategies only) and general (all memory-one strategies) strategy spaces. The paper provides explicit formulas for payoffs and demonstrates, both analytically and via simulation, that group size is a critical moderator of efficiency in repeated public goods games without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, increasing group size in repeated public goods games leads to lower efficiency, even when direct reciprocity is possible. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the baseline efficiency in control games without punishment will be high for small groups but will decline as group size increases. The paper does not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency change when punishment is enabled. However, it does highlight that, without additional mechanisms (like punishment), large groups are unlikely to sustain cooperation or high efficiency.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Wu, T., Chen, X. J., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2013). Mixed strategy under generalized public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary outcome in generalized public goods games with mixed strategies depends critically on the shape of the production function, the group size, the population size, and the cost-to-benefit ratio. For linear production functions, the outcome is determined by the cost/benefit ratio. For nonlinear functions, concave shapes favor stable coexistence (CESS), convex shapes favor bistability (repeller), and sigmoidal shapes can produce more complex dynamics with multiple equilibria. When the group size equals the population size, cooperation is always lost. The paper provides explicit conditions for the emergence of full cooperation, full defection, or intermediate equilibria, but does not address the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed theoretical predictions about how efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) in public goods games depends on the production function, group size, population size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it is highly informative for understanding baseline efficiency in control games without punishment, especially as a function of game design parameters such as group size, population size, and the nonlinearity of the public goods production function.

- source: Olejarz, J. W., & Nowak, M. A. (2014). Evolution of staying together in the context of diffusible public goods. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in public goods games with diffusible goods can evolve if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold determined by the probability of staying together (q) and the internal absorption efficiency (alpha_k). Clustering increases the efficiency of cooperation by allowing cooperators to preferentially benefit from their own public goods. The model also shows that if defectors and cooperators differ in their ability to absorb public goods, coexistence or bistability can occur. Introducing a cost to large clusters (gamma_k) can create an optimal cluster size for cooperation. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for these outcomes, but does not consider punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how structural and spatial factors (such as clustering and internal absorption efficiency) affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control games without punishment, or in environments where clustering and spatial structure are the main mechanisms supporting cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how efficiency depends on clustering, internal benefit allocation, and the benefit-to-cost ratio, but offers no evidence about punishment effects.

- source: He, S. M., & Zhu, X. (2023). Real-time monitoring in a public-goods game. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a four-player linear public-goods game, mechanisms that allow for incremental, irreversible commitments—especially when combined with a real-time clock—lead to much higher and more stable contributions than baseline, announcement, or final commitment mechanisms. Contributions in the main (clock) treatment reach about 75% of the maximum possible by the end, while in the baseline and announcement treatments, contributions decay to near zero. The paper provides detailed behavioral analysis of strategy adoption (trigger and near-dominant strategies) but does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, nor does it directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public-goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its findings are not directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, given game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does provide evidence that certain commitment mechanisms (incremental, clock-based) can substantially increase contributions in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not informative.

- source: Li, X. Y., Chen, T., Chen, Q., & Zhang, X. Y. (2020). The impact of retention time of donation list on cooperation in public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that publishing a donation list with a moderate retention time and listing a moderate proportion of players can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators and the total sum of contributions in a public goods game. Instant or very frequent list updates are less effective than lists with longer retention times (but not excessively long). Listing too many or too few players is also suboptimal; an intermediate proportion maximizes contributions. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the sum of contributions is used as a proxy for public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it does provide informative evidence about the effects of reputation-based mechanisms (public listing, retention time, and partial disclosure) on cooperation and total contributions. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the effects of non-punitive social incentives. The sum of contributions (S_c) is a close but not exact proxy for efficiency.

- source: Denant-Boemont, L., Masclet, D., & Noussair, C. (2011). ANNOUNCEMENT, OBSERVATION AND HONESTY IN THE VOLUNTARY CONTRIBUTIONS GAME. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that requiring subjects to make non-binding announcements of intended contributions, combined with making actual contributions publicly observable and attributable, significantly increases average contribution levels in a repeated public goods game. However, neither announcements alone nor observation alone have a significant effect. The increase in contributions is interpreted as arising from increased social pressure (guilt and shame) and greater honesty when actions are observable. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes (contribution, announcement, honesty).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it does provide informative evidence about the effects of communication (announcements) and observability (public attribution of actions) on contribution behavior. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding the baseline effects of non-punitive social mechanisms (communication, transparency) on cooperation. It also clarifies that, in the absence of punishment, such mechanisms can increase contributions, but do not always do so unless combined.

- source: Park, S. A., Jeong, S., & Jeong, J. (2013). TV programs that denounce unfair advantage impact women's sensitivity to defection in the public goods game. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a 10-round, 4-player, binary (all-or-nothing) threshold PGG, women are more cooperative than men in early rounds, but this difference disappears after both genders watch investigative TV programs about free-riding. After media exposure, women become more likely to free-ride in response to others' defection, adopting a tit-for-tat strategy. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and EEG measures; no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It does, however, provide detailed information about a binary, threshold PGG with 4 players and 10 rounds, and shows how external context (media exposure) can modulate cooperation rates, especially among women. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, but it does confirm that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates (and thus likely efficiency) can be sensitive to social context and gender. The absence of punishment and the focus on behavioral (not payoff) outcomes limit its direct relevance.

- source: Gianotti, L. R. R., Dahinden, F. M., Baumgartner, T., & Knoch, D. (2019). Understanding Individual Differences in Domain-General Prosociality: A Resting EEG Study. *Brain Topography*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that higher resting beta2 and beta3 current density in the right temporo-parietal junction (TPJ) is positively associated with higher domain-general prosociality, as measured by contributions in three one-shot PGGs (with multipliers 1.2, 2, and 3.2) and giving in a dictator game. The neural trait is stable and specific to the TPJ and beta bands. No relationship is found between prosociality and the honesty-humility personality scale. The study does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are limited to individual differences in prosociality (contribution/giving) and their neural correlates. Thus, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment or related game design dimensions.

- source: Chen, Y. Y., Lu, X. B., Li, Y. Z., Zeng, L. L., Yu, P., Luo, J., Ye, H., & Zheng, W. J. (2021). The Role of the Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex in Public Good and Public Bad Games: Evidence From a tDCS Study. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating VMPFC activity via tDCS can causally affect cooperative behavior in public bad games (loss domain), specifically decreasing cooperation at the start of the game, but has no effect in public good games (gain domain). The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution/loss contribution, beliefs, attitudes), not efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanction mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding neural mechanisms of cooperation, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as there is no punishment or sanction mechanism in the experimental design, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. The findings are limited to neural modulation of cooperation and beliefs in standard and loss-framed public goods games. It may provide some contextual background on behavioral outcomes in PGGs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Liu, J. Z., Peng, M., Peng, Y. C., Li, Y., Chu, C., Li, X. Y., & Liu, Q. (2021). Effects of inequality on a spatial evolutionary public goods game. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that when individual investment ability and enhancement factor are positively correlated (aligned inequality), cooperation is strongly promoted, with the system reaching full cooperation at lower enhancement factors than in the traditional PGG. When these are negatively correlated (misaligned inequality), cooperation is suppressed and may disappear entirely. The mechanism is that high-contribution cooperators form the core of cooperative clusters, attracting and sustaining cooperation among lower-contribution individuals. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in endowments and productivity affects cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or the impact of punishment. It should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2011). Coevolving agent strategies and network topology for the public goods games. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to adapt both their strategies and their network connections leads to higher and more robust levels of cooperation in public goods games. The time scale of network updating relative to strategy updating (parameterized by ω) significantly affects the prevalence of cooperation. Heterogeneous network structures and clustering emerge, which further support cooperation. However, the paper does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how network adaptation and coevolution can promote cooperation, but are not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Figuières, C., Masclet, D., & Willinger, M. (2013). Weak Moral Motivation Leads to the Decline of Voluntary Contributions. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals' moral motivation is weak (i.e., sensitive to others' contributions), average contributions in repeated public goods games decline over time. The model predicts that this decay is robust under both myopic and forward-looking behavior, and can explain several empirical regularities observed in experiments, such as the restart effect and slower decay in longer games. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Its main relevance is in providing a theoretical account of contribution decay in standard linear public goods games without punishment. It may be useful for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment, but does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Cheo, R., Huang, K. A., & Li, J. P. (2023). Group cooperation of village officials in Chinese rural resettlement: A lab in the field. *Contemporary Economic Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that higher average contributions by village officials in a public goods game are associated with higher self-reported support for a resettlement project among villagers. This relationship is robust to controls for demographic and village characteristics. Mechanism analysis suggests that more cooperative leadership teams inform villagers earlier about the resettlement, and that higher PGG contributions are correlated with more altruistic behavior in a dictator game. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes from the PGG. Actual sign-up rates for resettlement contracts are reported as supplementary evidence, showing a correlation with officials' PGG contributions, but these are not the main focus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a standard PGG without punishment or reward, and the main outcomes are non-payoff behavioral measures (contribution, support, information flow). There is no manipulation or analysis of punishment, nor any efficiency or group payoff outcome reported. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Lierl, M. (2016). Social sanctions and informal accountability: Evidence from a laboratory experiment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment studies a public goods game with two beneficiaries and one provider, where the provider can embezzle contributions. The main manipulation is the structure of costly, mutual sanctioning (punishment and reward) relationships: (1) no sanctioning, (2) only among beneficiaries, (3) between provider and one or both beneficiaries, and (4) combinations thereof. The key finding is that mutual sanctioning among beneficiaries increases public goods provision and efficiency compared to no-sanctioning control, but this effect disappears or reverses when the provider is also part of the sanctioning network. The highest efficiency is observed when only beneficiaries can sanction each other. When both beneficiaries and the provider can mutually sanction, efficiency is not improved and can be lower than control. Actual punishment/reward is rare; the effect is mainly anticipatory. The results challenge the idea that denser or more encompassing sanctioning networks always improve efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the network structure of sanctioning relationships. If only beneficiaries can punish each other, enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to control. If the provider can also punish or be punished, the effect is reduced or negative. For prediction, the presence and direction of punishment effects should be conditioned on who can punish whom. The game is a close variant of a standard PGG, with the addition of a third-party provider who can embezzle, so results are most directly applicable to co-production or agency settings. Control efficiency (no-sanction) is reported, and treatment efficiency (with punishment) varies by network structure. The findings suggest that simply enabling punishment is not always efficiency-improving; the details of the punishment network matter.

- source: Colombier, N., Masclet, D., Mirza, D., & Montmarquette, C. (2011). GLOBAL SECURITY POLICIES AGAINST TERRORISM AND THE FREE RIDING PROBLEM: AN EXPERIMENTAL APPROACH. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment simulating international counterterrorism cooperation as a public good with a stochastic loss, the authors find that, absent institutional incentives, most participants defect and invest in private protection rather than the collective good. Introducing punishment (sanction) or reward institutions significantly increases investment in the collective account. However, while both punishment and reward reduce the probability of a loss event and increase first-stage payoffs, only reward institutions lead to higher overall welfare. Punishment increases cooperation but the direct costs of punishing (borne by both punishers and targets) more than offset the efficiency gains from increased cooperation, resulting in lower final payoffs than the baseline. Reward institutions, by contrast, increase both cooperation and final payoffs. Thus, punishment is not efficiency-enhancing in this environment, while reward is.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public-goods-like environment with costly punishment, enabling punishment increases cooperation but reduces overall efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline. The costs of punishment (to both punisher and target) outweigh the benefits from increased cooperation. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a less positive or even negative effect on group payoff/welfare, especially when punishment is costly and the structure is similar to this experiment (n=4, 40 rounds, costly punishment, no identification of punishers, no chat, continuous contributions). The paper also shows that reward institutions can increase both cooperation and efficiency, in contrast to punishment.

- source: Abbink, K., Ryvkin, D., & Serra, D. (2020). Corrupt police. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three main conditions: no police, honest police (can only fine), and corrupt police (can fine or demand bribes). With no police, crime rates are very high and citizen payoffs are low. Honest police reduce crime and increase citizen payoffs. Corrupt police also reduce crime compared to no police, but citizen payoffs are much lower than with honest police due to high bribe payments to officers, resulting in strong inequality. Bribes are used as a substitute for fines, and are often targeted at law-breakers, but imperfectly so—many law-abiding citizens are also extorted. When the maximum bribe is capped at the fine level, crime rates rise to near the no-police level, showing that the deterrent effect of bribes depends on their size. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates (but not low crime rates) moderately reduce both bribery and crime, and increase citizen payoffs, but do not eliminate the efficiency loss from corruption.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling punishment (via police, even if corrupt) increases group efficiency compared to no-punishment baselines, but the efficiency gain is much smaller when punishment is corrupt and redistributive (bribes) rather than legal (fines). The efficiency effect of punishment depends on both the targeting and the cost/impact of punishment: when bribes are large and targeted at law-breakers, crime is deterred, but citizen payoffs are much lower due to wealth transfer to officers. If bribes are capped at the fine level, the deterrent effect is lost. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates can partially mitigate the efficiency loss from corruption. For prediction, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but the structure (honest vs. corrupt, bribe size, targeting) and the presence of anti-corruption incentives are critical moderators.

- source: Cason, T. N., & Gangadharan, L. (2016). Swords without covenants do not lead to self-governance. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment tested four treatments (baseline, punishment only, communication only, both) in a repeated nonlinear common pool resource game with groups of six. Peer punishment (costly, 3:1 fine-to-fee ratio, up to 5 points per target) did not significantly increase group yield or efficiency relative to baseline. Communication (anonymous chat, twice per session) led to dramatic and statistically significant increases in yield/efficiency, with most groups achieving near the social optimum. When both mechanisms were available, communication made punishment largely redundant and unused. The inefficacy of punishment is attributed to the complexity of the nonlinear environment and insufficient use of strong punishment. The findings challenge the generalizability of positive punishment effects from linear public goods games to more realistic, nonlinear settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in nonlinear common pool resource environments (with concave private benefits and endogenous group costs), enabling peer punishment does not improve efficiency over baseline. Therefore, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar nonlinear games, one should expect little or no efficiency gain, even with a punishment technology that is effective in linear games. The presence of communication is a much stronger moderator, with large positive effects on efficiency. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the payoff structure (linear vs nonlinear), and that control efficiency alone is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency without considering the game's nonlinearity and the availability of communication.

- source: Shreedhar, G., Tavoni, A., & Marchiori, C. (2020). Monitoring and punishment networks in an experimental common pool resource dilemma. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a nonlinear CPR lab experiment with three punishment network structures (complete, undirected circle, directed circle), the authors find that average appropriation (resource extraction) is similar across all networks. However, the amount and type of punishment, as well as efficiency (net payoffs), differ. The complete network, with perfect monitoring and punishment, leads to the highest punishment expenditures and the lowest efficiency. Both imperfect networks (undirected and directed circles) have lower punishment expenditures and higher efficiency. The undirected circle network elicits more severe and more prosocial punishment than the other two, but overall, both imperfect networks are more efficient than the complete network. The results suggest that reducing the number of potential punishers (i.e., making monitoring and punishment less than perfect) can increase efficiency by reducing costly punishment, even if cooperation (appropriation) does not change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in nonlinear CPR games with punishment enabled, the structure of the punishment network (i.e., the number of potential punishers and the network's density/directedness) can significantly affect efficiency. Specifically, reducing punishment opportunities (imperfect networks) increases efficiency compared to perfect monitoring and punishment, because less punishment is used and thus less is wasted on costly sanctions. Appropriation (cooperation) rates remain similar, so the efficiency gain is due to lower punishment costs. For prediction, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a perfect monitoring/punishment environment may reduce efficiency relative to imperfect punishment environments, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not always positive—it depends on the punishment network's structure. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the structure of punishment opportunities.

- source: van Klingeren, F., & Buskens, V. (2024). Graduated sanctioning, endogenous institutions and sustainable cooperation in common-pool resources: An experimental test. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a dynamic common-pool resource (CPR) game with four players and repeated rounds, the introduction of sanctioning (punishment) mechanisms—either strict or graduated—leads to higher cooperation and resource sustainability compared to no sanctioning. Strict sanctioning (fixed, high-cost punishment) initially results in higher resource size and profit, but graduated sanctioning (punishment severity increases with repeated defection) leads to greater cooperation and lower appropriation over time, eventually closing the gap in resource size and profit. When groups can endogenously choose their sanctioning mechanism (via majority vote every five rounds), cooperation and resource outcomes improve further, especially in the long run. However, the differences in group and individual profit between sanctioning types are small and diminish over time. The study provides partial support for the effectiveness of graduated and endogenously chosen sanctioning, especially for long-term cooperation, but strict sanctioning may be more effective in short games or early rounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, dynamic CPR games with endogenous resource dynamics and mutual monitoring. The results suggest that both strict and graduated punishment mechanisms increase efficiency (resource sustainability and group profit) relative to no punishment. Strict punishment is more effective in the short term, but graduated punishment becomes equally or more effective over longer time horizons. Endogenously chosen punishment mechanisms (via group voting) further enhance cooperation and resource outcomes, though the effect on profit is small. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency, with the magnitude and time course depending on the punishment regime and whether the institution is endogenously chosen. The findings are most applicable to settings with 4 players, 40 rounds, continuous contribution/appropriation, no chat, and mutual monitoring.

- source: Dughera, S. (2022). The evolution of workplace control leadership, obedience and organizational performance. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model finds that punishment (authoritarian leadership) can increase organizational performance relative to no control when workers are low-skilled or when monitoring is efficient, but is always less efficient than motivation (authoritative leadership) when workers are high-skilled and supervisors are charismatic. Multiple equilibria are possible: (1) 'Discipline' (universal cooperation with punishment), (2) 'Cooperation' (universal cooperation with motivation), (3) 'Conflict' (universal defection with punishment), and (4) mixed or cyclical regimes. The most efficient outcome is universal cooperation with motivation, but this requires high worker skill and supervisor charisma. Punishment can prevent the spread of shirking (the 'bad company' effect) by increasing procedural justice, but also reduces morale and is less efficient. Inefficient equilibria with widespread punishment and/or shirking can persist due to path dependence. The model provides explicit conditions for when each regime is stable and analyzes the welfare properties of each.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in a public-goods-like team production setting, conditional on game design dimensions such as monitoring efficiency (punishment tech), team size (player count), and the presence/absence of reward (motivation/charisma). It shows that punishment can increase efficiency relative to no control when monitoring is effective and workers are not highly skilled, but is always less efficient than a well-functioning reward/motivation regime. The model gives explicit parameter boundaries for when punishment is effective, and highlights the importance of initial conditions and path dependence. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to no-punishment in some parameter regimes (especially with low worker skill and high monitoring efficiency), but that the maximum efficiency is achieved with motivation/charisma rather than punishment. The model is highly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings where reward/motivation is not available or not effective.

- source: Vinayak, R. K. (2025). FARMER'S GAME: A GAME THEORETIC APPROACH TOWARDS BURNING OF CROP RESIDUE. *Journal of Dynamics and Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, without intervention, individual incentives lead to inefficient outcomes (universal burning of crop residue), analogous to the tragedy of the commons. Social pressure can sometimes induce cooperation, but is often not strong enough. The introduction of government punishment (penalty per burned plot) and/or reward (per non-burned plot) can shift the Nash equilibrium to full cooperation (no burning), maximizing group payoff and efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions for when this shift occurs: when the sum of penalty and reward per plot exceeds the net private benefit of burning minus the greener alternative and pollution cost. Simulations confirm these theoretical results, showing that moderate levels of punishment and reward are sufficient to achieve efficient, cooperative outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that introducing punishment (and/or reward) in a public-goods-like environment with negative externalities can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (all defect) to efficient (all cooperate), provided the policy parameters are set so that the penalty plus reward per unit exceeds the net private benefit of defection. The model is directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency: if the punishment is sufficiently strong (relative to the private incentive to defect), efficiency will increase dramatically, potentially reaching full efficiency. The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment is implemented as a per-action cost, is known to all, and is not so severe as to cause practical or political infeasibility. The model does not address empirical implementation issues, but provides clear guidance on the direction and mechanism of efficiency change.

- source: Okada, D., & Bingham, P. M. (2008). Human uniqueness-self-interest and social cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a two-stage game-theoretic model in which players can cooperate or not, and, if there are non-cooperators, cooperators can choose to fight (coerce) or not. The key result is that the cost structure of coercion (punishment) is decisive: if coercion is cheap and effective (as in synchronous/remote attack, e.g., human projectile weapons), then robust equilibria with high cooperation and high efficiency are possible. If coercion is costly and ineffective (as in asynchronous attack, e.g., most non-human animals), only non-cooperation is robust, and group efficiency is low. The model thus predicts that enabling effective, low-cost punishment can transform a low-efficiency public goods game into a high-efficiency one, provided certain thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling effective, low-cost punishment (i.e., high punishment effectiveness per unit cost) in a public-goods-like environment can shift the equilibrium from low to high efficiency, especially as group size increases. The model is directly informative for the effect of punishment cost and punishment technology (effectiveness), and indirectly for player count and the structure of the contribution decision. It suggests that, all else equal, lowering punishment cost or increasing punishment effectiveness should make punishment more likely to increase efficiency. The findings are most relevant for one-shot or short games with simultaneous moves and no communication, and may be less directly applicable to repeated or highly structured lab experiments with additional features.

- source: Lee, J. H., Sigmund, K., Dieckmann, U., & Iwasa, Y. (2015). Games of corruption: How to suppress illegal logging. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (via hired enforcers) can sustain high efficiency (cooperation and group welfare) only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The system is bistable: both high- and low-efficiency equilibria are possible. Small rates of random exploration (mutation) among enforcers can tip the system toward either high or low efficiency, depending on whether honest or corrupt exploration is more common. Providing information about enforcer honesty (i.e., making corrupt enforcers identifiable) greatly increases the likelihood of high-efficiency outcomes. The model shows that interventions targeting enforcer honesty (education, incentives, transparency) are more effective for improving efficiency than interventions targeting harvesters directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the integrity of the punishment institution. If enforcers are honest and/or can be identified as such, punishment can lead to high efficiency. If enforcers are corrupt and cannot be identified, punishment may fail and efficiency may be low. The model suggests that game design dimensions that increase the likelihood of honest enforcement (e.g., transparency, information sharing, enforcer education) will increase the efficiency gain from punishment. The presence of bistability means that initial conditions and small biases (e.g., in enforcer behavior) can have large effects. For prediction, this paper implies that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the effectiveness and honesty of the punishment mechanism, and on whether players can identify corrupt enforcers.

- source: De Silva, H., Hauert, C., Traulsen, A., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Freedom, enforcement, and the social dilemma of strong altruism. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical analysis and simulations demonstrate that costly punishment enables the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in joint effort games, but only when participation is voluntary. In the voluntary regime, the stationary distribution is dominated by punishers, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. In the compulsory regime, defectors dominate and group efficiency collapses. This result is robust for both 'weak' and 'strong' altruism (i.e., whether or not contributors benefit from their own contributions). The findings imply that the combination of voluntary participation and punishment is critical for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency is highly contingent on whether participation in the public goods game is voluntary or compulsory. If participation is voluntary, introducing punishment leads to a regime dominated by punishers and high group efficiency. If participation is compulsory, punishment does not prevent the dominance of defectors and group efficiency remains low. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of voluntary participation (i.e., whether players can opt out) is a key moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment. The model also suggests that these results are robust to variations in group size, population size, and the strength of selection, as long as the basic structure (voluntary participation, costly punishment) is maintained.

- source: Lee, J. H., Jusup, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2017). Games of corruption in preventing the overuse of common-pool resources. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that delegated punishment (enforcement) can support efficient, fully cooperative outcomes in common-pool resource games, but only if a sufficient fraction of enforcers are honest. If corruption is prevalent among enforcers, punishment loses its effectiveness, and the system converges to defection and resource overuse (low efficiency). The model exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions (notably the initial fraction of honest enforcers), the system ends up either in full cooperation (high efficiency) or full defection (low efficiency). Key parameters moderating the effect of punishment include group size (larger groups make cooperation harder), resource growth rate (higher growth rates favor defection), and the speed of resource dynamics (slower resource response masks the benefits of cooperation and impedes efficiency). The cost of harvesting has a mixed effect. The model provides direct predictions about how these dimensions affect the likelihood of efficient outcomes under punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like resource games depends critically on the honesty of enforcers and several game design dimensions. If punishment is implemented via delegated enforcement and enforcers are honest, punishment can enable high efficiency (full cooperation). However, if enforcers are corrupt, punishment fails and efficiency collapses. The model shows that increasing group size, increasing resource growth rate, and slowing resource dynamics all reduce the effectiveness of punishment in achieving efficiency. These findings should inform predictions about treatment efficiency: punishment will only improve efficiency if the enforcement mechanism is robust to corruption and the group size and ecological parameters are favorable. The model is most directly relevant to settings with delegated (not peer) punishment and where corruption is possible.

- source: Cooney, D. B. (2025). Exploring the Evolution of Altruistic Punishment with a PDE Model of Cultural Multilevel Selection. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment, when combined with group-level selection, can promote higher levels of cooperation and increase group efficiency compared to scenarios without punishment. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure (per-interaction vs. fixed), the strength of punishment, and the form of group-level competition. In some models, increasing punishment strength or cost leads to higher cooperation but lower efficiency, due to the collective cost of punishment outweighing its benefits. The paper provides analytical expressions for the threshold group selection strength needed to sustain cooperation and for the steady-state average payoff, showing that these can depend non-monotonically on punishment parameters. The results highlight that the design of punishment mechanisms and the structure of group competition are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, mechanistic, and quantitative guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with group structure. It shows that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the cost and strength of punishment, the type of cost (per-interaction or fixed), and the form of group-level competition. For prediction, the results suggest that: (1) punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when costs are per-interaction rather than fixed; (2) increasing punishment strength generally increases cooperation, but may decrease efficiency if costs are high; (3) the effect of punishment on efficiency can be non-monotonic, especially under group-level competition rules that normalize by maximum/minimum group payoffs; (4) the threshold group selection strength required for cooperation is lower with stronger or cheaper punishment. The paper's analytical formulas and numerical results can be used to estimate treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design parameters, especially in settings with group-structured populations and multilevel selection.

- source: Weibull, J. W., & Salomonsson, M. (2006). Natural selection and social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary stability and efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games depend on the structure of reproductive externalities. When group members' fitness is positively affected by others' payoffs (cooperative breeding), punishment and cooperation can become stable and lead to higher group welfare, especially if the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the cost is not too high. In contrast, under strong reproductive competition, punishment can be stable but may not increase group efficiency. The model shows that the presence and effectiveness of punishment as a mechanism for promoting cooperation and efficiency is highly sensitive to the underlying fitness function and game parameters. The analysis provides explicit conditions for when punishment leads to more efficient, cooperative outcomes and when it does not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on when punishment is likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like games, depending on the game design and the nature of social/reproductive externalities. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universal: it can be positive, nil, or even negative, depending on whether the environment favors cooperative or competitive externalities. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the structure of payoffs, and the degree of group interdependence (e.g., MPCR analogs) are critical moderators. The findings support the idea that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when group welfare is a significant component of individual fitness, and less likely (or even counterproductive) when individual competition dominates.

- source: Iwasa, Y., & Lee, J. H. (2013). Graduated punishment is efficient in resource management if people are heterogeneous. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that graduated punishment (punishment increasing with the harm caused by selfish actions) is only efficient—i.e., maximizes group welfare—when two conditions are met: (1) there is heterogeneity among players in their sensitivity to utility differences, and (2) there is a nonzero probability of false accusation (punishing prosocials by mistake). In standard rational or homogeneous quantal response models, optimal punishment is not graduated but rather jumps from zero to a high level. With heterogeneity and error, the optimal punishment increases smoothly with harm, matching the graduated punishment observed in successful resource management. The paper provides explicit formulas for optimal punishment and shows how efficiency-maximizing punishment depends on model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games depends critically on the presence of player heterogeneity and error in monitoring. In standard homogeneous or error-free settings, punishment is either absent or severe, with little efficiency gain from graduation. When heterogeneity and error are present, graduated punishment is optimal and can improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive in environments with player heterogeneity and monitoring errors, and that the optimal punishment level should increase with the harm caused by defection. The paper's analytic results can inform parameterization of efficiency predictions as a function of harm, benefit, error rates, and heterogeneity.

- source: Nikias, A., & Sy, A. (2021). Mutual monitoring in multi-period teams: an experimental investigation. *Team Performance Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering the cost of punishment in a two-period team production game (with payoffs analogous to a prisoner's dilemma) increases both the frequency of punishment and the rate of working (cooperation) in both periods. When punishment is costless, participants are more likely to punish non-cooperators, and this threat leads to higher cooperation. The design ensures that higher working rates directly translate to higher group output and expected utility, so efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly. The effect is statistically significant for both periods. The study isolates the effect of punishment cost by holding other factors constant and using a one-shot horizon to rule out future gains from cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player, two-period team production game with mutual monitoring and contingent punishment, reducing the cost of punishment increases cooperation and thus group efficiency. The design is closely related to public goods and prisoner's dilemma games, and the mapping from working to group payoff is explicit. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that lower punishment cost (punishment_cost dimension) will lead to higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, holding other design features constant. The findings are most directly applicable to small, short-horizon, mutual monitoring settings without communication or rewards.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The coevolution of altruism and punishment: Role of the selfish punisher. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote the evolution of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs) in both well-mixed and lattice-structured populations, but only under certain conditions. In the score-dependent viability model, punishment is more effective at suppressing defectors and promoting cooperation than in the fertility model. The presence of selfish punishers (defectors who punish other defectors) can help the evolution of altruistic punishers by first eliminating pure defectors, but pure altruists (who cooperate but do not punish) can hinder the evolution of punishment by being exploited. The benefit of cooperation, the fine imposed by punishment, and the cost of punishing all interact to determine evolutionary outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent and can be positive or negative depending on parameter values and population structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (group payoff) in public-goods-like games depends on the interaction of several design dimensions: population structure (well-mixed vs. lattice), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the benefit of cooperation, and the evolutionary update rule (viability vs. fertility). Punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when the punishment fine is large relative to the cost of punishing, and in spatially structured populations. However, if the cost of punishing is too high or if pure cooperators are common, punishment may not evolve or may even reduce cooperation. The findings suggest that predictions of treatment efficiency from control efficiency should account for these moderators, especially the structure of interactions and the punishment parameters. However, as the paper does not report explicit efficiency values, its support is indirect and based on evolutionary stability of cooperation.

- source: Perry, L., Shrestha, M. D., Vose, M. D., & Gavrilets, S. (2018). Collective Action Problem in Heterogeneous Groups with Punishment and Foresight. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing punishment in heterogeneous groups increases cooperation and group effort, especially when individuals have foresight and can anticipate the effects of their actions on others' future behavior. Foresight enables punishment to become utility-increasing, overcoming the second-order free-rider problem. However, the costs of punishment can be substantial, so higher cooperation does not always translate into higher group payoffs or efficiency. The model predicts a division of labor, with dominant individuals specializing in punishment and subordinates in contribution. The effect of punishment on group efficiency is context-dependent: it can be positive, negative, or neutral depending on parameter values (e.g., group size, benefit/cost ratio, hierarchy steepness, precision, and foresight weight).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the interplay between group heterogeneity, foresight, and the costs of punishment. If punishment is cheap and foresight is present, enabling punishment is more likely to increase efficiency by raising cooperation. If punishment is costly, the net effect on efficiency may be negative, as the costs of punishment can outweigh the gains from increased contributions. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is sensitive to group size (larger groups make cooperation harder), the benefit/cost ratio, and the degree of hierarchy. For prediction, the presence of foresight and the relative costs of punishment are key moderators. The model does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers qualitative guidance on when punishment will or will not improve efficiency.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Dieckmann, U. (2009). Runaway selection for cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured populations, the co-evolution of cooperation and punishment (with continuous strategies for both) can lead to runaway selection for both high cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. This dynamic is most pronounced when punishment is not too costly and cooperation is neither too cheap nor too expensive. The model shows that strict punishment norms (high strictness and severity) evolve naturally and reinforce high cooperation, resulting in much higher group-level cooperation than in the absence of punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed populations, punishment and cooperation do not evolve. The findings suggest that punishment can greatly enhance group cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, but only under certain cost structures and spatial conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group cooperation and, by extension, efficiency—especially when punishment is strict, severe, and not too costly.
