The model suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the cost structure of cooperation and punishment, and on the population structure (spatial vs. well-mixed). For prediction tasks, the findings imply that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when: (1) the game is spatially structured (analogous to local interactions), (2) punishment is strict and severe, (3) costs of punishment and cooperation are not too high, and (4) baseline cooperation is low without punishment. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical efficiency ratios, so its quantitative predictions should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Couto, M. C., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2020). Governance of risky public goods under graduated punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Graduated institutional punishment, where the severity and cost of punishment increase with the number of defectors, is more effective than strict (fixed) punishment in promoting cooperation and public good achievement in collective-risk dilemmas. This effect is especially pronounced at low risk levels and when both fines and costs are graduated. The model shows that graduated punishment reduces the time populations spend in all-defector states and increases the prevalence of cooperation and sustainable institutions. The approach also helps solve the second-order free-rider problem associated with institutional punishment. The findings are robust across a range of parameters and extend to linear public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in threshold public goods games with risk and institutional punishment, making punishment and its costs graduated (i.e., increasing with the number of defectors) leads to higher group achievement and overall welfare than strict, fixed punishment. While the main outcomes are not reported as efficiency ratios, the close relationship between group achievement, average fitness, and efficiency means the results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure (threshold, risk, institutional punishment), enabling graduated punishment will likely increase efficiency relative to strict punishment, especially at low risk and when punishment costs are also graduated. The results are robust to changes in group size, threshold, and other parameters.

- source: Amirova, I., Petrick, M., & Djanibekov, N. (2022). Investment traps and resilience to shocks: An experimental study of Central Asian collective water governance. *Irrigation and Drainage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat
  findings: The experiment found multiple stable equilibria (lock-ins) in collective investment in irrigation infrastructure. Communication (chat) treatments increased collective investment and made high-investment equilibria more likely, but did not guarantee them. Penalty (punishment) treatments, both low and high, reduced collective investment compared to baseline, contrary to theoretical expectations. The penalty treatments crowded out intrinsic motivation to cooperate, resulting in lower investment levels and less resilience to shocks. The main outcome is collective investment, which is a behavioral proxy for group efficiency, but the paper does not report explicit group payoffs or efficiency ratios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public-good-like field experiment with real farmers, introducing punishment (penalties) can reduce collective investment and thus lower efficiency-related outcomes, compared to a baseline with no punishment. Communication (chat) increases investment, but does not guarantee high efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar field settings may decrease efficiency, especially when intrinsic motivation is important. The main outcome is collective investment, not explicit group payoff, but the relationship is close. The findings are most relevant for field settings with real-world participants and repeated games with investment as the public good.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., Lelieveld, G. J., De Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & Van Dijk, E. (2022). Applying a logic of appropriateness to understand behavioral differences between common resource dilemmas and public good dilemmas. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, holding the underlying payoff structure constant, participants are more cooperative in CRDs than in PGDs. Punishment is used more frequently in CRDs, while reward is used more frequently in PGDs. These effects are robust to changes in the framing of the action (taking/leaving/giving/keeping). However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how the willingness to punish and cooperate differs between CRDs and PGDs, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper does not provide direct evidence, but it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used in CRDs and that cooperation is higher in CRDs. The game design dimensions are well described, so the paper is useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment opportunities, but not for predicting efficiency effects.

- source: Akpalu, W., & Martinsson, P. (2012). Ostracism and Common Pool Resource Management in a Developing Country: Young Fishers in the Laboratory. *Journal of African Economies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Allowing group members to ostracize (permanently exclude) over-extractors via majority vote, even at a cost to the remaining group, sharply reduced overfishing compared to a baseline with no ostracism. In the baseline, extraction effort increased over time toward the Nash equilibrium (over-exploitation), while with ostracism, effort dropped toward the social optimum. The effect was immediate and strong after ostracism was introduced. Voting to ostracize was targeted at over-extractors, and the number of votes received was the main determinant of reduced extraction. The costliness of ostracism did not prevent its use. The study does not report group payoffs or efficiency ratios, but the reduction in extraction effort implies improved efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a credible, group-administered punishment mechanism (ostracism) in a CPR environment can substantially reduce over-extraction and move behavior toward the social optimum, even when punishment is costly to the group. While efficiency is not directly measured, the reduction in extraction effort is a strong proxy for increased efficiency in this context. For prediction tasks, the presence of a strong, enforceable punishment mechanism (ostracism) is likely to increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, especially in settings with no communication, fixed group membership, and a known time horizon. The specific design (majority vote, permanent exclusion, cost to punishers) is important for generalization.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., & Vollan, B. (2017). Democracy and cooperation in commons management: experimental evidence of representative and direct democracy from community forests in Ethiopia. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Democratic procedures (either direct or representative) for rule or leader selection significantly increase cooperation in a dynamic CPR extraction game compared to imposed rules or leaders. The effect is not due to the presence of sanctions per se, but to the legitimacy conferred by democratic procedures. Sanctions only increase cooperation when democratically chosen. There is no significant difference in cooperation between direct and representative democracy. The main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or total group earnings, but the design allows for such calculation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that democratic procedures for rule or leader selection increase cooperation in CPR-like environments, and that sanctions only increase cooperation when democratically chosen. However, the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio), not efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation (lower HR) likely implies higher efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this. The game is a dynamic CPR extraction game, not a standard linear PGG, but is closely related. The findings suggest that the legitimacy of the process (democratic choice) is a key moderator of punishment/sanction effectiveness. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency, so its value for efficiency prediction is indirect and should be weighted accordingly.

- source: Esmaeili, P., Makui, A., Seyedhosseini, S. M., & Ghousi, R. (2022). The effect of probabilistic incentives to promote cooperation during the pandemics using simulation of multi-agent evolutionary game. *International Journal of Industrial Engineering Computations*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that probabilistic third-party incentives (reward and punishment) can trigger abrupt transitions from low to high cooperation in a networked volunteer dilemma game. Pure punishment is more effective than pure reward, but a combination of both (with a higher share for reward) is optimal for promoting cooperation at lower monitoring costs. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations and catastrophe theory analysis, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and probability of punishment (and reward) can cause sharp increases in cooperation rates in volunteer dilemma games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can be a powerful lever for increasing cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar public goods settings, but this is not directly demonstrated. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of incentive mechanisms and the potential for nonlinear effects, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Benard, S. (2012). Cohesion from Conflict: Does Intergroup Conflict Motivate Intragroup Norm Enforcement and Support for Centralized Leadership?. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that intergroup conflict increases the likelihood that group members will punish noncontributors (enforce norms) and support centralized leadership, but only when the outgroup is actively competing (i.e., high outgroup contribution). Conflict increases individual contributions to the group regardless of outgroup competitiveness. The effect of punishment is measured as the frequency of norm enforcement, not as its impact on group efficiency or total payoff. The presence of punishment does not appear to be experimentally manipulated as an on/off treatment; rather, punishment is always available, and the focus is on when and how much it is used. No direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about when group members are more likely to use costly punishment in public-goods-like games, specifically under intergroup conflict and high outgroup competition. However, it does not provide data on the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it compare efficiency with and without punishment. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, given game design dimensions and control efficiency. It does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used in conflictual, competitive intergroup settings, but does not address whether this improves or reduces group efficiency.

- source: Mulder, L. B., van Dijk, E., & De Cremer, D. (2009). When sanctions that can be evaded still work: The role of trust in leaders. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that the effectiveness of sanctions in promoting cooperation depends on both the trustworthiness of the leader who installs the sanction and the general trust participants have in authorities. When an alternative, more harmful defection option is available (i.e., sanctions can be evaded), participants with low general trust in authorities are less likely to cooperate if the leader is untrustworthy, and instead shift to the evasion option. High-trust participants are less sensitive to leader trustworthiness. The main outcomes are behavioral allocations; the paper does not report group payoff or efficiency, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of an evadable punishment option (i.e., a trilemma with an alternative defection route) can undermine the effectiveness of sanctions in promoting cooperation, especially among participants with low trust in authorities and when the leader is perceived as untrustworthy. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that in designs where punishment can be evaded, the positive effect of punishment on cooperation (and thus efficiency) may be reduced or even reversed for some subgroups. For prediction, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of evasion options and trust, but not about the quantitative effect on efficiency.

- source: McEvoy, D. M. (2012). Paying to punish on a free ride: member and nonmember punishments in voluntary coalitions. *Applied Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a public good game with voluntary coalition formation, allowing voluntary punishment (where any player can punish any other) does not increase public good provision relative to no punishment. However, when coalition members can credibly commit to punishing noncompliance (quasi-voluntary punishment), public good provision increases significantly. Both coalition members and nonmembers are equally likely to punish noncompliance, and the extent of nonmember punishment does not change when members can credibly enforce compliance. The main outcomes are contribution levels and punishment frequencies; efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that simply enabling voluntary punishment (without credible commitment) is not sufficient to increase efficiency or public good provision in coalition-based public good games. Only when punishment is structured so that coalition members can credibly commit to punishing noncompliance does cooperation (and likely efficiency) increase. For prediction, the presence of a credible, committed punishment mechanism is a key moderator; voluntary, non-committed punishment may have little or no effect on efficiency. The paper does not provide direct efficiency data, so predictions must be inferred from contribution outcomes.

- source: De Cremer, D., Hoogervorst, N., & Desmet, P. (2012). Procedural Justice and Sanctions in Social Dilemmas: The Moderating Effects of Group Feedback and Identification. *Journal of Applied Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that accurate sanctioning systems (those that monitor all contribution sessions) increase contributions relative to inaccurate systems, but this effect depends on group identification and feedback. For high group identifiers, procedural justice (accuracy) increases contributions only after group failure; for low identifiers, it increases contributions only after group success. The experiment uses a step-level public good game with a threshold and a punishment mechanism for the lowest contributor, but does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings. The findings highlight the importance of procedural justice in sanctioning systems for promoting cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about effects on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing contributions depends on the perceived procedural justice (accuracy) of the sanctioning system, and that this effect is moderated by group identification and feedback about group success or failure. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in step-level public good games with punishment, the design of the punishment system (especially its perceived fairness and accuracy) and the social context (identification, feedback) can strongly moderate behavioral responses, which may in turn affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment will be more effective at increasing cooperation (and possibly efficiency) when the system is accurate and procedurally just, but the effect is context-dependent.

- source: Zhang, B. Y., & Hofbauer, J. (2016). Quantal response methods for equilibrium selection in 2 x 2 coordination games. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a mini public goods game with punishment, the QRE method selects a cooperative equilibrium if the punishment is sufficiently strong (i.e., if the fine-to-fee ratio is high enough). The selection of cooperation depends on the effectiveness and cost of punishment. For intermediate punishment, cooperation can dominate for some parameter values even if the limiting QRE is defection. The results are derived analytically and illustrated with theoretical examples, but no empirical or simulated efficiency or payoff data are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical conditions for when punishment will lead to the selection of a cooperative equilibrium in a 2x2 mini public goods game. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it implies that strong punishment can shift equilibrium selection from defection to cooperation, which would be expected to increase efficiency in a public goods context. The results are most directly relevant for games with two players, binary choices, and explicit punishment options, and can inform predictions about the qualitative effect of punishment strength and cost on equilibrium selection, but not quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2024). Coevolution of norm psychology and cooperation through exapted conformity. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can evolve in collective action dilemmas when injunctive norms strongly encourage punishment, but not when they only encourage cooperation. Conformity (descriptive norm psychology) alone can support intermediate cooperation, but is unstable and easily undermined. When conformity is exapted (pre-existing), it can expand the conditions under which punishment-based cooperation evolves, acting as a scaffold for the emergence of stable, high-cooperation societies. The model identifies two stable types of cooperative societies: those based on conformity (unstable, lower cooperation) and those based on punishment (stable, higher cooperation). However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the importance of punishment norms (and the cost/benefit structure of punishment) in enabling high cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when conformity is present. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective than conformity at sustaining high cooperation, and that the presence of conformity can expand the parameter space where punishment-based cooperation emerges. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment mechanisms (especially when normatively encouraged) are likely to increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Cleve, J. (2016). Cooperation, conformity, and the coevolutionary problem of trait associations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the coevolution of cooperation, punishment, and conformist bias in a metapopulation model requires a strong initial within-group association (high F_ST) between these traits. If this threshold is not met, cooperation and punishment do not evolve to high frequency, and conformist bias does not stabilize cooperation. The threshold is around F_ST = 0.2 for large groups. The model assumes a public-goods-type group interaction with costly cooperation and punishment, and group competition is based on accumulated resources (which are higher with more cooperation). However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of the traits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus, by implication, efficiency) in public-goods-like environments depends critically on initial population structure (within-group association). In well-mixed or unstructured populations, punishment may not evolve or persist, and thus may not improve efficiency. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a controlled game setting. Its findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment can support cooperation in evolutionary or long-run settings, rather than for predicting short-run efficiency changes in experimental games.

- source: Solferino, N., Solferino, V., & Taurino, S. F. (2018). The economics analysis of a Q-learning model of cooperation with punishment and risk taking preferences. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public-goods-like game modeled with Q-learning, punishment can induce a stable cooperative equilibrium, but only if individuals have high enough exploration rates (i.e., are willing to try new strategies rather than always exploiting the best-known one). The required punishment must be proportional to the temptation to free-ride and increases with the exploration rate. If individuals are highly exploitative (low exploration), punishment is less effective unless intrinsic motivation for cooperation is high. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher cooperation rates would generally be associated with higher efficiency in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public-goods-like environments, but its effect depends on agents' willingness to explore new strategies (exploration rate/risk preference). The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in settings where learning and exploration are important. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it provides only indirect evidence for the prediction task. The model suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when exploration rates are high, but does not quantify efficiency outcomes.

- source: Clavien, C., & Klein, R. A. (2010). EAGER FOR FAIRNESS OR FOR REVENGE? PSYCHOLOGICAL ALTRUISM IN ECONOMICS. *Economics and Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that while experimental economics and neuroeconomics have revealed robust patterns of altruistic punishment and cooperation in public goods and related games, these studies do not provide decisive evidence about the underlying psychological motives (altruism vs. egoism). Behavioral outcomes such as costly punishment of free-riders are well established, but the interpretation of these actions (e.g., as motivated by fairness, revenge, or self-interest) remains open. Neuroimaging studies (e.g., de Quervain et al. 2004) show reward-related brain activation during punishment, but this does not conclusively distinguish between altruistic and egoistic motives. The paper argues that more fine-grained experimental designs and controls are needed to advance the debate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or variants. It is useful for understanding the psychological and motivational underpinnings of punishment behavior, but does not report or synthesize data on group efficiency, welfare, or total earnings. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers conceptual context but no quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes under punishment. It highlights the need for careful experimental design and the limitations of current evidence, but does not inform the mapping from game design dimensions to efficiency.

- source: Engelen, B. (2008). The sources of cooperation - On strong reciprocity and its theoretical implications. *Theory & Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that standard egoistic and instrumental accounts (kin selection, direct/indirect reciprocity, reciprocal altruism, costly signaling) cannot fully explain the high levels of cooperation and costly punishment observed in human experiments such as public goods games, ultimatum games, and trust games. Instead, it posits that 'strong reciprocity'—a norm-driven, non-instrumental willingness to cooperate and punish defectors—better accounts for the empirical findings. The paper highlights that such behavior is observed even in one-shot, anonymous interactions, and is motivated by internalized norms and emotions rather than by material self-interest. The author further argues that cultural group selection and the internalization of social norms are key to understanding the evolutionary origins of strong reciprocity and widespread human cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct quantitative or experimental evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, provide strong theoretical and interpretive support for the idea that the availability of punishment mechanisms can sustain higher levels of cooperation, even at personal cost, and that this is robust to one-shot and anonymous settings. For the prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment (especially when norm internalization is strong) is likely to increase cooperation, but it does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, but it does support the general mechanism by which punishment can increase cooperation in public goods environments.

- source: Zhosan, D., & Gardner, R. (2013). Problems of the commons: group behavior, cooperation and sanctioning in a two-harbor experiment. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment simulating a two-commons (two-harbor) resource dilemma, groups of 8 participants (students and professional fishermen) repeatedly chose how to allocate effort between two commons and a safe alternative. The experiment varied institutional features across blocks: baseline (no restrictions), enforcement (costly movement between commons), communication, and communication plus sanctioning. Efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) was the primary outcome. Communication led to large, significant increases in efficiency for all groups, with average net efficiency rising from ~0.8 to ~0.98. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings, then monetary penalties for repeated defection) further increased efficiency in most groups, sometimes reaching 1.0. The effect of sanctioning was generally positive but smaller than the effect of communication, and in one group, sanctioning led to a breakdown in cooperation. The results show that communication and sanctioning can both be effective in increasing efficiency in commons dilemmas, with communication having the largest and most consistent effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication among group members in a commons dilemma can dramatically increase efficiency, even before any formal or monetary sanctioning is introduced. The addition of a sanctioning mechanism (warnings and monetary penalties) yields further, though more modest, efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, the presence of communication and/or sanctioning should be expected to increase efficiency substantially relative to a control with neither. The effect of communication is robust and large; the effect of sanctioning is positive but may be context-dependent and sometimes less pronounced. The experiment's design and reporting allow for direct mapping to game design dimensions relevant for prediction.

- source: Buchholz, W., Falkinger, J., & Rübbelke, D. (2014). NON-GOVERNMENTAL PUBLIC NORM ENFORCEMENT IN LARGE SOCIETIES AS A TWO-STAGE GAME OF VOLUNTARY PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that voluntary contributions to a nongovernmental enforcement fund can sustain norm enforcement and increase public good provision (efficiency) in large societies, even when individuals are self-interested and there is no government. The positive effect of punishment/reward-based enforcement on efficiency is robust to increases in population size, provided the enforcement technology is nonrival. With rival enforcement, the positive effect diminishes as the population grows, but still remains above the no-enforcement baseline. The model also shows that the equilibrium level of public good provision is always below the first-best (Pareto-optimal) level, but is strictly higher than in the absence of enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment/reward mechanisms (via a funded enforcement agency) increases efficiency (public good provision) relative to the baseline voluntary contribution game without enforcement, especially when enforcement is nonrival. The model offers explicit comparative statics for how efficiency gains from punishment depend on player count, enforcement technology, and other design dimensions. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that the presence and effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms are key moderators of efficiency, and that the positive effect of punishment is robust to increases in group size if enforcement is nonrival, but attenuates with rival enforcement. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments with norm enforcement.

- source: Boyd, R., & Richerson, P. J. (2009). Voting with your feet: Payoff biased migration and the evolution of group beneficial behavior. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that payoff-biased migration—where individuals move from lower-payoff to higher-payoff groups and assimilate—can lead to the spread and predominance of group-beneficial behaviors, resulting in higher average group payoffs (efficiency). This effect is strongest when migration rates are low enough to maintain between-group cultural variation, but not so low as to prevent migration from having an effect. The model is general and can represent public goods, mutualism, and punishment-based norm enforcement. The presence of punishment is discussed as a mechanism that can stabilize group-beneficial equilibria, which are associated with higher group payoffs. The model predicts that, under plausible conditions, group-beneficial (efficient) norms will predominate, but alternative (less efficient) norms may persist in a polymorphic equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms which stabilize group-beneficial behaviors (including punishment) can lead to higher group efficiency, especially in structured populations with migration. The model is not a direct PGG experiment, but its payoff structure and discussion of punishment-based norm enforcement are closely related. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of effective punishment (or other norm-stabilizing mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when migration or group structure allows for between-group variation and selective migration. However, the model is abstract and does not provide quantitative estimates for specific game design parameters such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, so its guidance is qualitative: enabling effective punishment or norm enforcement should increase efficiency, especially when group structure and migration allow for the persistence and spread of efficient norms.

- source: Hsu, L. C. (2013). TAX AUDITING AS A PUBLIC GOOD GAME: AN EXPERIMENTAL STUDY ON PUNISHMENT AND COMPLIANCE. *Pacific Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The endogenous-audit (collective punishment) treatment produces much higher compliance rates than both the no-audit and random-audit treatments. In the final 10 rounds, compliance rates are 0.297 (no audit), 0.758 (random audit), and 0.921 (endogenous audit). However, average earnings are lower in the endogenous-audit treatment (85.2 points) than in the random-audit treatment (92.1 points), due to the distribution of fines and discovered taxes. Audit productivity (e.g., reported tax per audit) is higher in the endogenous-audit treatment in the latter half of the game. The main effect of punishment is to increase compliance, but the effect on efficiency (group payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly reported and may be ambiguous due to the cost of monitoring and the way fines are pooled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a collective punishment mechanism (endogenous audit) in a public-goods-like tax game increases compliance rates compared to both no-punishment and exogenous punishment (random audit) conditions. However, the effect on efficiency (group payoff relative to the full cooperation benchmark) is not directly reported and may be ambiguous, as average earnings are lower in the endogenous-audit treatment than in the random-audit treatment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment can increase compliance but may not always increase efficiency, depending on the cost structure and how punishment/fines are redistributed. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, audit cost structure, etc.) are well specified and can be mapped to the prediction task, but the lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation.

- source: Loukopoulos, P., Eek, D., Gärling, T., & Fujii, S. (2006). Palatable punishment in real-world social dilemmas? Punishing others to increase cooperation among the unpunished. *Journal of Applied Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that intermittent punishment (where only some group members are punished for defection in a given round) increases cooperation rates among those not directly punished, compared to a no-punishment baseline. This 'spillover effect' is robust to changes in the framing of the decision (individual vs. group focus) and to information about prior cooperation by others. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the payoff structure is such that increased cooperation would increase group earnings. The findings suggest that intermittent punishment can promote cooperation even among those not directly targeted by sanctions, and that this effect is not simply due to general factors that increase cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing intermittent punishment in a public-goods-like sequential social dilemma increases cooperation rates among unpunished group members, compared to no punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings suggest that even partial or probabilistic punishment can have positive behavioral effects on cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if the payoff structure rewards cooperation. The robustness of the spillover effect to framing and prior cooperation manipulations suggests that the effect of punishment on cooperation is not easily moderated by these factors. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that introducing punishment (even if not universal) can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Ehmke, M. D., & Shogren, J. F. (2009). Experimental methods for environment and development economics. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that experimental economics is a valuable tool for understanding behavior in environmental and development contexts, especially regarding cooperation, public goods provision, and the effects of punishment and peer enforcement. It reviews evidence that peer punishment (monetary and non-monetary) can increase contributions in public goods games, and that communication can sometimes be more effective than regulation. The paper also notes that the effectiveness of punishment and enforcement depends on social context, group composition, and cultural factors. However, it does not provide new quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods games, referencing key experimental studies. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or efficiency outcomes that can be used for prediction. Its main value is in summarizing the mechanisms and contextual factors that may moderate the effect of punishment, such as group composition, communication, and cultural context. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that punishment is generally associated with higher cooperation, but does not quantify the efficiency impact or provide game design-specific results.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Power, E. A. (2021). No good deed goes unpunished: the social costs of prosocial behaviour. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that prosocial acts can incur social costs, especially when observers infer self-interested motives or feel disadvantaged by others' generosity. It reviews evidence that in public goods games and similar environments, cooperators can be punished (antisocial punishment) or evicted, and that overtly generous individuals may be derogated or disliked. The review suggests that these social costs can limit the emergence and scale of cooperation, and that reputation management strategies (such as modesty or anonymity) are often used to avoid such costs. The paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, but highlights the importance of context, observer motives, and cultural variation in shaping responses to prosocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for understanding when punishment or sanctions may have counterproductive effects in public goods games and related environments, particularly through antisocial punishment of cooperators. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in a specific game design. It does suggest that the presence of antisocial punishment and reputational risks may reduce the positive impact of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, especially in contexts where observers feel threatened or infer strategic motives. For prediction tasks, this review supports the idea that punishment does not always increase efficiency and that context and social perception are important moderators.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Isaac, R. M., & Salmon, T. C. (2008). Endogenous group formation. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that group formation rules significantly affect both contribution behavior and efficiency in a public goods environment. Restricted entry (a form of social punishment) leads to higher contributions in mid-sized groups but smaller group sizes, resulting in lower overall efficiency compared to free entry/exit or restricted exit. Restricted exit leads to declining contributions over time and does not improve efficiency. The ability to punish by denying entry is more effective at increasing contributions than the ability to punish by reducing one's own contribution. However, the efficiency gains from higher contributions in restricted entry are offset by the smaller group sizes, so overall earnings are lower. The study highlights that the specific form of the punishment or sanction mechanism is crucial for its effect on cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the design of group formation and sanction mechanisms (such as voting to deny entry) can have substantial effects on both contribution behavior and efficiency in public goods games. However, the effect on efficiency is not always positive: mechanisms that increase contributions may also reduce group size, lowering overall group earnings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and type of punishment/sanction mechanism, as well as group formation rules, should be considered alongside standard game parameters. The findings caution that not all punishment-like mechanisms increase efficiency, and their effects may depend on how they interact with group size and formation dynamics.

- source: Lévy-Garboua, L., Montmarquette, C., Vaksmann, J., & Villeval, M. C. (2017). Voluntary Contributions to a Mutual Insurance Pool. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Contribution-based coverage, which ties insurance payouts to individual contributions, significantly increases the mean coverage rate (efficiency) compared to equal coverage, where payouts are independent of contribution. In the experiment (n=12, 50 rounds), the mean coverage rate is about 26% under equal coverage and 47% under contribution-based coverage for the full sample, with even higher rates for risk-averse (high-contributing) subgroups. However, full insurance is not achieved due to coordination failures and heterogeneity in risk attitudes. The endogenous 'punishment' of low contributors (via low coverage) is effective at raising efficiency, but only partially. Aggregate earnings are similar across treatments, but the distribution of earnings is more equitable under contribution-based coverage, with reduced transfers from high to low contributors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms which endogenously punish low contributors (by reducing their insurance coverage) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments with risk and voluntary contributions. The effect is robust but incomplete: efficiency rises from about 26% to 47% of the optimum when moving from equal to contribution-based coverage, but does not reach full efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing a punishment-like mechanism (here, endogenous sanctioning via coverage rules) in similar games. The results are most applicable to settings with continuous contributions, no explicit communication, and group sizes around 12. The paper does not study explicit costly punishment, but the contribution-based coverage mechanism functions as an endogenous, payoff-based sanction. Control efficiency (equal coverage) is low, and the treatment (contribution-based) nearly doubles efficiency. Heterogeneity in risk attitudes is a key moderator.

- source: Brent, D. A., Gangadharan, L., Mihut, A., & Villeval, M. C. (2019). Taxation, redistribution, and observability in social dilemmas. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Both Pigovian tax and increasing block rate mechanisms, when combined with redistribution, lead to significantly higher group efficiency (mean payoff as a ratio to the social optimum increases from 0.86 in baseline to 0.97–0.98 with tax, p<0.01) and lower inequality, regardless of whether individual decisions are observable. Observability alone (without tax) increases extraction and reduces efficiency (mean payoff ratio drops to 0.82), contrary to the expectation that it would promote cooperation. When tax mechanisms are present, observability has no significant effect on efficiency or extraction. There is no significant difference in efficiency between the two tax mechanisms, but the Pigovian tax reduces inequality more. The results are robust across group types and over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a CPR environment with heterogeneous extraction capacities, introducing a monetary sanction mechanism (taxation with redistribution) substantially increases efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, regardless of whether decisions are observable. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of observability (i.e., information about others' actions). The design is not a standard linear PGG, but the CPR game is closely related and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling a monetary sanction mechanism (taxation) will predictably increase efficiency, and the effect is not moderated by observability. The results do not speak directly to peer-to-peer punishment, but do inform the effect of centralized monetary sanctions.

- source: Herne, K., Kuyper, J., & Lappalainen, O. (2023). The Mode of Communication as a Driver of Sustainable and Equitable Asymmetric Common Pool Resource Use. *Environmental Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both unstructured and structured (deliberative) communication robustly increase group contributions and the efficiency of resource generation in an asymmetric common pool resource game, compared to a no-communication baseline. Deliberation (structured communication) reduces the impact of player position on contributions, leading to more equitable participation, but does not significantly increase total efficiency or equality of extraction compared to unstructured communication. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that communication—both unstructured and structured—substantially increases efficiency in asymmetric common pool resource games relative to a no-communication baseline. However, it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The design and results are highly relevant for understanding the effects of communication and deliberation, but not for punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, though it does establish a high-efficiency baseline for games with communication.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2019). Three-player repeated games with an opt-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a three-player repeated public goods game with an opt-out option (no explicit punishment), both tolerant and intolerant cooperators can survive, but their coexistence is not an evolutionary outcome. The equilibrium frequency of cooperators (and thus average payoff) increases with the benefit-to-cost ratio and decreases with the probability of group dissolution. The model shows that (partially) cooperative stable equilibria can exist, but the all-defector equilibrium is always asymptotically stable, making the initial evolution of cooperation difficult without additional mechanisms. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding the effect of voluntary partner refusal (opt-out) on cooperation and efficiency in small-group public goods games, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. It provides analytic results on how group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and group dissolution probability affect equilibrium cooperation and payoffs. However, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present. Its results may be useful as a baseline for comparison to punishment-enabled games, especially regarding the efficiency achievable through partner choice alone.

- source: Ohtsuki, H. (2004). Reactive strategies in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity settings with public-goods-game-type payoffs, discriminating strategies (which conditionally cooperate based on others' past behavior, akin to punishment of defectors) are not stable evolutionary outcomes. Instead, populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation (All C) or defection (All D), depending on whether the expected benefit of cooperation (modulated by information transfer and continuation probability) exceeds the cost. Discriminators can promote cooperation transiently by increasing the average 'discriminatoriness' in the population, but are ultimately outcompeted by more generous strategies. The paper also shows that errors (in perception or implementation) further destabilize discriminating strategies, making unconditional cooperation or defection more likely. The main outcomes are expressed in terms of average payoff (efficiency), and the model provides explicit threshold conditions for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-game-like environments with indirect reciprocity, strategies that punish defectors (by refusing to cooperate with them) do not lead to stable increases in group efficiency or payoff. Instead, such strategies are only transient promoters of cooperation, and populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation or defection depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio and information transfer. The model does not include explicit costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments, so its direct predictive value for the effect of enabling punishment in a PGG is limited. However, it suggests that punishment-like strategies may not robustly increase efficiency unless additional mechanisms (e.g., standing, justified punishment) are present. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that simply enabling punishment (in the form of conditional cooperation) may not reliably increase efficiency, especially in the presence of errors.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2023). Evolution of spite in an n-player game with an opting-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player games with an opting-out option, spiteful (costly harming) strategies can sometimes persist, but only under restrictive conditions: low cost-to-harm ratio, small group size, and specific opting-out rules. As group size increases, it becomes harder for spiteful strategies to achieve higher payoffs than nonspiteful strategies, and the basin of attraction for all-nonspiteful populations grows. The opting-out rule (how easily groups disband) is a key moderator: when homogeneous groups (all spiteful or all nonspiteful) are forced to disband, spite can persist more easily, but this is a somewhat artificial rule. The model is mathematically explicit about when spiteful strategies can dominate, and these conditions almost always correspond to lower group efficiency compared to all-nonspiteful populations. The analysis is directly about payoff and efficiency, not just behavior.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like n-player games with an opting-out mechanism, the introduction of costly punishment (here, spiteful harming) generally reduces group efficiency unless the cost-to-harm ratio is extremely low and the group size is small. As group size increases, the conditions for punishment/spite to persist become more restrictive, and group efficiency is more likely to be maximized when punishment/spite is absent. The details of the opting-out rule (e.g., whether homogeneous groups are forced to disband) can moderate this effect, but in most realistic settings, punishment/spite is not efficiency-enhancing. The model is not an exact PGG with standard punishment, but the payoff structure and evolutionary logic are closely related, and the results are directly about payoff/efficiency. Thus, the paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment/spite in large groups with standard cost structures will not increase efficiency, and may reduce it.

- source: Fontanari, J. F., & Santos, M. (2024). Solving the prisoner's dilemma trap in Hamilton's model of temporarily formed random groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the 'others-only' n-person prisoner's dilemma (a strong altruism scenario), cooperation cannot evolve under asexual reproduction or sexual reproduction with selfing, but can evolve and become stable under biparental sexual reproduction if the group benefit multiplier (r) is sufficiently large relative to group size (n) and the cost of cooperation (c) is not too high. The model does not include punishment or reward, and the main mechanism enabling cooperation is the genetic structure imposed by biparental reproduction. The main outcome is the probability that the population reaches the all-cooperator state (fixation probability), which is a direct measure of group efficiency or welfare. The results are summarized in phase diagrams showing regions where cooperation is stable, bistable, or unstable, as a function of c, r, and n. The findings are robust to demographic noise and extend to dioecious populations. The paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment, communication, or other institutional design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments that lack punishment or reward mechanisms, especially when the game structure is the 'others-only' variant of the n-person prisoner's dilemma. It shows that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (measured as fixation of cooperation and group payoff) is only achievable under specific genetic and demographic conditions—namely, biparental sexual reproduction, sufficiently high group benefit multiplier (r), and not-too-large group size (n) or cost (c). The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is generally low unless these conditions are met. However, the paper does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency difference between control and punishment-enabled conditions. Its main value is in establishing a baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is generally low except under the specified genetic/demographic conditions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment.

- source: Sugaya, T., & Wolitzky, A. (2023). Bad apples in symmetric repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in large repeated public goods or similar games, the possibility of sustaining cooperation and high efficiency depends critically on the ability to punish defectors specifically. If the only available incentives are rewards or withdrawal of provision (i.e., voluntary mechanisms), then cooperation collapses and efficiency is low when there are even a few commitment types (bad apples) who always defect. Involuntary punishment (such as fines) that can be targeted at defectors restores the possibility of cooperation and high efficiency. The main technical result is that if a 'population dominant' action exists (i.e., defecting yields a higher payoff than the population average, regardless of others' actions), then equilibrium efficiency converges to the inefficient outcome as the population grows. If no such action exists, and punishment can be targeted, then high efficiency is possible. The results are general and apply to a wide class of symmetric repeated games, including public goods games with or without transfers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods and related games. It implies that enabling targeted, involuntary punishment (e.g., fines for non-contributors) is necessary for sustaining high efficiency in large groups with possible commitment types. If only rewards or voluntary mechanisms are available, efficiency will be low in the presence of even a small fraction of defectors. The paper's results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is symmetric, the population is large, and there is a possibility of commitment types. For the prediction task, the presence or absence of targeted punishment (punishment_tech), the nature of the incentive (reward_exists, punishment_cost), and the ability to distinguish defectors are critical moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) will be low in large groups with commitment types unless involuntary punishment is available; enabling such punishment can dramatically increase efficiency.

- source: Foster, K. R. (2004). Diminishing returns in social evolution: the not-so-tragic commons. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes mathematical models of social evolution in group-structured populations, focusing on how the functional form of returns to investment (linear vs. diminishing) affects group performance (efficiency) in the face of selfish competition. It finds that, under diminishing returns, the negative impact of competition on group efficiency is greatly reduced, and group performance can be close to the optimum even with low relatedness and no explicit enforcement. The paper provides examples from social vertebrates, insects, and microbes, arguing that diminishing returns are common in real biological systems. Explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are not modeled, but enforcement is discussed as one of several mechanisms that can limit the tragedy of the commons.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of selfish competition in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the shape of the returns to investment. In prediction tasks, if the underlying game or biological system features diminishing returns to cooperation or competition, the efficiency loss from selfish behavior (and by extension, the potential efficiency gain from adding punishment) may be much smaller than predicted by linear models. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the paper does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of enabling punishment in a PGG. It does, however, suggest that in environments with diminishing returns, the baseline efficiency may already be high, potentially reducing the incremental value of punishment.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Stoddard, B. V., & Walker, J. M. (2019). The market for talent: Competition for resources and self-governance in teams. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Competition for a common-member's contributions creates 'winners' and 'losers': groups that start with higher contributions retain the common-member's loyalty and sustain higher contributions, while low-contribution groups decline further. Providing dedicated-members with more information about the common-member's contributions increases the common-member's total contributions and reduces discrimination between groups, but does not significantly raise dedicated-members' contributions in low-performing groups. Allowing ostracism (costless exclusion by majority vote) leads to higher and more equal contributions across groups, as both common- and dedicated-members increase contributions and the threat of exclusion disciplines low contributors. Ostracism is targeted at low contributors and rarely used against high contributors. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (ostracism) and information on contribution behavior in public goods games with divided loyalties. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that ostracism (a form of endogenous, costless group-level punishment) can increase and equalize contributions across groups, especially in settings where initial disparities would otherwise persist. The results also show that information about others' contributions can increase contributions by pivotal members. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling ostracism or increasing transparency may improve group outcomes in public goods games, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Gomez-Ruiz, L., & Sánchez-Expósito, M. J. (2020). The Impact of Team Identity and Gender on Free-Riding Responses to Fear and Cooperation Sustainability. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that strong team identity significantly reduces free-riding responses to fear and increases cooperation rates among men, but not among women. The effect of team identity on cooperation sustainability (maintenance of cooperation over time) is marginally significant for men and not significant for women. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. No formal punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; the study focuses on informal control (team identity) and gender differences. The findings suggest that team identity can be an effective informal mechanism to sustain cooperation among men in repeated social dilemmas with binary contribution choices and feedback, but not among women.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It studies a repeated binary-choice social dilemma (close to a public goods game) without any punishment or reward mechanism. The main manipulations are team identity and gender. The outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does provide contextual evidence about the role of informal mechanisms (team identity) and gender in sustaining cooperation. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in binary-choice, feedback-rich, no-punishment settings, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hauert, C. (2006). Spatial effects in social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that spatial structure can both promote and inhibit cooperation in social dilemmas, depending on the type of game (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, by-product mutualism, coordination) and the microscopic update rule. In strict prisoner's dilemma or public goods settings, spatial structure allows cooperators to survive by forming clusters, but the parameter range for this effect is limited. In snowdrift-type games, spatial structure can actually reduce cooperation. The effect of spatial structure is highly sensitive to the update rule: the death-birth process is most favorable to cooperation, while the replicator rule can lead to reduced cooperation in some parameter regions. The main outcome is the equilibrium fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it is informative about how spatial structure, group size, cost-benefit ratios, and update rules affect the equilibrium level of cooperation in group social dilemmas. If the prediction task involves spatial structure or local interactions, the findings suggest that such structure can have complex, sometimes counterintuitive effects on cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency. The absence of punishment as a mechanism means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The main value is in understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatially structured games.

- source: Billard, E. A. (1996). Evolutionary strategies of stochastic learning automata in the prisoner's dilemma. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in resource dilemmas (including public goods and commons dilemmas) is shaped by individual value orientation (pro-social vs egoistic), situational constraints (such as group identification, communication, and social norms), and the degree and type of uncertainty (social and environmental). It proposes that pro-social individuals are more likely to cooperate regardless of uncertainty, while egoistic individuals are more likely to defect, especially under uncertainty. Situational factors (including the possibility of sanctions or rewards) can constrain egoism and promote cooperation. The paper reviews prior findings that rewards increase cooperation and sanctions decrease defection, but notes that the costs of such interventions may outweigh collective gains and that effects may not persist when interventions are removed. The analysis is theoretical and hypothesis-generating, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on the psychological mechanisms that may moderate the effect of punishment, reward, and uncertainty on cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to their costs, and that their effects may not persist. The paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of situational constraints (including punishment/reward), group identification, communication, and uncertainty, and in warning that interventions may have costs that offset their benefits. However, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Salomonsson, M. (2010). Group selection: The quest for social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that group selection can, under certain conditions, support the evolution of cooperation and altruism in public-goods-like and Prisoner's Dilemma games. Three main approaches are discussed: fixation (requiring isolated pure groups), assortative group formation (e.g., via conformity or signalling), and reproductive externalities (where group-level payoffs affect evolutionary success). The paper notes that group selection is more likely to support cooperation when migration is low, groups are small, or positive externalities can be internalized. It also reviews how cultural mechanisms (like conformity) and group competition can foster social preferences. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, and only discusses punishment as a possible mechanism in the context of conformity and social preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group structure, migration, and mechanisms like conformity or signalling can affect the evolution of cooperation in public-goods-like games. It is useful for understanding the broader conditions under which cooperation and social preferences may arise, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group structure and the ability to internalize externalities are important moderators, but it does not inform the specific effect of punishment interventions in experimental public goods games.

- source: Centola, D. (2013). A Simple Model of Stability in Critical Mass Dynamics. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
