  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency, group payoff, and resource sustainability, especially when compared to environments without such mechanisms. The presence of monitoring and enforcement is highlighted as a key moderator: systems with sanctions are much more likely to achieve efficient outcomes than those relying on norms alone. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates but offers strong theoretical and qualitative support for the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment/sanctions should be coded as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially in settings analogous to common pool resources.

- source: Bohnet, I., & Baytelman, Y. (2007). Institutions and trust - Implications for preferences, beliefs and behavior. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional constraints, including a post-play punishment option, increase trustors' willingness to send money and trustees' willingness to return money in a trust game scenario, compared to an anonymous one-shot game. This suggests that efficiency (total group payoff) is higher with punishment. However, the presence of punishment and other institutional constraints reduces the role of intrinsic motivation (social orientation) in driving trust, supporting the 'crowding out' hypothesis. For trustworthiness, the effect of punishment is indirect: trustees return more because they expect to receive more, not because their intrinsic motivation changes. The study uses a within-subject design with senior executives and hypothetical payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that adding a punishment option to a trust game increases the amounts sent and returned, which is a proxy for increased efficiency. While the design is not a public goods game, the trust game with punishment is adjacent and the direction of effect is likely to generalize to PGG-like settings: punishment increases efficiency-related outcomes. However, the paper also highlights that the mechanism is not an increase in intrinsic motivation, but rather a shift in beliefs and expectations. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment in a similar game design will increase efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low. The effect is robust across several institutional manipulations, but the crowding out of intrinsic motivation may have long-term implications not captured by short-run efficiency.

- source: Wei, X., Xu, P., Du, S. T., Yan, G. H., & Pei, H. Y. (2021). Reputational preference-based payoff punishment promotes cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputational preference-based payoff punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas (PDG and SDG) significantly increases the fraction of cooperators. At low punishment intensity, both cooperators and defectors may have low or negative average payoffs due to the cost of punishment, but as punishment intensity increases, cooperators' average payoff and fitness rise, and defectors' payoffs drop to zero. At high punishment intensity, the system can reach full cooperation with high average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is more pronounced in the snowdrift game, where coexistence of cooperators and defectors persists over a wider parameter range. The study does not directly report group-level efficiency, but the increase in average payoffs for cooperators at high punishment intensity suggests a positive effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that introducing a costly, reputation-based punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas increases cooperation rates and, at sufficient punishment intensity, increases average payoffs for cooperators, leading to higher group efficiency. The effect is robust across both prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games, with stronger effects in the latter. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and shared cost) will increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, particularly in spatially structured games. However, the results are based on simulation, not empirical data, and do not use the public goods game directly, so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious but is likely informative.

- source: Rand, D. G., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Direct reciprocity with costly punishment: Generous tit-for-tat prevails. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, the addition of a punishment option does not increase cooperation or group payoffs in evolutionary simulations. Even when Nash equilibrium analysis allows for cooperative equilibria using punishment, evolutionary selection in finite populations favors generous tit-for-tat strategies that do not use costly punishment. Costly punishment is rarely used, and when it is, it is associated with lower payoffs. The presence of costly punishment does not allow cooperation to evolve in parameter regions where it would not already be supported by direct reciprocity alone. These findings are robust to variations in game parameters and are supported by experimental data from Dreber et al. (2008).
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling costly punishment in a repeated two-player PD-like environment does not increase efficiency or group payoff, regardless of the punishment cost or effectiveness. Evolutionary dynamics select against strategies that use costly punishment, and the most successful strategies are those that reciprocate cooperation and defect against defectors (generous tit-for-tat), not those that punish. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar repeated games, one should not expect an increase in efficiency or group payoff, and may even expect a negative association between punishment use and payoff. However, the results are specific to repeated two-player PDs and may not generalize to larger group public goods games.

- source: Hu, B., & Du, Y. X. (2023). Integrated analysis of employee cooperation and conflict behaviors in the context of digital technology. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (penalty for conflict) is a key determinant of stable cooperation in a group setting modeled as an evolutionary game. If the penalty for conflict exceeds half the cost of cooperation, cooperation becomes the evolutionary stable strategy, and the group achieves higher average perceived payoffs. If the penalty is lower, the group exhibits unstable, turbulent behavior with frequent reversals between cooperation and conflict, and the average perceived payoff from cooperation is often lower than from conflict. The model also shows that increasing the proportion of cooperators and the perceived benefit of cooperation stabilizes group behavior. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and simulation, not on real-world or laboratory data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the penalty for non-cooperation (punishment) above a critical threshold (half the cost of cooperation) can shift group behavior from unstable, low-payoff states to stable, high-cooperation, higher-payoff states. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators. However, the outcomes are modeled as perceived payoffs and behavioral states, not as direct group efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is a salient, well-understood, and enforceable mechanism, and less so for empirical prediction of actual group efficiency in experimental PGGs.

- source: Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2014). Selfish punishment with avoiding mechanism can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that introducing selfish punishment with an avoiding mechanism into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemmas, depending on the punishment cost, fine, and identification probability. The presence of selfish punishers allows for the stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers, and under certain parameter regimes, reduces the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders. Theoretical predictions using extended pair approximation and interaction web analysis match well with simulation results. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy type, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (especially when including mechanisms for selfish punishment and avoidance) can promote cooperation and reduce free riding in spatially structured social dilemmas. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and the main outcomes are strategy frequencies rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the cost/fine ratio and the ability to identify defectors, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and qualitative moderators of punishment effects, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Promote or hinder? The role of punishment in the emergence of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the pattern of punishment and the presence of anti-social punishment. Centralized (pool) pro-social punishment is more effective than decentralized (peer) punishment at promoting cooperation. When anti-social punishment is possible and both pro- and anti-social punishments are in the same pattern, the positive effect of punishment is largely neutralized. However, if pro-social punishment is centralized and anti-social punishment is decentralized, cooperation can still be promoted. The results are derived from theoretical evolutionary game models and focus on the stationary abundance of strategies, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative theoretical evidence about how punishment design (centralized vs decentralized, presence of anti-social punishment) can affect the evolutionary success of cooperation in social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that centralized punishment mechanisms are more robust to anti-social punishment and more likely to sustain cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that games with centralized punishment (pool) are more likely to see positive effects of punishment on efficiency, especially when anti-social punishment is possible. However, the results are based on evolutionary dynamics and strategy abundance, not direct efficiency measures, and the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game.

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). IS PUNISHMENT EFFECTIVE - COERCIVE STRATEGIES IN SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment is effective in increasing compliance (frequency of rewarding the punisher) when it is used frequently, contingently, and with high magnitude. The combination of high probability and high magnitude of punishment produces the highest compliance. Retaliation by the target does not increase with more frequent or stronger punishment, and negative affect toward the punisher is actually lower when punishment is strong and consistent, due to the resulting mutually rewarding exchanges. The effects are robust to the target's ability to retaliate and to variations in the probability and contingency of punishment. However, the main outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, but the frequency of compliance in a dyadic exchange. The results challenge classical exchange and bargaining theories that predict punishment provokes retaliation and reduces cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dyadic reciprocal exchange settings with both reward and punishment options, frequent and contingent punishment increases compliance (rewarding behavior) by the target, without increasing retaliation or negative affect. However, because the primary outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, and the setting is not a public goods game or direct variant, the findings should be transferred to PGG-like environments with caution. The results suggest that, under conditions of high punishment frequency, contingency, and magnitude, punishment can be effective at increasing the target's prosocial behavior, but the implications for group efficiency in multi-person public goods games remain indirect. The paper is most informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment rather than direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Rauhut, H. (2009). HIGHER PUNISHMENT, LESS CONTROL? EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE ON THE INSPECTION GAME. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the strength of punishment reduces both the rate of inspection (control) and the rate of theft (crime), contrary to the strict game-theoretic prediction that only inspection should decrease. The effect on inspection is strong and robust, while the effect on theft is also significant but not as strong as predicted by standard theory. The behavioral adaptation to changes in punishment is slower than predicted by rational learning models. The main outcomes are theft and inspection rates; efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured, but the design ensures that theft is inefficient (gamma = 0.5). Inspectees lose money over time, while inspectors' earnings are roughly stable. The findings suggest that punishment has mixed effects on strategic behavior in inspection settings, and that human adaptation is slower and less complete than predicted by theory.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the behavioral effects of punishment in an inspection game with inefficient theft, which is structurally adjacent to public goods games. The main outcomes are theft and inspection rates, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, since theft is inefficient (gamma < 1), reductions in theft due to higher punishment would increase group efficiency, while reductions in inspection (which is costly) could also increase efficiency if inspection is not needed. The paper shows that higher punishment reduces both theft and inspection, suggesting that the net effect on efficiency would depend on the relative magnitudes of these changes and the cost structure. The design and findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is formal, inspection is costly, and theft is inefficient. The lack of direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits the ability to calibrate quantitative predictions, but the qualitative direction of effects (higher punishment reduces both crime and control) is informative for adjacent settings.

- source: Dorrough, A. R., Olsson, M. I. T., Froehlich, L., Glöckner, A., & Martiny, S. E. (2021). Does she compensate the victim while he punishes the perpetrator? No gender differences in anonymous economic games across 11 nations. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two large studies (one multinational, one German student sample), there is no evidence for systematic gender differences in third-party punishment or compensation behavior in response to unfairness. Both men and women are more likely to compensate the victim than to punish the perpetrator. Increasing the effectiveness of punishment (punishment impact per unit cost) does not substantially change this pattern. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the game is not a repeated public goods game but a one-shot third-party punishment/compensation scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral use of punishment and compensation options in a third-party context, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves norm enforcement and costly punishment, but is not a repeated or group-based contribution game. The main decision-relevant finding is that, even when punishment is made more effective, participants prefer compensation over punishment, suggesting that punishment may be underutilized in similar designs. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not measured, the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding behavioral preferences for punishment versus compensation, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ferguson, E. (2021). Inequality averse and compassionate blood donor: implication for interventions. *Vox Sanguinis*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Blood donors are more likely to choose compensation (directly helping the victim) over punishment (sanctioning the perpetrator) in a one-shot 3PPC game, while organ donors are more likely to choose punishment. The compensation and punishment options are both costly to the third party, and the game is designed so that each £1 spent on compensation or punishment has a 2x effect. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, but describes how different choices affect the distribution of payoffs. A follow-up experiment finds that an intervention message based on advantageous inequality aversion increases non-donors' willingness to take information about blood donation, with some evidence that guilt-based appeals may have negative effects on current donors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about individual preferences for compensation versus punishment in a third-party context, which is adjacent to public goods games with punishment. The design is a one-shot, three-player game with both punishment and compensation options, and the main outcomes are behavioral choices, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of a compensation option may reduce the use of punishment, and that individuals' philanthropic identities (e.g., blood donor vs. organ donor) influence their preference for direct help versus norm enforcement. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in repeated public goods games, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited and indirect.

- source: Klempt, C., & Pull, K. (2018). The hidden costs of control revisited: Should a sanctioning policy be announced in advance?. *Managerial and Decision Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the way sanctions are implemented and communicated matters: when the principal preannounces a sanctioning policy, agents interpret this as a signal of distrust, which can reduce their voluntary effort (crowding out). When the sanctioning decision is not announced in advance or is made by a computer, agents do not interpret it as a negative signal, and effort is higher. The study provides evidence for both incentive and signaling effects of sanctions, but all results are in terms of individual effort, not group payoff or efficiency. There is no direct measurement or reporting of efficiency or total surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the importance of how punishment is announced and who imposes it. If the prediction task involves settings where punishment is preannounced and personalized, one should expect possible crowding out of intrinsic motivation, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. If punishment is not preannounced or is impersonal (e.g., random or computer-imposed), the negative signaling effect is absent, and the incentive effect dominates, likely leading to higher effort (and by analogy, higher efficiency). However, since the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, these implications are indirect and should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Lois, G., & Wessa, M. (2019). Creating sanctioning norms in the lab: the influence of descriptive norms in third-party punishment. *Social Influence*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants' third-party punishment behavior in a repeated dictator game was strongly shaped by social feedback about peer punishment. Exposure to high or low average peer punishment led to persistent differences in participants' own punishment levels, even after feedback was removed. The presence of a single free rider (a peer who never punished) in a group with otherwise high punishment reduced average punishment and increased the frequency of free riding, despite the same average peer punishment as the high group. These effects persisted over time, indicating the formation of stable sanctioning norms. The study demonstrates the importance of descriptive norms and the 'bad apple' effect in shaping costly punishment behavior, but does not directly measure or report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that descriptive norms and the presence of free riders can substantially moderate the level of costly punishment in repeated norm enforcement games. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned, free riding) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments. The study suggests that the presence of free riders can undermine the effectiveness of punishment as a norm enforcement tool, which could plausibly reduce efficiency gains from punishment, but this is not directly tested. The game is a repeated third-party punishment dictator game, not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency should be made with caution.

- source: Sánchez, A., & Cuesta, J. A. (2005). Altruism may arise from individual selection. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (altruistic punishment) can arise and persist in agent-based evolutionary models of the Ultimatum Game under individual selection, without the need for group or cultural selection, repeated interactions, or reputation. The key mechanism is the role of fluctuations and finite selection intervals, which allow altruistic punishers to survive and proliferate. The model reproduces the empirical finding that offers and acceptance thresholds are much higher than the self-interested Nash equilibrium. However, the paper does not analyze or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the distribution and evolution of behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the possibility that punishment (in the form of rejection of unfair offers) can evolve and persist under individual selection in one-shot, anonymous games. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding the evolutionary plausibility of punishment behaviors, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited, though it may inform mechanistic expectations about the robustness of punishment behaviors.

- source: Gummerum, M., López-Pérez, B., Van Dijk, E., & Van Dillen, L. F. (2022). Ire and punishment: Incidental anger and costly punishment in children, adolescents, and adults. *Journal of Experimental Child Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that experimentally induced incidental anger increases costly punishment of unfair offers in the ultimatum game (second-party punishment) for children, adolescents, and adults. In third-party punishment games, incidental anger increases punishment severity for unfair offers in adults and adolescents, but not in children. The effect of anger is specific to unfair (unequal) offers and does not increase punishment of fair (equal) offers. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment rates and severity), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the motivation for costly punishment in response to unfairness is influenced by emotional state and developmental stage, with self-relevance and norm appraisals playing key roles.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or related games. Its main contribution is to show that emotional state (incidental anger) can increase costly punishment of unfairness, especially when the violation is self-relevant (second-party) or, for adults, when it violates fairness norms (third-party). For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment rates and severity can be modulated by emotional and developmental factors, but it does not inform how these changes in punishment behavior translate into changes in efficiency or group payoff. The game designs used (ultimatum and third-party punishment games) are adjacent to, but not the same as, standard public goods games, and no efficiency or welfare outcomes are reported.

- source: Houser, D., Xiao, E., McCabe, K., & Smith, V. (2008). When punishment fails: Research on sanctions, intentions and non-cooperation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that threats of punishment (a $4 fine for not meeting the requested back-transfer) do not increase cooperation and can even reduce it, especially when the requested return is high relative to the sanction. This effect is the same whether the punishment is intentionally imposed by the investor or randomly assigned by nature, indicating that incentives, not intentions, drive the effect. When the sanction is used to enforce a low request, it increases full cooperation; when used to enforce a high request, it increases complete defection. The main outcomes are the percentage of the tripled amount returned and the distribution of cooperation/defection types. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation in trust/investment games depends on the relationship between the sanction size and the requested return. Punishment can reduce cooperation when the sanction is small relative to the demand. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for predicting behavioral responses (cooperation/defection) to punishment, especially in one-shot, two-player, trust-like games with continuous contribution and fixed punishment cost/effectiveness. The lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Myers, C. D. (2016). Participation and punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that when punishment is possible, participation (turnout) increases dramatically (from 60% to 88%). Punishment is overwhelmingly directed at abstainers, and abstainers incur a net loss from being punished. Egoist subjects are more responsive to punishment than pro-social subjects, leading to a more egoist composition among participants when punishment is available. However, because participation is costly and does not increase the value of the prize, increased participation does not improve group efficiency or welfare. The study does not report treatment effects on efficiency or group payoff, and the design makes clear that increased participation is not efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases participation in a binary-choice, team-based contest game where participation is individually costly and does not increase group welfare. However, it does not provide evidence that punishment increases efficiency or group payoff; in fact, the design ensures that increased participation is efficiency-reducing. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment depends critically on the structure of the game: in games where participation is not efficiency-enhancing, punishment may increase costly participation without improving (and possibly reducing) efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral responses (participation/cooperation) to punishment, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in standard public goods games.

- source: Li, J., Zhu, L. Q., & Chen, Z. (2018). The association between punishment and cooperation in children with high-functioning autism. *Journal of Experimental Child Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Punishment (administered by a third party, the experimenter) for defection in a repeated PDG increases the rate of cooperation among both HFA and TD children. The effect is robust to IQ and group (HFA vs TD), with no significant interaction. The study provides evidence that children with HFA understand the concept of punishment and respond to it by increasing cooperation. However, the study does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency or welfare cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing punishment in a repeated two-player PDG increases cooperation rates among children, including those with high-functioning autism. However, because the primary outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games (PDG vs PGG), and the punishment is third-party and automatic for defection. The study supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperative behavior, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or welfare effects.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2017). Effects of Gender on Costly Punishment. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that costly punishment is used frequently in a two-player prisoner's dilemma with a punishment option, primarily as moralistic punishment (punishing defection after cooperating). Participants spend more money punishing defecting female partners than male partners, but this is due to higher cooperation rates with female partners, not a direct gender bias in punishment. Female participants are more likely to cooperate with and punish female partners, while male participants do not differentiate by partner gender. The presence of punishment by partners increases cooperation rates, but the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it compare efficiency with and without punishment in a way that would allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment is used in a two-player prisoner's dilemma, and that the opportunity to punish is strongly influenced by cooperation rates, which in turn are affected by gender dynamics. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, nor does it provide a control (no-punishment) condition for direct comparison. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation when the threat of being punished exists, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games or their variants. The design is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral findings may inform models of punishment and cooperation, but not efficiency prediction per se.

- source: Fang, X., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of trust in the N-player trust game with individual reward and punishment. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The introduction of individual reward and punishment in the N-player trust game can promote the stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, which is interpreted as a promotion of trust. The size of the attraction domain for this coexistence increases with both the overall incentive strength and the share of incentives devoted to reward (as opposed to punishment). Reward is found to be more effective than punishment in promoting trust. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that mechanisms increasing trust and trustworthiness can move the system toward more socially optimal states.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment (and especially reward) mechanisms can promote cooperation-like behavior in trust-based social dilemmas. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report quantitative efficiency outcomes for control vs. punishment-enabled conditions. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its findings may be cautiously extrapolated to similar environments, especially regarding the positive effect of incentive strength and the relative effectiveness of reward over punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that increasing the strength and effectiveness of incentives (especially reward) is likely to expand the basin of attraction for cooperative outcomes, but does not provide direct efficiency data.

- source: Christoforou, E., Anta, A. F., Georgiou, C., Mosteiro, M. A., & Sánchez, A. (2013). Crowd Computing as a Cooperation Problem: An Evolutionary Approach. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment for cheating in a repeated master-worker (principal-agent) setting with reinforcement learning leads to faster convergence to a state where all workers cooperate (i.e., provide correct answers), compared to no punishment. However, punishment is not strictly necessary for eventual correctness if other conditions (such as sufficient reward relative to aspiration and cost) are met. The system is robust to high tolerance for defectors, but as group size increases, high tolerance can reduce the probability of achieving full cooperation. The main outcomes are the probability of cheating and the system's convergence to correctness, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it shows that punishment reduces the average cheating probability and accelerates convergence to a fully cooperative state. In settings where efficiency is closely tied to the rate of cooperation (e.g., if only correct answers generate group benefit), these results suggest that punishment would increase efficiency, especially in the short-to-medium term. However, the absence of explicit payoff or efficiency data limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task. The effects of group size, tolerance, and aspiration are also informative: higher group size and higher tolerance to defectors can undermine the effectiveness of punishment in achieving full cooperation.

- source: Chang, Q., & Zhang, Y. (2021). Peer pressure in extortion game can resolve social dilemma. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing peer pressure (punishment) into a spatial donation/extortion game can promote the prevalence of cooperation, especially at moderate punishment intensities. When punishment is too strong, extortioners dominate. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment helps cooperation, but high punishment harms it. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are based on simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment can promote cooperation in spatial evolutionary games with extortion strategies, but only within a certain range of punishment intensity. However, because the outcomes are frequencies of strategies rather than group payoff or efficiency, and because the model is a spatial donation/extortion game (not a standard public goods game), the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, but it does suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) is non-monotonic and context-dependent.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Chen, Z. Q., & Zhang, J. L. (2017). Punishment in the form of shared cost promotes altruism in the cooperative dilemma games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (where only cooperators punish and share the cost), the evolutionary outcome depends on the punishment parameters and group size. Higher punishment effectiveness (greater penalty to defectors) and lower cost to punishers promote the spread and fixation of cooperation. Larger group size and higher temptation to defect make cooperation harder to sustain. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation can dominate, coexist with defection, or be eliminated. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment with shared cost can promote the fixation of cooperation under certain parameter regimes, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. The effect of group size (player_count) and temptation to defect (analogous to MPCR) are also analyzed. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment with shared cost is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) when punishment is strong and not too costly, but the effect is weaker or negative when punishment is weak or costly, or when group size is large. However, because the model outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency, the translation to efficiency must be inferred.

- source: Sekiguchi, T., & Nakamaru, M. (2009). Effect of the presence of empty sites on the evolution of cooperation by costly punishment in spatial games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of empty sites in spatially structured populations (lattices) promotes the evolution of cooperation and does not reduce the effect of costly punishment. In contrast, in completely mixing populations, empty sites reduce the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation. The evolutionary success of strategies depends on the benefit of cooperation, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the density of empty sites. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather which strategies dominate under different parameter regimes. The findings suggest that spatial structure and population density (via empty sites) are important moderators of the evolutionary dynamics of punishment and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that spatial structure (analogous to networked or local interaction in PGGs) and the presence of empty sites (analogous to non-participation or dropout) can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. In spatial settings, punishment remains effective even with empty sites, while in well-mixed settings, empty sites undermine punishment's effectiveness. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to promote cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) depending on spatial structure and participation rates, but do not provide quantitative efficiency effects.

- source: Liu, W. W., & Yang, J. N. (2018). The Evolutionary Game Theoretic Analysis for Sustainable Cooperation Relationship of Collaborative Innovation Network in Strategic Emerging Industries. *Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that government intervention through incentives and punishments can significantly influence the frequency of positive cooperation between enterprises and URIs in collaborative innovation networks. When the sum of incentives and punishments exceeds the total additional cost (TAC) of cooperation, positive cooperation becomes stable. If incentives and punishments are too low, negative cooperation prevails. The model does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare, but focuses on the conditions for stable cooperation strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (and incentives) can increase the frequency of cooperative behavior in a multi-agent innovation network, but it does not quantify effects on efficiency or group payoff. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves free-riding and cooperation, but the outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies) rather than payoff-based. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, increasing punishment and incentives above a threshold can shift the system toward more cooperation, which may imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly modeled or measured.

- source: Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Banerjee, J., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2021). Eco-evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in the presence of policing. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that the introduction of punishment (policing) as a third strategy in a repeated PD game can lead to stable coexistence or oscillatory dominance cycles among cooperators, defectors, and punishers, depending on ecological and game parameters. The strength of punishment, temptation to defect, and ecological benefits (from 'free space') all modulate which strategies persist. Moderate punishment can suppress defectors and promote cooperation, but high temptation to defect can still undermine cooperation. The model also demonstrates that initial conditions and parameter choices can lead to multistability, with different attractors (e.g., dominance by one strategy, coexistence, or extinction). The focus is on the eco-evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can promote the persistence of cooperation in social dilemma settings, but the results are framed in terms of population dynamics (strategy frequencies and coexistence) rather than efficiency or group payoff. The model is based on a repeated PD game, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation. Therefore, while the findings support the idea that punishment can help sustain cooperation under certain conditions, they do not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency changes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment. The results may be useful for understanding qualitative moderators (e.g., the importance of punishment strength, temptation, ecological context), but are not directly transferable to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2020). The dynamics of injunctive social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of punishment (active disapproval) increases the stability and prevalence of norm compliance, can create multiple stable equilibria, and increases the complexity of group behavioral dynamics. Heterogeneity in individual values and costs leads to diverse group outcomes. Punishment stabilizes cooperative equilibria and is mainly administered by those with high norm internalization. The models are general and can be applied to a range of social norm contexts, including public-goods-like settings, but do not directly model or report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can stabilize norm compliance and cooperative behavior, especially in heterogeneous populations. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical data. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and cost of punishment, as well as heterogeneity in values and beliefs, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on group behavior. The models are adjacent to public goods games but do not map directly onto the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Bosworth, W. (2022). Social Power and Non-cooperative Game Theory. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper argues that the credibility of threats and offers (punishments and rewards) is central to understanding social power and governance in non-cooperative games. It shows that resources, reputation, and coordination problems determine the ability to credibly punish or reward, and thus to exercise power. The analysis highlights the importance of commitment mechanisms and tipping points in reputation for sustaining credible threats. However, the paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical insights into how punishment and reward mechanisms may affect cooperation and social power in collective action settings, emphasizing the importance of credibility, resources, and coordination. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is limited to high-level mechanism discussion and may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other sources.

- source: Toelstede, B. (2020). The Absence of Policing and the Political Economy of Contribution and Defection. *Democracy & Security*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in large groups, the disappearance of institutional punishment leads to a delayed decline in public goods contribution and a rapid increase in defection, while the reappearance of punishment leads to a delayed recovery of contribution and a rapid decline in defection. The length of these delays depends on factors such as trust in the punisher, transparency, media behavior, and regime support. The paper highlights the asymmetry between small and large groups, and between peer and institutional punishment, in terms of the speed and nature of behavioral responses. It also discusses the role of blame assignment and political manipulation in shaping these dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers theoretical and qualitative insights into how the effect of punishment on contribution (and by extension, efficiency) may be moderated by group size, institutional structure, trust, and information transparency. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that in large, real-world groups, the positive effect of punishment on contribution (and thus efficiency) may be delayed or attenuated compared to small, transparent, lab-like settings. The paper is not suitable for direct quantitative prediction but may inform qualitative expectations about moderators.

- source: López-Pérez, R. (2008). Aversion to norm-breaking:: A model. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which agents internalize social norms (specifically, an 'E-norm' combining efficiency and equality) and experience emotional costs (shame, anger) when violating or observing violations of these norms. The model predicts that people will cooperate and punish norm violators even at a cost to themselves, and that punishment is targeted at those responsible for norm violations. The model is shown to qualitatively account for a range of experimental findings in games such as dictator, ultimatum, trust, centipede, Cournot, and public goods games. In the context of public goods games, the model predicts that the introduction of punishment opportunities increases cooperation by deterring selfish behavior, as selfish agents comply to avoid being punished by principled agents. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical rationale for why punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance in public goods and related games, suggesting that the presence of punishment opportunities should increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment, especially when a sufficient proportion of players are 'principled' and willing to punish norm violations. However, the model is qualitative and does not specify the magnitude of efficiency gains or how they depend on specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR). The model does not provide direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game parameters, but supports the general expectation that punishment opportunities will have a more positive effect on efficiency in environments where norm internalization and willingness to punish are present.

- source: Ziegler, R. (1997). The normative structure of solidarity and inequality. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both dyadic and collective solidarity games (including public-goods-like settings), strategies that combine defection with active sanctioning of others (Hypocritical Cooperation) can robustly enforce high levels of solidarity (cooperation), provided the costs of sanctioning are not too high. However, this can also lead to 'overcompliance' or excessive norm enforcement unless checked by counterstrategies (Full Opposition). The evolutionary outcome depends on the mix of strategies, the costs and effectiveness of sanctions, and the distribution of 'poor' and 'rich' in the population. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that informal punishment can sustain cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that informal punishment mechanisms can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive and when the population includes actors motivated to enforce norms even while defecting themselves. However, the findings are based on simulation and focus on the prevalence of strategies and 'level of solidarity' rather than explicit group efficiency or payoff. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency will depend on the cost and effectiveness of sanctions, the strategic mix in the population, and the degree of inequality. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, so its value for direct prediction is limited to qualitative insights about when punishment is likely to be effective.

- source: Bshary, R., & Bergmueller, R. (2008). Distinguishing four fundamental approaches to the evolution of helping. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper clarifies that punishment is a key control mechanism in the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, especially in repeated game structures. It distinguishes punishment from other mechanisms (e.g., reward, sanctions, partner switching) and situates it within a broader taxonomy of strategies that can stabilize helping behavior. The authors emphasize that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on the game structure, ecological context, and cognitive abilities of the players. However, the paper does not provide quantitative findings or direct predictions about efficiency outcomes in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. It supports the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation when certain conditions are met (e.g., repeated interactions, ability to inflict costs, cognitive capacity for contingent strategies), but it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding which dimensions (e.g., repeated rounds, punishment cost, cognitive requirements) are theoretically important moderators of punishment's effect on cooperation and efficiency. It does not provide direct evidence for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Midlarsky, M. I. (2004). Nihilism in Political Chaos: Himmler, bin Laden, and Altruistic Punishment. *Studies in Conflict & Terrorism*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that in the absence of effective political institutions, groups may achieve cooperation and unity through costly punishment of defectors, a process the author terms 'altruistic punishment.' Drawing on public goods game experiments (Fehr & Gächter, 2002), the author claims that the availability of punishment increases cooperation, and extends this logic to explain extreme forms of group cohesion and violence in historical and contemporary political movements. However, the paper does not report any new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in group settings, referencing public goods game experiments as evidence. However, it does not provide empirical data, efficiency outcomes, or direct analysis of game design dimensions. Its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited to its summary of prior experimental findings (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, 2002), not to any new or directly relevant evidence.

- source: Smith, D. (2020). Cultural group selection and human cooperation: a conceptual and empirical review. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the mechanisms often posited by CGS models to stabilize cooperation—such as conformist social learning and a norm psychology that motivates indiscriminate punishment—are not strongly supported by empirical evidence. Instead, individuals tend to use adaptive, context-sensitive strategies, and punishment is typically self-interested, aimed at increasing future cooperation or personal payoff rather than enforcing norms for their own sake. The review also notes that institutions facilitating cooperation often arise endogenously through rational design rather than through stochastic group-level processes. While punishment can stabilize cooperation, its effectiveness and the resulting group efficiency depend on the alignment of individual and group interests, the structure of institutions, and the presence of incentives. The review does not provide new quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants, but it synthesizes theoretical and empirical arguments relevant to the design and interpretation of such experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important conceptual guidance for predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It suggests that the impact of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether punishment is self-interested (aligned with individual and group payoffs) or norm-based (potentially maladaptive). The review argues that, in practice, punishment is usually self-interested and embedded in institutional contexts that align individual incentives with group benefit, which tends to support higher efficiency. However, it cautions that models assuming indiscriminate or maladaptive punishment may not reflect real human behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that game designs with punishment mechanisms are more likely to increase efficiency when they include features that align individual and group interests (e.g., rewards for punishers, reputation systems, endogenous institution formation), and less likely to do so when punishment is costly, norm-based, and not incentivized. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers a framework for interpreting when and why punishment may or may not improve efficiency.

- source: Nakamaru, M. (2006). Lattice models in ecology and social sciences. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes theoretical and simulation results showing that lattice-structured populations (as opposed to well-mixed populations) can promote the evolution of cooperation and punishment (Altruist-Punisher), as well as spiteful behavior, depending on the model's updating rules. In particular, the Altruist-Punisher strategy can invade selfish populations more easily in lattice models, and punishment is more effective in promoting cooperation in these settings. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on the evolutionary success of strategies and the spread of norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that spatial or network structure (such as lattice models) can moderate the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's findings are only adjacent: they suggest that punishment may be more effective in structured populations, but do not quantify the effect on efficiency or provide data on payoff outcomes. The paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and contextual moderators than for direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Andrews, J., & Davidson, D. J. (2013). Cell-Gazing Into the Future: What Genes, Homo heidelbergensis, and Punishment Tell Us About Our Adaptive Capacity. *Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that punishment is a fundamental mechanism for stabilizing cooperation in large groups, enabling the evolution and maintenance of pro-social norms and group-level adaptation. It synthesizes evidence from evolutionary theory, ethnography, and game theory to claim that humans' unique adaptive capacity is rooted in our ability to cooperate, which is supported by both formal and informal punishment of defectors. The paper does not present new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but it does highlight the importance of punishment for sustaining cooperation and adaptive capacity in complex social systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms are important for sustaining cooperation in group settings, which is relevant to public goods games and their variants. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in experimental games. The arguments may inform the expectation that enabling punishment will generally increase cooperation and potentially efficiency, but the paper does not address how specific game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. Thus, its value for quantitative prediction of treatment efficiency is limited to high-level theoretical justification for including punishment as a positive moderator of cooperation.

- source: Ashlock, D., Smucker, M. D., Stanley, E. A., & Tesfatsion, L. (1996). Preferential partner selection in an evolutionary study of Prisoner's Dilemma. *Biosystems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in dyadic repeated Dictator Game settings, observers rate those who withdraw cooperation from defectors as more cooperative and less vengeful than those who punish. Both withdrawers and punishers are seen as less exploitable than non-sanctioners, but only when the sanctioner ends up with a positive payoff. Punishers are least preferred as future partners. The reputational consequences of punishment are negative compared to withdrawal, and the effect on perceived exploitability depends on whether the sanctioner retains a positive payoff. No actual efficiency or group payoff is measured, so the findings are about perceptions and reputational inferences, not realized payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about reputational perceptions in dyadic games, not about actual payoffs or efficiency. However, it suggests that punishment may have reputational costs compared to withdrawal of cooperation, and that the effect of sanctions on perceived exploitability depends on whether the sanctioner retains a positive payoff. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the game structure is dyadic, not group-based.

- source: Syi (2014). Hidden patterns of reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper formalizes and compares four second-order action rules for direct reciprocity (e.g., Tit-for-tat, Contrite Tit-for-tat, Pavlov, Grim Trigger) and four second-order assessment rules for indirect reciprocity (e.g., Image Scoring, Simple Standing, Stern Judging, Shunning). It shows that these rules can be paired and exhibit similar patterns, especially in their relationship to punishment. The analysis highlights that moderate punishment policies (e.g., Simple Standing, Stern Judging) are most successful at maintaining cooperation, while extreme policies (e.g., Shunning, Grim Trigger) can undermine cooperation. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insight into how different punishment and reciprocity rules may affect cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its value for the prediction task is limited to theoretical framing: it suggests that moderate, justified punishment rules are more likely to sustain cooperation (and thus potentially higher efficiency), while overly harsh or overly lenient rules may be less effective. However, these insights are not tied to specific game design dimensions or empirical efficiency data.

- source: Morese, R., & Palermo, S. (2020). Altruistic Punishment and Impulsivity in Parkinson's Disease: A Social Neuroscience Perspective. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence that altruistic punishment is a key mechanism for maintaining cooperation and is influenced by neural systems affected in PD, such as the dopaminergic reward system and the default mode network. It highlights that PD patients, especially those with impulse-control disorders and on dopaminergic medication, may show altered punishment behavior. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or group payoff, and its main focus is on the mechanisms and behavioral expressions of punishment rather than on payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and mechanistic context for how punishment behavior may be altered in populations with neurodegenerative disorders like PD, especially in relation to impulsivity and dopamine. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. As such, it offers limited direct support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment from game design dimensions, but may be useful for understanding potential moderators (e.g., impulsivity, neurobiological state) in special populations.

- source: Berger, J., & Hevenstone, D. (2016). Norm enforcement in the city revisited: An international field experiment of altruistic punishment, norm maintenance, and broken windows. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that direct and indirect norm enforcement behaviors (including costly punishment) occur in natural, anonymous, one-shot interactions, but at lower rates than in laboratory experiments. Enforcement is more common in Switzerland than in New York City. The presence of disorder (litter) reduces the likelihood of both direct and indirect enforcement. Making it easier to maintain the norm (by being close to a trashcan) increases norm maintenance behaviors. Subtle sanctions and norm maintenance are more frequent than direct confrontation. Individual characteristics (in-group status, age) also predict enforcement. The study does not report any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so its findings are not directly transferable to predictions about group efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that costly punishment and other forms of norm enforcement are less frequent in real-world, anonymous, one-shot settings than in laboratory public goods games. It also shows that environmental context (disorder, opportunity structure) and location matter for the frequency of enforcement. However, because the study does not measure group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value for prediction is as indirect, contextual evidence about the real-world prevalence and moderators of costly punishment, suggesting that laboratory findings may overestimate the frequency and possibly the efficiency impact of punishment in naturalistic settings.

- source: Eriksson, K., Strimling, P., Andersson, P. A., Aveyard, M., Brauer, M., Gritskov, V., Kiyonari, T., Kuhlman, D. M., Maitner, A. T., Manesi, Z., Molho, C., Peperkoorn, L. S., Rizwan, M., Stivers, A. W., Tian, Q. R., Van Lange, P. A. M., Vartanova, I., Wu, J. H., & Yamagishi, T. (2017). Cultural Universals and Cultural Differences in Meta-Norms about Peer Punishment. *Management and Organization Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that across eight countries, punishment of norm violators in a resource-sharing scenario is judged as more appropriate when enacted by the group rather than by an individual. There is substantial cross-cultural variation in the appraisal of punishers versus non-punishers: in more individualistic countries (USA, Netherlands, Sweden, Japan), punishers are rated less favorably than non-punishers, while in more collectivistic countries (Pakistan, UAE, Russia, China), this difference is absent or reversed. These patterns hold for both retributive and restorative forms of punishment. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are limited to social appraisals and meta-norms about punishment, not behavioral or payoff outcomes. However, it may inform predictions about the acceptability or likelihood of punishment being used in different cultural contexts, which could indirectly affect the implementation or effectiveness of punishment in real-world or experimental settings.
