For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only contextual or indirect evidence, not direct evidence about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.

- source: Suzuki, K., & Ishiwata, R. (2022). Impact of a Carbon Tax on Energy Transition in a Deregulated Market: A Game-Based Experimental Approach. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study used a 4-player, 30-round online game simulating energy companies in a deregulated market, with and without a carbon tax (punishment for fossil fuel use). The carbon tax was introduced in the second half of the game and was known to players in advance. The main findings are: (1) The carbon tax significantly increased renewable energy usage after its implementation, accelerating the energy transition. (2) Total group profit (efficiency) was higher with the tax, but the difference was not statistically significant. (3) The tax did not significantly affect R&D investment or market price in aggregate, but did increase market price after the tax was imposed. (4) Information about future taxation alone did not change behavior or reduce uncertainty; only actual taxation affected actions. (5) Players' perceptions of uncertainty and obstacles to transition were not reduced by the tax. Overall, the carbon tax improved efficiency-related outcomes by promoting renewables and increasing group profit, but only after its implementation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (carbon tax) in a competitive, multi-player, repeated game increases group efficiency (total profit) and accelerates the transition to socially optimal behavior (renewable energy use). The effect is most pronounced after the punishment is actually implemented, not merely announced. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is salient and directly affects payoffs. The game structure is adjacent to a public goods game, with competition and investment choices, so the findings are most applicable to PGG variants with market competition and investment dynamics. The effect size on efficiency is positive but may not always reach statistical significance, especially in small samples.

- source: Chassang, S., & Takahashi, S. (2011). Robustness to incomplete information in repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoners' dilemma games, the set of robustly sustainable equilibrium payoffs (i.e., efficiency) is sharply affected by concerns about incomplete information. Under complete information, grim-trigger strategies can sustain cooperation if the discounted value of future cooperation exceeds the temptation to defect. However, when robustness to incomplete information is required, the cost of being the 'sucker' (cooperating while the other defects) becomes critical, and the set of sustainable payoffs can shrink dramatically. Selective-punishment strategies, which punish only the deviator, are more robust than grim-trigger strategies, and can sustain cooperation under a wider range of parameters. The paper provides explicit characterizations of when efficient cooperation is robustly sustainable and when only inefficient outcomes (permanent defection) are robust. The main outcomes are theoretical characterizations of efficiency/welfare as a function of game parameters and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma–like games under incomplete information. It shows that the design of the punishment mechanism (grim-trigger vs. selective punishment), the cost of being punished, and the enforceability of asymmetric outcomes are critical moderators. If only symmetric cooperation (CC) is enforceable, robust efficiency collapses to permanent defection. If asymmetric outcomes (DC or CD) are enforceable, robust efficiency can be as high as in the complete information case. The results are directly about efficiency/welfare, not just contribution rates. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment scheme and the game's payoff structure, especially under incomplete information. However, the results are for repeated prisoners' dilemma, not public goods games per se, so transfer to PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2004). How should we define goodness? Reputation dynamics in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that only a small subset of reputation assignment rules—those that treat defection against bad individuals as good (i.e., as a form of justified punishment or sanction)—can sustain high efficiency (group payoff close to the maximum possible) in indirect reciprocity games. These 'leading eight' rules enable stable cooperation by allowing individuals to refuse help to bad actors without losing their own good reputation. The model shows that, under these rules, the average payoff in the population approaches the maximum possible (b-c), minus a small term due to errors. The results are robust to error rates and to the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as b > c. The paper provides a formal evolutionary justification for the importance of sanctioning/punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in reputation-based social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms—specifically, the ability to sanction or refuse help to bad actors without reputational cost—are critical for sustaining high efficiency in indirect reciprocity environments. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related: it is a repeated, pairwise helping game with a social dilemma structure. The findings suggest that, in environments where individuals can sanction defectors (and where such sanctioning is not itself punished), group efficiency can approach the maximum possible. For prediction tasks, this implies that enabling punishment or justified sanctioning in a game design is likely to increase efficiency, provided the reputation system is designed so that justified punishment does not harm the punisher's reputation. The results are robust to errors and to variations in the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as cooperation is individually costly but socially beneficial.

- source: Nakamura, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2014). Indirect reciprocity in three types of social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player simultaneous-move social dilemmas, the stability and efficiency of cooperation via indirect reciprocity depend on the structure of the social norm (reputation updating rule) and the underlying game. In Snowdrift and Prisoner's Dilemma, strong punishment of defectors (assigning bad reputations for defection against good players) is necessary to maintain high efficiency (i.e., stable cooperation with high group payoff). In Stag Hunt, such punishment is less critical, and norms that unfairly favor reciprocators (always assign good reputation to reciprocation) can enhance the robustness and efficiency of cooperation. The paper also shows that punishing unconditional cooperators (who help bad players) is important for maintaining efficient cooperation in all three games. The efficiency outcome (i.e., group payoff relative to full cooperation) is maximized under certain social norms, but the effect of punishment-like mechanisms is context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (via reputation loss) on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the underlying game structure and the details of the punishment mechanism (social norm). In games with strong temptation to defect (SG, PD), enabling punishment (bad reputation for defectors) increases efficiency by stabilizing cooperation. In coordination games (SH), such punishment is less necessary, and norms that favor reciprocators can be more effective. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency should be moderated by the type of social dilemma and the specific implementation of the punishment/reputation mechanism. The results are most directly relevant to two-player games, not standard multi-player PGGs, but the logic may transfer to small-group settings with reputation-based punishment.

- source: Levine, D. K., & Pesendorfer, W. (2007). The evolution of cooperation through imitation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models where players can imitate successful strategies and occasionally innovate, the long-run outcome depends on the ability to identify others' strategies. With perfect identification, efficient cooperation enforced by punishment is stable and the group achieves high efficiency. With imperfect or noisy identification, punishment becomes less effective, and the long-run outcome is less efficient, sometimes even less efficient than the static Nash equilibrium. The model shows that punishment strategies that condition on signals about others' types can sustain cooperation, but only if those signals are sufficiently informative. The presence of strategies that can 'masquerade' as cooperators (i.e., avoid detection) can undermine cooperation and efficiency unless masquerading is costly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on the information structure—specifically, the ability to identify defectors. If the game design allows for perfect or highly informative identification of defectors, punishment can sustain efficient cooperation. If identification is noisy or easily circumvented, punishment is less effective and may not improve efficiency. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one should pay close attention to the information available to players about others' actions or types, and to the possibility of undetectable defection or masquerading. The model is not an empirical test, but it provides a mechanistic basis for expecting positive, null, or even negative effects of punishment on efficiency depending on these design features.

- source: Castro, L., Serrano, J. M., & Toro, M. A. (1998). Conceptual capacity to categorize and the evolution of altruism. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the Assessor strategy, which incorporates a punishment-like mechanism (reprobation) based on observed or reported selfish behavior, achieves higher fitness (i.e., greater group efficiency or payoff) than standard reciprocal strategies like Tit-for-Tat or Observer Tit-for-Tat when competing against always-defect strategies. This advantage is robust across both unstructured and family-structured populations, provided certain thresholds of cooperation frequency are met. The Assessor strategy is especially effective when individuals can observe or be informed about others' reprobation of selfish behavior, allowing them to withhold cooperation from defectors more efficiently. The model predicts that such punishment-like mechanisms can support higher levels of cooperation and group payoff than reciprocity alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms (here, reprobation) in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency/payoff relative to control conditions without such mechanisms. The findings are most directly relevant to iterated prisoner's dilemma settings, which are adjacent to public goods games, and suggest that the ability to observe and act on others' punishment signals is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, the results imply that, all else equal, introducing a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when players can observe or learn about others' punishment actions. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes or empirical validation.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The leading eight: Social norms that can maintain cooperation by indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations, only eight specific social norms (the 'leading eight') can maintain both evolutionary stability and a high level of cooperation/efficiency, even when errors in action and assessment occur. These norms require players to cooperate with good-reputation individuals, punish defectors, justify punishment, and allow for apology and forgiveness. Under these norms, the average payoff per interaction is close to the theoretical maximum (b-c), and the fraction of cooperative (good) players remains high, with only a small reduction due to errors. The paper provides analytical conditions for when these norms are stable and efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations and one-shot interactions, the structure of the social norm (reputation dynamics) is critical for achieving high efficiency. Specifically, norms that combine justified punishment (refusing to help bad actors and not being penalized for it) and forgiveness (allowing recovery from bad reputation) can maintain efficiency close to the maximum. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms in repeated or reputation-based social dilemmas. The results suggest that, when punishment is structured as justified refusal to cooperate (and not as costly peer punishment), and when forgiveness is possible, efficiency losses due to punishment can be minimized or avoided. The paper does not address group size, communication, or other PGG-specific dimensions directly, but it does show that the design of sanctioning and reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2008). Mutualism, market effects and partner control. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the threat of terminating an interaction (partner control) can suppress cheating and maintain cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the relative abundance of exploiters and victims (market effects). When victims are rare, they can enforce cooperation and achieve higher payoffs; when they are common, exploiters can cheat more, reducing the efficiency of the interaction. If exploiters gain more control over the duration of interaction, they can extract more benefit, potentially driving the system into parasitism (negative payoffs for the victim). The model provides explicit conditions for when mutualism (positive payoffs) or parasitism (negative payoffs) will occur, and shows that efficiency is maximized when partner control is strong and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like partner control on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the structure of partner choice and market conditions. If the 'punishment' (exit threat) is credible and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters, efficiency is increased. If exploiters can easily find new victims, or if they gain control over the interaction, efficiency can decrease or even become negative for the exploited party. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will depend on parameters analogous to partner abundance, search costs, and control over interaction duration. The model does not map directly onto standard PGGs, but its logic is relevant for settings where partner choice or exit is possible.

- source: Gintis, H. (2003). The hitchhiker's guide to altruism: Gene-culture coevolution, and the internalization of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that internalized prosocial norms, including altruistic punishment, can become evolutionarily stable through gene-culture coevolution, especially when the fitness benefits of prosocial norms are high, extraparental socialization is strong, and assortative mating is present. Simulations show that groups with internalized altruistic punishment achieve high mean fitness and eliminate antisocial norms. Altruistic punishment is particularly effective at sustaining cooperation and group efficiency because its cost is lower and more concentrated than the cost of direct cooperation. The models predict that, in the presence of punishment, group efficiency and mean fitness are higher than in its absence, especially in large groups or when repeated interaction is infrequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments increases group efficiency and mean payoff, especially when direct cooperation is not stable due to high costs. The models suggest that punishment is a robust mechanism for sustaining cooperation and high group payoff, even in large groups or with high migration, as long as the cost of punishment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where internalized norms and cultural transmission play a role, and less so to short-run laboratory games, but the core logic supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like dilemmas.

- source: Engle-Warnick, J., & Slonim, R. (2006). Inferring repeated-game strategies from actions: evidence from trust game experiments. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study uses a novel inference method to map observed actions in repeated trust games to underlying strategies. In indefinite games (with a continuation probability of 0.8), many subjects use harsh trigger strategies that punish non-cooperation, supporting cooperative outcomes and higher payoffs. Over time, the proportion of play consistent with these strategies increases, and average payoffs move toward the cooperative equilibrium. In finite games (5 rounds), strategies with end-game defection dominate, and payoffs decline toward the non-cooperative equilibrium. The paper provides evidence that punishment-like strategies (grim trigger) are effective in sustaining cooperation and higher payoffs in indefinite games, but not in finite games.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (in the form of harsh trigger strategies) in improving efficiency or payoffs depends strongly on the game horizon: indefinite repetition with sufficient continuation probability enables punishment to sustain cooperation and higher payoffs, while finite repetition leads to unraveling and lower payoffs. The trust game is not a standard PGG, but the findings are likely to transfer to other repeated social dilemmas with similar strategic structure. The presence of punishment strategies is associated with more positive efficiency effects in indefinite games, but not in finite games.

- source: Furuzono, Y., Yamakawa, T., Ohnishi, S., & Kaneko, T. (2013). Combined effects of social pressure from the inside and outside of fishing communities promote cooperation for stock management. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled socioeconomic-bioeconomic model of a fishing community, where individuals choose to cooperate with management or not, and non-cooperators experience psychological punishment ('social pressure') that depends on both internal (community-based) and external (outside community) sources. The model shows that when both internal and external social pressures are sufficiently strong, the system can reach full cooperation, resulting in sustainable fish stocks and maximum sustainable yield (MSY)—a proxy for group efficiency. If only one type of social pressure is strong, or both are weak, the system may stabilize at lower cooperation and lower stock levels. The model highlights the importance of both community relationships and external societal pressure in promoting cooperation for resource management.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that psychological punishment mechanisms (social pressure) can increase group efficiency in common-pool resource settings analogous to public goods games, especially when both internal and external sources are present. While the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, it shows that the presence and strength of punishment-like mechanisms (social pressure) are critical moderators of group outcomes. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling both internal and external punishment/sanction mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency, while the absence or weakness of either reduces the effect. The model also suggests that the effect of punishment is moderated by group size (player count), as social pressure per individual decreases with larger groups.

- source: Pin, P., & Rogers, B. W. (2015). Cooperation, punishment and immigration. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the punishment (expulsion) rate for immigrant defectors increases the overall cooperation rate in society, but decreases the cooperation rate among citizens. This is due to an equilibrium substitution effect: as immigrants are more strongly incentivized to cooperate, the overall level of cooperation rises above its 'natural' level, making defection more attractive for citizens. The model shows that the net effect of punishment is smaller than would be predicted if equilibrium effects were ignored. The paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but expected payoffs for different agent types are derived. The results are robust to heterogeneity in agent preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment targeted at a subset of players (immigrants) can increase overall cooperation rates, but may also induce more defection among the untargeted group (citizens), reducing the net effect on cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff may be less positive than expected if substitution effects are present. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its quantitative value for predicting efficiency is limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it is based on a networked prisoner's dilemma with binary (all-or-nothing) choices and dynamic linking, rather than a standard PGG. The findings are most relevant for understanding equilibrium behavioral responses to targeted punishment policies.

- source: Lehmann, L., & Keller, L. (2006). The evolution of cooperation and altruism - a general framework and a classification of models. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and altruism can only evolve when at least one of four general conditions is met: (1) direct benefits to the actor, (2) repeated interactions with information about partner behavior, (3) preferential interactions with kin, or (4) genetic linkage between recognition and helping traits (greenbeard effect). Punishment, coercion, and policing are highlighted as mechanisms that can shift the cost-benefit ratio in favor of cooperation, making it more likely to evolve and persist. The paper reviews and classifies a large number of theoretical models, showing that all can be mapped onto these four categories. It emphasizes that punishment is effective in promoting cooperation when it increases the cost of defection relative to cooperation, but notes that the stability of punishment depends on its own cost and on whether it is linked to cooperation genetically or behaviorally.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase the efficiency (fitness, group payoff, or productivity) of cooperative systems by making defection less attractive. It does not provide quantitative predictions for specific game designs, but it clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its ability to alter the cost-benefit ratio and on the presence of at least one of the four general conditions for cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments if it is not prohibitively costly and if it is implemented in a way that aligns with one of the four evolutionary pathways. However, the paper does not address specific design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, chat) in the context of laboratory or field experiments, so its guidance is primarily mechanistic and qualitative.

- source: Jung, Y. B., Hall, J., Hong, R. Y., Goh, T., Ong, N., & Tan, N. (2014). Payback: Effects of relationship and cultural norms on reciprocity. *Asian Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that reciprocity norms (reward and punishment) are shaped by relationship and culture. Collectivists are more generous to friends after positive treatment and less likely to punish after negative treatment (even to strangers), sometimes offering an 'olive branch' (positive or neutral response to negative treatment). Individualists are more likely to punish norm violators (destroy money at a cost to themselves) regardless of relationship. The main dependent variable is the amount given or destroyed, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not use a public goods game or a repeated multi-player setting, but rather a dyadic hybrid trust-dictator game with a single round (though participants believed there would be five rounds).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral drivers of punishment and reward in dyadic trust/dictator game settings, especially the influence of culture and relationship on willingness to punish at a cost. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or multi-player repeated setting. The findings may inform expectations about the likelihood and intensity of costly punishment or reward in similar dyadic games, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the psychological and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: DAVIS, D. D., & HOLT, C. A. (1994). EQUILIBRIUM COOPERATION IN 3-PERSON, CHOICE-OF-PARTNER GAMES. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the ability to punish by switching partners does not increase cooperation in two-stage games compared to single-stage controls, but does lead to much higher cooperation rates in ten-stage games. The increase in cooperation in longer games is attributed to both the opportunity for reputation building and the higher cost of being punished (switched away from) early in a long sequence. The punishment mechanism is salient and does not require coordination. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that salient, unilateral punishment mechanisms (switching partners) can increase cooperation rates in repeated games, but only when the game is sufficiently long to allow for reputation effects and to make the cost of being punished significant. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the number of rounds (num_rounds) is a critical moderator of punishment effectiveness on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results are most informative for predicting behavioral (cooperation) outcomes rather than efficiency, and the game is not a standard public goods game but an adjacent partner-selection game with similar strategic features.

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2013). The coevolution of culture and environment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly resource conservation (including punishment of defectors) can evolve in structured populations facing renewable resource constraints and environmental shocks. Groups with many defectors are more likely to overharvest and collapse, while groups with more cooperators and punishers are more resilient. Punishment reduces the within-group advantage of defection, but is itself costly. Environmental shocks (weather) accelerate the elimination of non-cooperative groups. Between-group interactions (resource-conflict and harvest-sharing) modulate the evolution of cooperation: resource-conflict increases the frequency of resource conservation by increasing collapse of defector-heavy groups, while harvest-sharing reduces group collapse and allows more defection. Larger group size has a non-monotonic effect: above a threshold, larger groups are more likely to collapse, which can favor the evolution of conservation. Policies that increase the cost of extraction or penalize defectors promote conservation. However, the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome, focusing instead on behavioral strategy frequencies and group survival.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment and resource conservation can evolve and persist in group-structured resource dilemmas, especially under environmental variability and when group collapse is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model's outcomes are mainly about the frequency of cooperative/punishing strategies and group survival, not efficiency as defined for the prediction task. Thus, while the mechanisms discussed (e.g., punishment reducing defection, group collapse selecting for cooperation) are relevant for understanding when punishment might improve group outcomes, the paper does not provide data or results that can be directly mapped to efficiency predictions for specific game design dimensions.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2011). Resolving the iterated prisoner's dilemma: theory and reality. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that while the strict assumptions of the IPD are rarely met in nature, the pay-off structure (temptation to cheat, mutual investment) is relevant to many real-world cooperative interactions. Punishment (negative reciprocity) is identified as a mechanism that can sustain cooperation in IPD-like games, as illustrated by the cleaner fish example, where dominant individuals punish defectors to promote future cooperation. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is sustained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation in iterated social dilemmas, especially when cooperation is not all-or-nothing and when partner choice or punishment is possible. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically vary game design dimensions. As such, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, but it does support the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in IPD-like settings.

- source: Giardini, F. (2012). Deterrence and transmission as mechanisms ensuring reliability of gossip. *Cognitive Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that deterrence (punishment for unreliable gossip) and transmission (the ability to hide the source of information) jointly ensure the reliability and evolutionary stability of gossip. It suggests that the threat of punishment alone would make gossip too risky, but the ability to transmit information as 'reputation' (with an unknown source) allows individuals to avoid direct responsibility and punishment. The paper references agent-based simulations showing that when agents can only use direct experience, average payoffs are lower than when communication (gossip/reputation) is allowed. It also cites experimental economics studies where reputation mechanisms increase cooperation and prevent overuse of public resources. However, these findings are not original to this paper and are used to support the theoretical argument.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and reputation mechanisms may affect cooperation and, indirectly, group efficiency in social dilemmas. It does not provide direct empirical evidence or parameterized results that can be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it supports the general idea that punishment (deterrence) can sustain cooperation, but only when paired with mechanisms (like transmission/reputation) that allow information to spread without excessive risk to informers. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation and possibly efficiency, but the effect depends on the balance between deterrence and the ability to avoid punishment via transmission. The lack of direct empirical or payoff data limits its utility for precise prediction.

- source: Gallo, E., Riyanto, Y. E., Roy, N., & Teh, T. H. (2022). Cooperation and punishment mechanisms in uncertain and dynamic social networks. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated networked Prisoner's Dilemma with 12 players per group and endogenous link formation/removal, the presence of reputational uncertainty (15% chance that intended actions are flipped) leads to a substantial decrease in both cooperation rate and welfare (group payoff), regardless of whether the network is updated slowly or quickly. Fast network updating (more frequent opportunities to change links) has only a marginally positive effect on welfare and cooperation, and does not offset the negative impact of uncertainty. The mechanism is that uncertainty makes players more lenient and forgiving in network punishment (less likely to remove links from defectors), which disrupts the formation of cooperative clusters. Action punishment (retaliatory defection) is also weakened under uncertainty. The study provides direct evidence that punishment mechanisms relying on accurate reputation signals are less effective in promoting efficiency when monitoring is noisy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dynamic networked repeated games with endogenous partner selection, the effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (here, network punishment via link removal) in increasing efficiency is highly sensitive to the accuracy of reputation signals. When there is reputational uncertainty, punishment is less effective and group efficiency (welfare) is significantly reduced. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of noise or uncertainty in monitoring should be treated as a major moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency. The results also indicate that simply increasing the frequency of punishment opportunities (e.g., faster network updating) does not robustly increase efficiency, especially when uncertainty is present. The findings are most directly relevant to networked PD games, but the mechanisms may transfer to PGG-like environments with endogenous partner choice and noisy monitoring.

- source: Lacomba, J. A., Lagos, F., Reuben, E., & van Winden, F. (2014). On the escalation and de-escalation of conflict. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in one-shot or randomly matched (Strangers) conflict games, conflict expenditures are high and escalate over time, leading to low efficiency (mean earnings well below the endowment). When losers can destroy resources (akin to punishment), conflict expenditures decrease, and in the 'Resistance' game (where destruction can be conditioned on the winner's appropriation), mean earnings are significantly higher than in the baseline. However, destruction itself is wasteful and only increases efficiency if it deters appropriation and reduces conflict. In repeated interaction (Partners), a substantial minority of groups achieve long-lasting peace (zero conflict, high efficiency), but most do not. Peace is achieved through costly signaling (not fighting, low appropriation), not through destruction. Overall, the effect of punishment-like destruction on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by reducing conflict, but can also reduce it if destruction is frequent. The design dimensions (matching protocol, post-conflict options) strongly moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, the ability of losers to destroy resources) on efficiency in conflict games is highly context-dependent. In one-shot or random-matching settings, allowing punishment (destruction) can reduce conflict expenditures and, if destruction is conditional on appropriation, can increase efficiency. However, if destruction is frequent, efficiency may not improve. In repeated games, the possibility of peace (high efficiency) depends more on costly signaling and restraint than on punishment. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (destruction) is likely to reduce conflict and increase efficiency only if it credibly deters appropriation and is not overused. The matching protocol (repeated vs. random) and the structure of post-conflict options are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is low in these games; enabling punishment can increase efficiency in some variants (notably Resistance/RE), but not always.

- source: Chalub, F. A. C. C., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2006). The evolution of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that, in a two-level selection model with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, simple and unambiguous social norms emerge that maximize group efficiency (average payoff). The dominant norm (10011001) enforces cooperation by rewarding help to good individuals and punishing refusal to help good individuals or helping bad individuals. This norm allows for forgiveness and rapid recovery of reputation, but also implacable punishment for bad actions. Under this norm, cooperative strategies (CO, OR) dominate, and islands reach maximal possible fitness. The model demonstrates that the evolution of such norms is robust across a wide range of benefit parameters (b), and that a small amount of migration between islands is essential for the evolution of efficient norms and strategies. The findings support the idea that reputation-based punishment and norm enforcement can sustain high efficiency in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that reputation-based punishment (loss of reputation for non-cooperation) in repeated social dilemma games can lead to the evolution of norms that maximize group efficiency. The model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a give-and-receive game with binary actions and reputation updating. However, the core mechanism—punishment via reputation loss, indirect reciprocity, and norm enforcement—closely parallels the logic of punishment in public goods games. The findings suggest that, when such mechanisms are present, enabling punishment (via reputation or direct sanctions) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The results are robust across a range of benefit-to-cost ratios and do not depend on fine-tuning of parameters. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that introducing effective punishment or norm enforcement in repeated social dilemma environments will increase efficiency, especially when group structure and reputation are salient.

- source: Takahashi, S. (2010). Community enforcement when players observe partners' past play. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement—whereby third parties punish cheaters based on first-order information about partners' past play—can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with random matching. Under the independent and indifferent equilibrium concept, for sufficiently high discount factors, any symmetric group payoff between 0 (full defection) and 1 (full cooperation) can be achieved. The paper also shows that these results are robust to noise in actions or records, and that the equilibrium construction extends to finite populations. The threshold for sustaining cooperation does not depend on population size under this mechanism, in contrast to contagious equilibria. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and analyzes the stability of cooperation under shocks and noise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with random matching and first-order information, enabling community enforcement (punishment by third parties based on partners' past play) can increase group efficiency up to the full cooperation level, provided players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to population size and some forms of noise. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in analogous public-goods-game-like environments, enabling punishment mechanisms (even with limited information) can substantially increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the discount factor is high. The paper also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the structure of the stage game (supermodular vs. submodular) and the information available to players.

- source: Inaba, M., Takahashi, N., & Ohtsuki, H. (2016). Robustness of linkage strategy that leads to large-scale cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that a linkage strategy—where defectors in a social dilemma are excluded from indirect reciprocity benefits—can be evolutionarily stable and maintain high group efficiency, but only when there are implementation errors in the social dilemma and no perception errors. In the absence of errors, linkage and non-linkage strategies are neutral, allowing non-linkage strategies to drift in and eventually enable defectors to invade, collapsing cooperation and efficiency. Perception errors (misjudging others' actions) undermine the stability and efficiency of linkage strategies, as they disrupt the ability to assortatively cooperate and exclude defectors. The results highlight that the effectiveness of exclusionary sanctions (linkage) for maintaining efficiency is highly sensitive to the type and prevalence of errors in the system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like exclusion mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that exclusionary sanctions (linkage) can increase efficiency, but only if players can reliably observe and agree on others' actions (i.e., low perception error) and if there is some noise in implementation (so that linkage strategies can distinguish themselves from non-linkage cooperators). If perception errors are common, the efficiency benefit of punishment/exclusion is undermined. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct costly punishment, and where the information environment is well-specified. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, but the mechanisms and moderators (error types, information) are highly relevant for prediction.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2021). Cooperation evolves by the payoff-difference-based probabilistic reward. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic reward mechanism, where the probability of rewarding a cooperator increases with the payoff difference between the player and the cooperator, leads to higher average payoffs and more cooperation in most cases. The effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, though the strength and range of the effect depend on the network structure and the reward parameter t. The paper does not directly study punishment in this model, but situates its findings in the context of prior work on punishment and reward in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a well-designed reward mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially under certain network structures and parameter ranges. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare punishment and reward within the same experimental or simulation framework. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by analogy and through its discussion of prior punishment studies.

- source: Fishman, M. A. (2006). Involuntary defection and the evolutionary origins of empathy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated dyadic cooperation games with involuntary defection, unconditional punishment (retaliation for any defection) undermines cooperation and reduces efficiency, as it leads to cycles of mutual defection. In contrast, an empathic retaliator strategy—where punishment is withheld if the opponent's defection follows one's own involuntary defection—can be evolutionarily stable and support high efficiency, especially when the expected number of repeated interactions is large. The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the cost of helping, the probability of being able to help, and the expected number of interaction cycles. The paper provides explicit conditions and payoff matrices for these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms critically affects efficiency in repeated cooperation games. Specifically, unconditional punishment (retaliation) in the presence of involuntary defection leads to lower efficiency, while empathic (conditional) punishment can sustain high efficiency if repeated interactions are frequent. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on whether punishment is unconditional or conditional/empathic, the frequency of repeated interactions, and the possibility of involuntary defection. However, the model is not a public goods game, and punishment is not costly in the sense of standard PGG experiments; rather, it is refusal to help. Thus, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution, but the qualitative insight is that punishment can either harm or help efficiency depending on its design and the game context.

- source: Fehr, D., & Sutter, M. (2019). Gossip and the efficiency of interactions. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that enabling third-party gossip in a repeated trust game increases both trustor investments and trustee return ratios, resulting in higher efficiency (total group earnings) compared to a baseline with no third party and to a treatment with only observation (no communication). Efficiency in the Gossip treatment is 13% higher than baseline and 5% higher than Observation. The effect is robust to the cost of gossip and the amount of information available to gossipers, but is reduced when gossip is noisy (messages may be misdelivered). The presence of a third party who can observe and potentially gossip is more effective at increasing efficiency than simply providing first-hand information to trustors. The results suggest that the threat or possibility of gossip acts as a cost-effective social sanction, increasing cooperation and efficiency without direct material punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, enabling a third-party gossip mechanism (even when costly and unverifiable) increases efficiency relative to both a baseline and an observation-only condition. The efficiency gain is substantial and robust to variations in the cost and informativeness of gossip, but is reduced if gossip is unreliable. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely relevant for adjacent environments where indirect social sanctions (gossip, reputation) are possible. For prediction, the presence of a gossip/observation mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, with the largest gains when gossip is reliable and can be selectively transmitted. The effect is not due to direct punishment, but to reputational concerns and information transmission.

- source: Fonseca, M. A., & Peters, K. (2018). Will any gossip do? Gossip does not need to be perfectly accurate to promote trust. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling gossip (reputational information transmission) in a repeated trust game increases both trust and trustworthiness, leading to higher efficiency (joint payoffs) compared to a no-message baseline. Even when gossip is substantially inaccurate (either endogenously or exogenously), efficiency remains higher than in the absence of gossip, though it is lower than with accurate information. The effect is robust across treatments, with the largest efficiency gains observed when information is accurate, but meaningful gains persist even with high inaccuracy. The mechanism is reputational concern and discrimination, not direct costly punishment. The study provides direct evidence that non-monetary, reputation-based social sanctions (gossip) can substitute for formal punishment in promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a reputational mechanism (gossip) in repeated trust-based interactions increases efficiency, even when the information is not perfectly accurate. For prediction tasks, the presence of a gossip/reputation channel should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a no-communication baseline, with the effect size depending on the accuracy of the information. However, the mechanism is not direct costly punishment, but rather indirect social sanctioning via reputation. The findings are most directly applicable to trust games and adjacent to public goods games, especially in settings where partner choice or discrimination is possible. The results suggest that even imperfect reputation systems can yield substantial efficiency gains, and that the absence of any reputational channel is particularly detrimental to efficiency.

- source: Nunney, S. J., van der Schalk, J., & Manstead, A. S. R. (2022). Emotion and intergroup cooperation: How verbal expressions of guilt, shame, and pride influence behavior in a social dilemma. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two lab experiments using an intergroup centipede game, the authors find that when a group expresses shame (or guilt) after behaving uncooperatively, the opposing group is more likely to cooperate in subsequent rounds, resulting in higher group payoffs (tickets won) and more prosocial behavior. In contrast, when a group expresses pride after uncooperative behavior, the opposing group is less likely to cooperate, resulting in lower payoffs and more costly punishment. The effect of emotion expression is causal (Study 2) and operates above and beyond the effect of prior behavior. The study does not use a standard public goods game or implement a formal punishment stage as in PGGs, but does include a costly punishment measure in Study 2.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that communication—specifically, the expression of self-conscious emotions—can significantly moderate the effect of prior uncooperative behavior on subsequent group efficiency in repeated, sequential social dilemmas. While not a standard PGG, the centipede game shares key features (sequential cooperation, group payoffs, potential for exploitation). The findings suggest that, in environments where communication is possible, expressions of shame or guilt can substitute for or augment formal punishment mechanisms in restoring cooperation and efficiency, while pride expressions can undermine them. However, the study does not manipulate punishment cost, technology, or other standard PGG design dimensions, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled PGG efficiency is limited. It does, however, highlight the importance of communication and emotional signaling as moderators of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Martin, J. M., Gonzalez, C., Juvina, I., & Lebiere, C. (2014). A Description-Experience Gap in Social Interactions: Information about Interdependence and Its Effects on Cooperation. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that providing more information about interdependence in a two-player, 200-round IPD increases both mutual cooperation and joint payoffs (efficiency). Four information conditions were tested: No-Info (players unaware of the other), Min-Info (aware of other but cannot see their actions), Mid-Info (can see other's actions and outcomes), and Max-Info (can also see the full payoff matrix). Joint performance (sum of points) was lowest in No-Info and Min-Info, higher in Mid-Info, and highest in Max-Info. The effect of information on performance is fully mediated by mutual cooperation. Punishment (defection after defection) is high in all conditions and does not vary with information. The results suggest that awareness and transparency of interdependence are key to achieving higher efficiency in repeated social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated dyadic social dilemmas structurally similar to the IPD, increasing the amount of information about interdependence (especially showing others' actions and the payoff matrix) leads to higher efficiency (joint payoff). For prediction, if a public-goods-like game allows players to see others' actions and/or the payoff structure, one should expect higher efficiency than in games where such information is hidden. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, simultaneous-move games without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but the mechanisms (reciprocation, not punishment) may generalize to other settings. The paper does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, nor does it include explicit costly punishment or reward options, so its direct predictive value is limited for games with those features.

- source: Deb, J., & González-Díaz, J. (2019). Enforcing social norms: Trust-building and community enforcement. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that, in large enough populations and with sufficiently patient players, efficient (cooperative) outcomes can be sustained in equilibrium in repeated anonymous random matching games, even when players only observe their own transactions and have no information about others' actions or identities. The mechanism involves a trust-building phase followed by community enforcement (contagion-based punishment). The set of achievable equilibrium payoffs includes those arbitrarily close to the efficient frontier, as long as the stage game has a strict Nash equilibrium and a one-sided incentive profile. The paper provides detailed conditions and mathematical proofs for when and how this is possible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated games with random matching, minimal information, and community enforcement (contagion-based punishment), the introduction of a punishment mechanism (in the form of Nash reversion triggered by observed deviations) can enable the group to achieve efficiency levels arbitrarily close to full cooperation, provided the population is large enough and players are sufficiently patient. The results are not specific to the public goods game but apply to a broad class of two-player games with certain properties. The findings suggest that, in environments with similar structure (random matching, minimal information, repeated play, and the possibility of community-wide punishment), enabling punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, even when direct monitoring is not possible. However, the results are theoretical and depend on equilibrium reasoning, not empirical observation.

- source: Raub, W., Buskens, V., & Frey, V. (2019). Strategic tie formation for long-term exchange relations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when actors can invest in strategic tie formation (i.e., commit to repeated interaction at a cost), and when the continuation probability and payoff structure are favorable, they will rationally choose to form ties and cooperate, resulting in higher group efficiency (payoff) than in the absence of such ties, where defection prevails. The efficiency gain from tie formation is explicitly quantified as the difference in equilibrium payoffs between mutual cooperation and mutual defection, net of tie formation costs. The model also generalizes to other social dilemma games (e.g., Trust Game) and discusses the role of institutional design in facilitating or hindering efficient outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling mechanisms for repeated interaction and voluntary commitment (analogous to endogenous punishment or sanctioning institutions) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, compared to control conditions without such mechanisms. While the model is not a direct PGG or experimental study, its formal results on equilibrium payoffs, tie formation, and cooperation are highly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in repeated, two-player social dilemmas with commitment options. The explicit mapping of efficiency gains to game parameters (payoff structure, continuation probability, cost of commitment) can inform predictions about the impact of punishment-like or commitment-enabling design features in related experimental games.

- source: Hilbe, C., Traulsen, A., & Sigmund, K. (2015). Partners or rivals? Strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper provides a comprehensive theoretical analysis of memory-one strategies in the IPD, showing which strategies can enforce mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency), competitive advantage, or linear payoff relationships (ZD strategies). It proves that partner strategies (including Grim and Tit-for-Tat under certain conditions) can enforce mutual cooperation, and that generous ZD strategies can promote cooperation by aligning payoffs. The existence and effectiveness of these strategies depend on the discount factor (patience) and the payoff matrix. The paper does not analyze the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature, but it shows that strategies implementing contingent punishment (e.g., Grim) are critical for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how strategy design in repeated games can enforce efficient outcomes, but it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature in public goods games. Its results are most relevant for two-player repeated games (IPD) and show that strategies implementing contingent punishment (like Grim or Tit-for-Tat) are necessary for sustaining cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, the paper suggests that the ability to implement credible punishment (via strategy) is essential for efficiency, but it does not address institutional punishment mechanisms or group settings directly. Thus, its value is mainly theoretical and mechanistic, not empirical or directly predictive for PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Rob, R., & Yang, H. X. (2010). Long-term relationships as safeguards. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous relationship formation and dissolution can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in a repeated prisoner's dilemma, but only if the fraction of always-defect (bad) types is neither too low nor too high. When the fraction of bad types is in an intermediate range, a 'good equilibrium' exists in which strategic players cooperate, and social welfare is maximized at the lower end of this range. If the fraction of bad types is too low, there is no credible punishment for defection, and cooperation unravels. If too high, the probability of forming a cooperative relationship is too low, and defection dominates. The model also characterizes mixed strategy equilibria and shows that welfare can drop discontinuously as parameters cross critical thresholds. The main outcome is social welfare (efficiency), which is explicitly calculated as a function of the type distribution and model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that endogenous social structure (relationship formation and dissolution) can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency, but only under certain conditions on the population's type distribution. The efficiency of the 'treatment' (i.e., the equilibrium with endogenous punishment via exclusion) is maximized when the fraction of bad types is just high enough to make the threat of exclusion credible, but not so high as to make cooperation infeasible. The model does not map directly onto standard public goods games with explicit punishment options, but it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner choice or ostracism mechanisms. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is non-monotonic and highly sensitive to the underlying population structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the presence and credibility of punishment (even if not costly or explicit) can have strong, but non-linear, effects on efficiency.

- source: Fujiwara-Greve, T., Okuno-Fujiwara, M., & Suzuki, N. (2015). Efficiency may improve when defectors exist. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the most efficient Nash equilibrium is not the symmetric trust-building equilibrium (where all players initially defect to build trust), but rather a bimorphic equilibrium where some players always cooperate and others always defect and leave. This equilibrium is locally stable and, under a 'small stake' condition (when the temptation to defect is not too large), is more efficient than any equilibrium with only trust-building strategies. The efficiency gain comes from allowing a higher share of cooperators, even though some are exploited by defectors. The paper provides explicit conditions for when this efficiency dominance holds and characterizes the parameter regions where the presence of defectors improves efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the introduction of unconditional defectors can increase equilibrium efficiency compared to trust-building (punishment-based) equilibria, especially when the temptation to defect is not too large (small stake condition) and the effective discount factor (survival rate) is high. The efficiency of the equilibrium is explicitly characterized as a function of the payoff parameters and the share of cooperators/defectors. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment, it is highly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner selection and limited punishment options. The results suggest that, under certain conditions, the presence of defectors can be efficiency-improving, and that efficiency does not always monotonically increase with the strength or prevalence of punishment strategies.

- source: Lipman, B. L., & Wang, R. Q. (2000). Switching costs in frequently repeated games. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that adding small switching costs to frequently repeated games can overturn standard results from finitely repeated game theory. In the Prisoners' Dilemma, switching costs can enable cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) if the incentive to punish is greater than the incentive to cheat, but can also make cooperation impossible if the reverse is true. In coordination games, switching costs can make commitment to efficient outcomes credible, sometimes leading to unique, efficient equilibria. In some games, switching costs allow for more complex credible threats, which can result in equilibrium payoffs above Stackelberg levels for some players. The overall effect of switching costs on efficiency is thus mixed and highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, switching costs that make threats and commitments credible) on efficiency in repeated games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure and the relative size of incentives to cooperate, cheat, or punish. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment (or switching costs) on efficiency cannot be determined solely from the presence of punishment, but must also account for the specific incentive structure of the game. In some cases, enabling punishment (via switching costs) increases efficiency by sustaining cooperation; in others, it has no effect or even reduces efficiency by making punishment non-credible. The results are not directly in public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for settings where punishment credibility and switching costs are relevant.

- source: Ghachem, M. (2016). The institution as a blunt instrument: Cooperation through imperfect observability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that institutional tagging, even when imperfect, can substantially increase the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in social dilemmas like the Prisoner's Dilemma. The key mechanism is that tagged cooperators are more likely to be chosen for interaction, leading to non-uniform interaction rates that favor cooperators. The model provides analytical conditions under which cooperation is favored by selection, is risk dominant, or is more abundant in the mutation-selection equilibrium. The positive effect on efficiency is robust to moderate costs of tag production and search. The effect is highly sensitive to the institution's error rates, especially the rate at which defectors are mistakenly tagged as cooperators. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but institutional tagging serves a similar function by enabling partner selection based on observed tags.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional mechanisms which increase the observability of cooperation (even imperfectly) can increase group efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the findings are adjacent and likely transferable to PGG-like settings where partner choice or reputation is possible. The model suggests that, in environments where institutions can credibly tag cooperators and individuals can use these tags to select partners, enabling such mechanisms will increase efficiency relative to a control with no tagging. The effect is stronger when the institution is more accurate (especially in avoiding false positives for defectors), when more of the population is screened, and when individuals can search more for partners. The results are less directly applicable to settings where punishment is the main intervention, but the underlying logic (institutional information enabling discrimination) is relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like or reputation-based interventions.

- source: Kraines, D. P., & Kraines, V. Y. (2000). Natural selection of memory-one strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
