  findings: The paper finds that, in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with memory-one strategies and evolutionary dynamics, the population tends to evolve toward a small set of stable strategies. The Pavlov (Win-Stay Lose-Switch) strategy is particularly robust, achieving high average payoffs and being resistant to invasion by other strategies, especially in the presence of noise. The analysis partitions the space of strategies into basins of attraction: cooperative (high payoff), defecting (low payoff), alternating (zero payoff), and a small equilibrium plane. The size of these basins depends on the initial conditions and the payoff matrix. The paper also shows that strategies that combine trust with readiness to retaliate (but not excessive forgiveness) are most successful. While explicit punishment mechanisms are not modeled, the evolutionary success of strategies that retaliate after being exploited is highlighted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or explicit punishment institutions, it analyzes the efficiency (average payoff) of strategies in a closely related repeated social dilemma (the IPD) under evolutionary dynamics. The findings suggest that strategies incorporating contingent retaliation (analogous to punishment) can sustain high efficiency, but only if they avoid excessive forgiveness or spite. The mapping of strategy space to efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the likely efficiency of repeated games with different behavioral rules, especially regarding the role of contingent sanctions. However, the lack of explicit group structure, multi-player interactions, and institutional punishment limits direct transfer to public goods games.

- source: Shibayama, S. (2015). Academic commercialization and changing nature of academic cooperation. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the prevalence of commercial actors (who do not cooperate gratis) in an academic community undermines the reputation mechanism that sustains indirect reciprocity. This leads to a decline in unconditional cooperation, a rise in demand for direct rewards (e.g., coauthorship, barter), and an increase in abstention from cooperation. The overall effect is a reduction in group efficiency and mean payoff, as the system moves away from the collectively optimal state. Centralized rewarding can partially restore cooperation and efficiency, but only if the reward is carefully calibrated; excessive rewards can backfire by incentivizing defectors. The model provides formal predictions and phase diagrams showing how the basin of attraction for efficient cooperation shrinks as commercialization increases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and design of punishment, reward, and reputation mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While not a direct PGG or lab experiment, the model is closely related and analyzes efficiency outcomes as a function of game design parameters (e.g., cost/benefit of cooperation, reward size, reputation availability, prevalence of defectors). For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling effective punishment or reward (especially centralized, carefully sized rewards) can increase efficiency, but that the presence of many defectors or commercial actors will reduce the effectiveness of reputation-based cooperation and lower efficiency. The model also highlights the importance of the matching rate for rewards and the availability of reputation information. However, the absence of empirical or experimental data means that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Masuda, N. (2012). Ingroup favoritism and intergroup cooperation under indirect reciprocity based on group reputation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of cooperation in group-structured indirect reciprocity games depends critically on the social norm used for reputation assignment and the frequency of intragroup versus intergroup interactions. Full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency, with payoff b-c per round) is stable only when all observers use a 'serious' social norm (standing or judging) for all types of interactions. If observers use double standards (different norms for ingroup and outgroup interactions), then ingroup favoritism emerges, leading to lower efficiency (payoff reduced by the fraction of intergroup interactions). The key condition for any cooperation to be stable is b*r_in > c, where r_in is the probability of intragroup interaction. The model shows that changing the social norm (e.g., forcing consistency across interaction types) can shift the equilibrium from inefficient ingroup favoritism to efficient full cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but reputation loss/gain serves a similar function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with group structure. It shows that the efficiency outcome (full cooperation, partial, or ingroup favoritism) is determined by the social norm for reputation assignment and the structure of interactions (r_in). In the absence of explicit punishment or reward, but with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, efficiency is maximized only if the social norm is consistent and 'serious' (standing/judging) across all interaction types. If the game design allows or encourages double standards (different norms for ingroup/outgroup), expect lower efficiency due to ingroup favoritism. The model's efficiency results are directly parameterized by the benefit-to-cost ratio and the frequency of intragroup interaction, which can be mapped to MPCR and group structure in PGG-like games. However, the absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most applicable to games where reputation is the main enforcement mechanism.

- source: Kayser, K., & Lampert, A. (2021). Survivor's dilemma: The evolution of cooperation in volatile environments. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in the survivor's dilemma, cooperation can be evolutionarily stable if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and/or environmental volatility is low. In these cases, mutual cooperation leads to higher expected lifespan (efficiency) for all. However, if the benefit-to-cost ratio is low or volatility is high, defection dominates and efficiency is lower. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, indirect punishment occurs because defectors lose partners and thus reduce their own survival prospects. The findings are presented for both 2- and 3-player games, with detailed analysis of parameter regions where cooperation or defection is stable. The results are directly about efficiency (expected lifespan) and its dependence on game parameters, but do not address the effect of enabling or disabling explicit punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence about how efficiency (expected lifespan) in repeated social dilemmas depends on group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and environmental volatility. It does not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment, as no explicit punishment mechanism is present. However, it does show that indirect forms of 'punishment' (loss of cooperative partners) can sustain cooperation and efficiency under certain conditions. For prediction tasks involving explicit punishment in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation depend strongly on the benefit-to-cost ratio and the likelihood of future interaction (volatility), and that even without explicit punishment, cooperation can sometimes be stable. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment.

- source: Forges, F., & Horst, U. (2018). Sender-receiver games with cooperation. *Journal of Mathematical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in sender-receiver games with the possibility of credible punishment (a uniform punishment decision), there always exists an equilibrium (CTE) where both players cooperate and communicate, leading to expected payoffs for both that are at least as high as in the baseline game without communication, and often strictly higher. The existence of credible punishment is key: without it, such efficient equilibria may not exist. The paper provides examples where the introduction of a UPD enables partially revealing communication and joint decisions that strictly improve welfare for both players. The findings are theoretical and general, but the examples show that the efficiency gain from enabling credible punishment can be substantial.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling credible punishment (via a uniform punishment decision) in games with communication and cooperation can increase group efficiency/welfare relative to a no-punishment baseline. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the logic is adjacent: credible punishment for non-cooperation enables more efficient equilibria. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that, in environments where a credible punishment mechanism is available and can be used as a threat, enabling punishment should not decrease and may increase efficiency, sometimes strictly. The magnitude and details depend on the structure of the game and the nature of the punishment, but the direction is clear. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Kroumi, D. (2025). The role of selective extinction in promoting cooperation in a many demes model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that structuring a population into demes and introducing selective extinction—whereby groups with more defectors are more likely to go extinct—can dramatically increase the evolutionary success (fixation probability) of cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma. Without selective extinction (\alpha=0), defection is always favored, and cooperation cannot persist. As the selective extinction parameter \alpha increases, the fixation probability of cooperation increases, and that of defection decreases. There are explicit threshold values of \alpha (functions of game payoffs and demographic parameters) above which cooperation is favored by selection, and above which it is more likely to fix than defection. The model also shows how the baseline extinction rate (v) and the structure of payoffs (T-R, P-S) modulate these thresholds. The results are robust to several alternative life-cycle assumptions. The main outcome is that group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction) can overcome the individual-level advantage of defection and promote group-level efficiency (cooperation).
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in structured populations with group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction of defect-dominated groups), the efficiency of the system (measured by the fixation probability of cooperation and thus long-run group payoff) can be substantially increased by increasing the strength of selective extinction (\alpha). In the absence of such feedback, efficiency remains low (defection dominates). The model gives explicit formulas for the critical value of \alpha needed to shift the system from defection-dominated to cooperation-dominated, as a function of game payoffs and demographic parameters (deme size, number of demes, baseline extinction rate). While the model is not a direct public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, the selective extinction process is functionally analogous to a group-level sanction. Thus, the findings support the prediction that introducing or strengthening group-level sanctions (even if not individual punishment) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the control (no-sanction) efficiency is low.

- source: Garay, J., Csiszár, V., & Móri, T. F. (2014). Under multilevel selection: When shall you be neither spiteful nor envious?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations with multilevel selection, the evolutionary stability of strategies depends on the relative strength of group-level survival effects versus within-group competition. When group survival (due to predation or intergroup conflict) is highly sensitive to group size (i.e., total fecundity), strategies that maximize group fecundity (neutral or donator) are favored, leading to higher group efficiency. When group-level effects are weak, spiteful or envious strategies can dominate, reducing group efficiency. The model shows that increasing the benefit of donation or the cost of spite/envious behavior further promotes efficient, cooperative strategies. The results are robust to parameter changes and apply to both predation and intergroup conflict scenarios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in environments where group-level survival or payoff is highly sensitive to group size or total contribution, mechanisms that increase group efficiency (such as donation or cooperation) will be favored over spiteful or envious (punitive) strategies. While the model does not directly implement a public goods game with explicit punishment, it is adjacent: 'spiteful' and 'envious' strategies are analogous to costly punishment, and 'donator' is analogous to cooperation or reward. The findings suggest that increasing the importance of group-level outcomes (e.g., by increasing the marginal per-capita return or the impact of group payoff on survival) will make punishment less stable and cooperation more stable, thus increasing efficiency. However, the model is not a direct PGG and does not include explicit punishment technology or cost parameters as in standard PGG experiments.

- source: Bravo, G. (2011). Agents' beliefs and the evolution of institutions for common-pool resource management. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The key finding is that enabling agents to build management institutions in a CPR environment leads to much higher efficiency and group payoffs compared to scenarios where agents act only on individual beliefs. In the base (no-institution) model, payoffs are 12–35% of the optimum; with institution building, payoffs rise to 72–91% of the optimum. The effect is robust to changes in cost, resource abundance, and agent tolerance. The models suggest that the feedback loop between beliefs and institutions is critical: institutions prevent the selection of purely self-interested, over-exploiting agents, allowing cooperation and sustainable resource use to persist. The results are consistent with empirical findings from CPR experiments and field studies, though the models do not include explicit punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that the introduction of effective, collectively agreed-upon management institutions in CPR/public-goods-like environments can transform group efficiency from low (tragedy of the commons) to high (near-optimal). While the models do not include explicit punishment, the institution acts as a binding rule, functionally similar to enforced cooperation. The results suggest that, in designs where institution-building is possible and effective, one should predict a large positive jump in efficiency relative to the control. However, the absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means the findings are most directly relevant to institution-building or rule-setting treatments, not to punishment per se.

- source: Golubski, A. J., & Klausmeier, C. A. (2010). Control in mutualisms Combined implications of partner choice and bargaining roles. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to appropriate surplus (analogous to control over punishment/reward) and the ability to choose partners (partner choice) interact to determine the evolution of mutualism and the distribution of payoffs in mutualistic associations. When the guild with partner choice also receives the surplus, mutualistic strategies are favored and average payoffs are high. However, the presence of empty slots or high frequencies of exploiters can lead to cyclical dynamics where exploitative strategies temporarily increase, reducing average payoffs. The model predicts that partner choice is most effective at selecting against moderately exploitative partners, but highly exploitative partners can persist under some conditions. The overall effect on efficiency is mixed: partner choice and surplus control can increase efficiency, but not always, especially if exploiters are present or if association opportunities are limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (partner choice, surplus appropriation) can increase efficiency (group payoff) in mutualism-like bargaining games, especially when combined. However, the effect is not universally positive: the presence of exploiters and the structure of association opportunities (e.g., empty slots) can lead to reduced efficiency or cyclical dynamics. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling partner choice and surplus control generally increases efficiency, but the effect may be moderated or reversed if exploiters can persist or if association opportunities are limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its predictions should be transferred with caution, but it provides informative indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment-like mechanisms improve or fail to improve efficiency.

- source: McCabe, K. A., Rassenti, S. J., & Smith, V. L. (1998). Reciprocity, trust, and payoff privacy in extensive form bargaining. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in two-person extensive form bargaining games, the possibility of punishment (even if not rational in the subgame) increases the frequency of efficient, cooperative outcomes (SJM) compared to games where punishment is not possible. Repeated play with the same partner further increases cooperation and efficiency. When payoff information is private, cooperation and efficiency decline, and noncooperative (SP) outcomes predominate. The paper reports expected payoffs and frequencies of efficient outcomes by treatment, showing that punishment and repeated interaction both have positive effects on group efficiency. However, the effect of punishment is less pronounced than the effect of repeated interaction and complete information. The findings suggest that both punishment opportunities and the ability to build trust/reciprocity are important for achieving high efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While not a standard public goods game, the design is adjacent: it is a two-player extensive form game with opportunities for cooperation, defection, and punishment. The results show that enabling punishment increases the frequency of efficient outcomes and average payoffs, especially in single-play settings. Repeated interaction and complete information further enhance efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency relative to control, but the effect is moderated by information conditions and the possibility of repeated interaction. The paper supports the prediction that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when players can observe each other's payoffs and when repeated interaction is possible.

- source: Büyükboyaci, M., Karagözoglu, E., & Küçüksenel, S. (2025). Productive and Unproductive Effort Choices in Groups and Sharing: An Experimental Study. *Managerial and Decision Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the opportunity cost of unproductive investment (i.e., making productive investment more attractive) does not significantly increase productive investment rates. Subjects' own allocation choices do not significantly affect their giving in the distribution stage, but they give less to human partners who chose unproductive investment, indicating a punishment mechanism. This punishment is not applied when the unproductive choice is made by a computer. The size of the surplus (group payoff) is determined by the sum of productive investments, but the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or compare group payoffs across treatments. The main behavioral outcome is the use of distributive punishment in response to unproductive choices by humans.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment. The design is adjacent to a PGG: resource allocation between productive and unproductive uses, with endogenous surplus and a contest for control. There is no explicit punishment stage, but distributive punishment (giving less in the dictator game) serves a similar function. The main implication is that increasing the marginal return to productive investment (analogous to MPCR) does not necessarily increase productive investment or efficiency. Punishment (via lower giving) is selectively applied to human partners who make unproductive choices, but this does not feed back to increase productive investment rates. Thus, the presence of a punishment-like mechanism does not guarantee higher efficiency in this context. For prediction, this suggests that punishment may not always improve efficiency, especially if it is not structured as a direct, costly sanction and if it does not change initial contribution behavior.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2004). The logic of reprobation: assessment and action rules for indirect reciprocation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that indirect reciprocity models with assessment rules that distinguish justified from unjustified defections (STANDING) are more effective at sustaining cooperation and suppressing defectors than simple scoring rules, especially in the presence of errors. However, even simple scoring can support cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and players have enough interactions. The model demonstrates that the structure of the population, the number of observed interactions, and the error rates in action and perception all influence the stability of cooperation. Analytical results show that, under certain assumptions about the distribution of the number of rounds, stable mixtures of discriminating and non-discriminating altruists can exist, leading to high average payoffs. The findings suggest that costly punishment (refusing to help) can sustain cooperation, but is less robust than non-costly forms (STANDING).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus efficiency) depends on whether punishment is costly, the benefit-to-cost ratio, error rates, and the number of interactions. While the model is not a standard PGG, the parallels are strong: refusing to help is analogous to punishing defectors, and the group payoff is analogous to efficiency. The results imply that costly punishment can increase efficiency, but is less robust than non-costly punishment, and that the details of the assessment and action rules, as well as the game structure (e.g., number of rounds, observation probability), are important moderators. For prediction, the paper supports the view that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and errors are not too frequent, but the effect is sensitive to the costliness and implementation of punishment.

- source: Ma, Y. P., Gonçalves, S., Mignot, S., Nadal, J. P., & Gordon, M. B. (2009). Cycles of cooperation and free-riding in social systems. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, depending on parameters, communities can settle into fixed points (Nash equilibria) with stable mixtures of cooperators and free-riders, or exhibit persistent cycles in the fractions of cooperators and free-riders. The presence and width of the distribution of moral costs (idiosyncratic disutility for free-riding) are crucial for the emergence of cycles. Stronger social interactions (bandwagon effects) can stabilize high-cooperation equilibria and suppress cycles. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning actions, but models the deterrent to free-riding as an internalized moral cost. The efficiency or total surplus of the group is not directly compared across different institutional designs (e.g., with/without punishment), so the findings are not directly predictive for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model explicit punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency outcomes in the standard sense. However, it provides indirect evidence that internalized costs for free-riding (moral disapproval) can sustain cooperation and lead to complex group dynamics, including cycles. The model's structure and results may be informative for understanding how heterogeneity in social preferences and costs can affect cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative predictions for the effect of punishment interventions or for the mapping from control to treatment efficiency in PGGs.

- source: Aktipis, C. A. (2004). Know when to walk away: contingent movement and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a simple contingent movement rule ('Walk Away') enables cooperation to evolve and persist in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, outperforming more cognitively complex strategies like TFT and PAVLOV under many conditions. Walk Away is robust to invasion, can invade populations of defectors at lower initial frequencies than other strategies, and is most successful when movement costs, error rates, and agent density are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are measured by the evolutionary success of strategies (frequency in the population), not by direct group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that contingent movement (exit) can substitute for more complex memory-based or punishment-based strategies in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment, but shows that contingent movement (exit after defection) can promote cooperation and evolutionary success in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing agents to avoid defectors (analogous to exit or partner choice) can be highly effective, even without explicit punishment or memory. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding the role of partner choice, mobility, and spatial structure as alternatives or complements to punishment.

- source: Angourakis, A., Santos, J. I., Galán, J. M., & Balbo, A. L. (2015). Food for all: An agent-based model to explore the emergence and implications of cooperation for food storage. *Environmental Archaeology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that cooperation in food storage is most likely to emerge and persist when (1) cooperative storage is at least as efficient as household storage, (2) there is moderate to high dependency on storable food, and (3) social norms regulating access to the cooperative stock are neither too strict nor too lax. Too strict enforcement (high reciprocity requirement) can undermine cooperation by excluding agents who fail to contribute due to bad luck, while too lax enforcement allows free-riding to undermine the common stock. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, but shows that cooperation increases the probability of avoiding shortages and, under certain conditions, can lead to surplus. The findings suggest that moderate sanctioning (via access control) is optimal for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff in a public goods game with explicit punishment, it models a closely related common-pool resource dilemma where access to the common resource is conditional on past cooperation (a form of sanction). The results suggest that intermediate levels of sanctioning (not too strict, not too lax) maximize cooperation and system performance (in terms of shortage avoidance and potential surplus). For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment or exclusion mechanisms that are too harsh may reduce efficiency by discouraging recovery from bad luck, while too little enforcement allows free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of storage efficiency and dependency on the resource, which may map onto MPCR and baseline efficiency in public goods games. However, direct quantitative predictions of efficiency with and without punishment are not available from this model.

- source: Watson, R., Morgan, T. J. H., Kendal, R. L., van de Vyver, J., & Kendal, J. (2024). Investigating the effects of social information on spite in an online game. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants overwhelmingly chose altruism over spite when given a costly choice to increase or decrease an anonymous partner's payoff. Exposure to social information indicating others had behaved spitefully (vs. altruistically) reduced the degree of altruism, but did not substantially increase spiteful acts. The source of social information (majority vs. most successful) had no effect. The study does not report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the design is a one-shot, two-player, non-iterated game with a continuous choice from costly spite to costly altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated group games. The design is a one-shot, two-player, continuous-choice game where participants can pay to harm or help an anonymous partner, and the main outcome is a behavioral slider, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no repeated interaction, no group context, and no explicit public goods structure. The findings suggest that when both costly spite and costly altruism are available, spite is rare and social information can reduce altruism, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Rilling, J. K., Goldsmith, D. R., Glenn, A. L., Jairam, M. R., Elfenbein, H. A., Dagenais, J. E., Murdock, C. D., & Pagnoni, G. (2008). The neural correlates of the affective response to unreciprocated cooperation. *Neuropsychologia*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that unreciprocated cooperation (being defected on after cooperating) in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma elicits strong negative emotions and increased neural activity in regions associated with aversive and social pain (anterior insula, hippocampus, amygdala). These neural responses are stronger when the unreciprocated cooperation follows a cooperative move by the subject, suggesting sensitivity to perceived partner intent. Functional connectivity between the anterior insula and lateral orbitofrontal cortex predicts whether the subject will defect in the next round. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma games. It does, however, provide evidence that unreciprocated cooperation (partner defection) triggers strong negative affect and neural responses that bias subjects toward defection in subsequent rounds. This suggests a psychological mechanism by which cooperation can unravel in the absence of punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that, without punishment, negative experiences with defectors can lead to reduced cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or payoff, nor does it test the effect of introducing punishment.

- source: Manapat, M. L., Rand, D. G., Pawlowitsch, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2012). Stochastic evolutionary dynamics resolve the Traveler's Dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in the Traveler's Dilemma, evolutionary dynamics favor higher (more cooperative) claims when the reward/penalty R is small relative to the maximum claim M, and lower (less cooperative) claims when R is large. The model reproduces the empirical finding that average claims decrease as R increases. The analysis is focused on the distribution of claims, not on group efficiency or total payoff, and the Traveler's Dilemma is structurally different from public goods games: it is a two-player, competitive undercutting game with a specific bonus/penalty mechanism, not a multi-player contribution game. The findings are relevant for understanding how incentive structures (like R) shape cooperative behavior, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or punishment effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in a two-player social dilemma with a reward/penalty for undercutting (Traveler's Dilemma), increasing the size of the penalty/reward (R) leads to less cooperative behavior (lower claims). However, the game structure is not a public goods game, and the outcomes are not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the average claim. There is no direct manipulation or analysis of punishment in a PGG or direct efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform general intuitions about how incentive structures affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Terai, S., Kiyonari, T., Mifune, N., & Kanazawa, S. (2007). The social exchange heuristic - Managing errors in social exchange. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in one-shot PDGs is significantly higher when participants perceive themselves to be in an ongoing social exchange relationship (i.e., when the SEH is activated) compared to when they do not. In Experiment 1, participants who deliberated about the game and their partner before being assigned to a specific exchange relationship (delayed-play) cooperated less than those who made decisions immediately after being assigned a partner (immediate-play). In Experiment 2, participants who made their contribution decision after being told they were matched with a specific partner (partner-specified) contributed more than those who made their decision before being matched (partner-unspecified). The study attributes these effects to the activation of the SEH, which frames the game as an Assurance Game and increases conditional cooperation. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency. However, it does provide informative evidence that cues of social exchange and perceived interdependence can substantially increase cooperation rates in dyadic social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that psychological framing and the presence of cues indicating ongoing relationships may be important moderators of cooperation, but the paper does not address punishment or efficiency directly.

- source: Baik, K. H., Chowdhury, S. M., & Ramalingam, A. (2021). Group size and matching protocol in contests. *Canadian Journal of Economics-revue Canadienne D Economique*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Tullock contests, partner matching (fixed pairs) leads to lower bids and less overbidding compared to random stranger matching, but only in two-player groups. In three-player groups, matching protocol has no significant effect on bids or overbidding. Bid dispersion is also lower in smaller groups. The results suggest that identifiability and repeated interaction facilitate tacit collusion in small groups, reducing wasteful overbidding. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral measures (bids and overbidding) rather than direct efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in contest-like environments, especially regarding the effects of group size and repeated interaction (partner matching). While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the finding that partner matching reduces overbidding in two-player groups suggests that efficiency losses from overbidding may be mitigated in such settings. For prediction tasks, the results imply that small group size and repeated interaction can reduce wasteful resource expenditure (overbidding), potentially increasing efficiency, but this is inferred from bid behavior rather than direct efficiency measurement. The absence of punishment or public goods framing limits direct transfer to PGG-like environments.

- source: Müller-Leinss, J. M., Enzi, B., Flasbeck, V., & Brüne, M. (2018). Retaliation or selfishness? An rTMS investigation of the role of the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex in prosocial motives. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases both retaliation against previously unfair opponents and selfish behavior (less fairness) towards previously fair opponents in a Dictator Game following an Ultimatum Game. The manipulation did not affect rejection rates in the UG. The findings suggest the right DLPFC is involved in controlling selfish motives, but the design does not distinguish between retaliation and pure selfishness. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or direct punishment paradigm.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent: it uses sequential UG and DG with role reversal and rTMS manipulation, focusing on individual-level retaliation/selfishness rather than group-level cooperation or efficiency. There is no manipulation of punishment institutions, costs, or group payoff structure as in PGGs. The main outcomes are behavioral (fair/unfair offers), not efficiency or payoff. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for the prediction task, except as context for the neural basis of retaliation/selfishness in economic games.

- source: Townsend, C., Aktipis, A., Balliet, D., & Cronk, L. (2020). Generosity among the Ik of Uganda. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that Ik participants in unframed Dictator Games give on average 23.3% of their endowment, which is within the expected range for similar societies. Framing the game to invoke both need and supernatural punishment significantly increases giving (to 43.2%), while either frame alone does not have a significant effect. The results challenge the claim that the Ik are uniquely selfish and show that cultural norms and beliefs can increase generosity. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not include punishment as a costly sanctioning option, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a Dictator Game, not a PGG or its variant, and the 'punishment' manipulation is a framing invoking supernatural punishment, not a real, costly, player-administered sanction. The outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The findings may be weakly informative for understanding how cultural beliefs about punishment can increase generosity, but they do not provide evidence about the efficiency effects of actual punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bigoni, M., Potters, J., & Spagnolo, G. (2019). Frequency of interaction, communication and collusion: an experiment. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that, without communication, cooperation (collusion) rates are low and not significantly affected by the frequency of interaction (flexibility). When pre-play communication is allowed, cooperation rates increase dramatically across all treatments, and the non-monotonic effect of flexibility predicted by theory (higher cooperation at intermediate flexibility) becomes more apparent, but is still secondary to the effect of communication. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and there is no explicit punishment mechanism; instead, punishment is implemented via Nash reversion (endogenous). The findings suggest that communication is a much stronger facilitator of cooperation than flexibility in this setting with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that communication (chat) has a very strong positive effect on cooperation rates in a repeated game with imperfect monitoring, even in the absence of explicit punishment. However, because the game is a Cournot duopoly (not a public goods game), there is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that enabling communication can greatly increase cooperation, but the effect of flexibility (frequency of interaction) is non-monotonic and only emerges when communication is present. The results do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but suggest that communication is a key moderator.

- source: Stiff, C. (2008). Are They Bothered? How the Opportunity to Damage a Partner's Reputation Influences Giving Behavior in a Trust Game. *Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The opportunity to leave feedback (damage a partner's reputation) increases the amount participants are willing to give in a one-shot trust game, but only when the partner is perceived to care about their reputation. This effect is strongest when the partner's future interaction group consists of strangers rather than friends. The effect of feedback opportunity and group familiarity on giving is mediated by the participant's perceived control over the partner's reputation. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or total welfare, and the design is a dyadic trust game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based indirect sanctions (opportunity to leave feedback) can increase cooperative behavior (giving/trust) in dyadic trust games, especially when the target is reputation-sensitive. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of reputation-based sanctions depends on the perceived concern for reputation and the social context of future interactions. For prediction tasks, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that indirect, reputation-based sanctions can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency effects or direct evidence for public goods games with explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., & Tsujimoto, M. (2010). Evolution of cooperation in rotating indivisible goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a rotating indivisible goods game (a model of ROSCAs), cooperation collapses unless both a peer selection rule (based on reputation) and a forfeiture rule (excluding non-contributors from receiving goods) are present. Peer selection alone or forfeiture alone is insufficient. The model tracks the evolution of cooperation probabilities before and after receiving goods, showing that only the combination of both rules leads to sustained high cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that in a public-goods-adjacent environment with indivisible goods, the presence of both peer selection (reputation-based group formation) and a forfeiture rule (exclusion of non-contributors) is necessary to sustain high cooperation rates. For prediction, this suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (here, exclusion and peer selection) may only be effective in combination, and that the structure of the game (indivisible goods, rotation, exclusion) is critical. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Chen, M. K., & Hauser, M. (2005). Modeling reciprocation and cooperation in primates: evidence for a punishing strategy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that tamarins' cooperative behavior in repeated food-exchange games is best described by 'punish-2' and 'punish-3' strategies, which are more punishing than standard tit-for-tat. These strategies require two or more consecutive cooperative acts to restart cooperation after a breakdown, leading to stable but sometimes brittle cooperation. The analysis uses maximum-likelihood fitting of a suite of behavioral models, showing that these punishing strategies account for over 90% of the posterior model weight. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all results are based on behavioral patterns (pulling rates and model fits).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that more punishing strategies (requiring multiple acts of cooperation to restore cooperation) can stabilize cooperation in repeated dyadic interactions. The findings may inform mechanistic expectations about how punishment-like strategies affect cooperation dynamics, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The game is not a standard PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2024). Adjustment of link weights based on tolerance promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reduce interaction intensity with neighbors who defect beyond a tolerance threshold increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, if the tolerance threshold is too high (i.e., individuals are too forgiving), cooperation is undermined. The effect is stronger when more individuals in the population are 'environmental adaptors' who adjust their interaction intensity. The main outcome is the equilibrium frequency of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are robust to changes in network size and initial cooperator fraction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about mechanisms that may promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, specifically through adaptive partner selection or ostracism based on tolerance for defection. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The results suggest that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce interaction with defectors (after a tolerance threshold) can increase cooperation rates, but excessive tolerance undermines this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not analyze punishment costs, efficiency, or public goods structures.

- source: Lyle, H. F. (2017). Volver a Nuestras Raices: The Reemergence and Adaptation of Traditional Forms of Andean Reciprocity. *Journal of Latin American and Caribbean Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of formal or institutionalized punishment, collective action in the community is maintained through a combination of symbolic capital (reputation for generosity, skill, and reliability) and indirect social exclusion (withholding support from free riders). Households that invest more in collective action have larger and more robust support networks, suggesting positive assortment among prosocial individuals. Free riding is tolerated to some extent, and there is no evidence of direct punishment or sanctions. The system is sustained by the interplay of modified traditional reciprocity practices (ayni and minga), which have adapted to local needs. There is no direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but the system appears stable and resilient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as there is no experimental manipulation of punishment, no formal sanctions, and no measurement of efficiency or group payoff. However, it does suggest that in real-world settings, cooperation can be maintained without formal punishment through social mechanisms like reputation and indirect exclusion. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful as contextual evidence that alternative mechanisms (symbolic capital, social networks) can substitute for formal punishment, but it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Pandula, N., Akcay, E., & Morsky, B. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with abductive reasoning. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that abductive reasoning in indirect reciprocity models can, under certain social norms and low error rates, promote higher levels of cooperation than non-reasoning models. For the Scoring norm, abductive reasoning enables stable cooperative states that are not possible without reasoning. For other norms (Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), abductive reasoning can outperform or underperform non-reasoning depending on the error rates and the degree of confirmation/falsification bias. Under the Shunning norm, abductive reasoning leads to only defection. The model is more robust to errors in observation/assessment than to errors in action. The main reported outcomes are the equilibrium frequencies of cooperative strategies and intended cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how reasoning about errors and reputation assignment can affect the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that the cognitive process for assigning reputations (abductive reasoning) and the social norm in use can be critical moderators of cooperation rates. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper is only adjacent: it does not model explicit PGGs, group-level payoffs, or direct costly punishment, but it does highlight the importance of error handling and norm choice for sustaining cooperation. Its findings may inform mechanistic priors about the robustness of cooperation to errors and the role of cognitive biases, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2006). A theory of reciprocity. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a psychological game-theoretic model of reciprocity, where individuals' utility depends on both material payoffs and the perceived kindness or unkindness of others' actions, which in turn depends on both outcomes and intentions. The model explains why people sometimes punish defectors or reward cooperators, even at a cost to themselves, and why the same material outcome can elicit different responses depending on the perceived intention behind it. The theory is shown to be consistent with a range of experimental findings, such as higher rejection rates for unfair offers in the ultimatum game, positive wage-effort relationships in gift-exchange, and conditional cooperation in public goods games. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding when and why individuals will punish or reward others in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It highlights the importance of both outcomes and intentions in driving reciprocal behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper offers conceptual guidance on the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment, but does not supply direct evidence or parameter values for efficiency outcomes. Its value is mainly in informing the types of behavioral responses to expect under different game structures, especially regarding the role of intentions and available alternatives.

- source: Giardini, F., & Conte, R. (2012). Gossip for social control in natural and artificial societies. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that gossip, understood as the spread of reputation (meta-evaluations with hidden sources), enables more efficient social control by reducing the costs and risks (especially retaliation) associated with punishing or excluding cheaters. Ethnographic evidence shows that gossip is structured to avoid direct responsibility and retaliation, while agent-based simulations demonstrate that reputation-based information exchange can maintain higher product quality and more robust partner selection even with many cheaters. However, these findings are in the context of market-like artificial societies and do not directly address efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that decentralized, reputation-based information exchange (gossip) can improve the effectiveness and reduce the costs of social control mechanisms, such as punishment or exclusion, in environments where cheating is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The agent-based simulation results suggest that reputation mechanisms can sustain higher system performance (e.g., product quality) under high cheating rates, but these are not directly translatable to PGG efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform mechanistic reasoning about the role of information and retaliation risk in punishment systems, but does not provide parameter-level evidence for the prediction task.

- source: West, S. A., Griffin, A. S., & Gardner, A. (2007). Social semantics: altruism, cooperation, mutualism, strong reciprocity and group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that much confusion in the literature on cooperation, altruism, and punishment arises from inconsistent or ambiguous use of key terms. It clarifies that in many models (including public goods games), behaviors labeled as 'altruistic' or 'strong reciprocity' may actually be mutually beneficial, providing direct fitness benefits to the actor as well as to the group. Punishment, often called 'altruistic punishment,' can also be selfish or spiteful, depending on the context. The paper argues that clear definitions and careful distinction between proximate and ultimate explanations are essential for progress in the field. It also notes that the group selection and kin selection frameworks are mathematically equivalent in most cases relevant to cooperation and public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers important theoretical context: it cautions that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on the underlying fitness consequences and the structure of the game. The paper suggests that punishment can increase cooperation and group benefit if it provides a direct or indirect fitness advantage, but the terminology used in the literature may obscure whether the effect is truly altruistic or mutually beneficial. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the need for precise definitions of efficiency and careful mapping of model assumptions to experimental designs. It also highlights that the effect of punishment may depend on whether it is costly, whether it increases group survival or productivity, and how benefits are distributed.

- source: Suratin, A., Utomo, S. W., Martono, D. N., & Mizuno, K. (2023). Indonesia's Renewable Natural Resource Management in the Low-Carbon Transition: A Conundrum in Changing Trajectories. *Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Indonesia's NRM practices are primarily allocation-focused and legal-normative, which limits their effectiveness in promoting sustainable, cooperative behavior. By integrating the PD/game theory approach into a socio-ecological governance framework, the authors argue that institutions can be designed to foster trust, reciprocity, and cooperation. They highlight that social punishment (e.g., social disapproval) may be more effective than financial sanctions in promoting norm compliance and cooperation, and that non-utilitarian values (e.g., stewardship, feelings of guilt) should be incorporated into NRM incentive structures. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment (especially social punishment) can increase cooperation in NRM dilemmas, and that institutional and value-based design features are important moderators. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or quantitative predictions of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. For downstream prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on whether it is social or financial, the presence of trust and reciprocity, and the integration of non-utilitarian values, but it does not provide direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting relevant mechanisms and moderators for consideration in empirical synthesis.

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). DEPENDENCE AND RISK - TRANSFORMING AND STRUCTURE OF SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the risk inherent in social exchange relations (mutual dependence) can be reduced by structuring transactions as negotiated (joint decision, binding agreements) or as serially dependent (allowing for contingent action and trust development). Negotiated transactions most directly transform dependence into interdependence, reducing risk and making outcomes more predictable. Serially dependent transactions (such as repeated reciprocal exchanges) also reduce risk, but less so, and rely more on emergent trust and norms. Generalized exchange (indirect reciprocity) is riskier and less amenable to these risk-reducing mechanisms. The paper suggests that these structural features influence the likelihood of stable cooperation, trust, and solidarity, but does not provide empirical evidence or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how structural features of social exchange (e.g., repeated interaction, negotiation, direct vs. generalized exchange) may moderate the risk of defection and the emergence of cooperation. While it does not address public goods games or punishment directly, it suggests that mechanisms which increase interdependence (such as repeated play or enforceable agreements) should improve group outcomes and stability. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game designs with more interdependence, repeated interaction, or enforceable agreements are likely to see higher efficiency or more stable cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence. The relevance to punishment is indirect: punishment is mentioned as a possible influence tactic in negotiation, but not as a central mechanism.

- source: Cordes, C., Elsner, W., Graebner, C., Heinrich, T., Henkel, J., Schwardt, H., Schwesinger, G., & Su, T. Y. (2021). The collapse of cooperation: the endogeneity of institutional break-up and its asymmetry with emergence. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in groups and societies is fragile and subject to collapse due to endogenous factors such as group growth, increasing complexity, cognitive limits, and the ceremonialization of institutions. Collapse is often sudden and asymmetric with respect to emergence, meaning that the conditions under which cooperation breaks down are not simply the reverse of those under which it emerges. Theoretical mechanisms include the role of group size (with a cognitive upper limit for informal cooperation), the importance of network structure (with clustered and heterogeneous networks supporting more robust cooperation), and the increasing costs of monitoring and punishment as cooperation declines. The paper also notes that high punishment cultures may be associated with lower cooperation and socio-economic thriving. However, all findings are theoretical or based on secondary synthesis; no new empirical or experimental data are presented.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group size, network structure, and the costs of monitoring and punishment may affect the emergence and collapse of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. - source: Gold, N. (2020). HOW SHOULD WE RECONCILE SELF-REGARDING AND PRO-SOCIAL MOTIVATIONS? A RENAISSANCE OF DAS ADAM SMITH PROBLEM. *Social Philosophy and Policy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper proposes that motivation crowding (the phenomenon where extrinsic incentives undermine intrinsic or pro-social motivations) can be explained by changes in the framing of a situation. When a task or interaction is framed in terms of monetary gain or punishment, individuals may shift from pro-social or intrinsic motivations to self-regarding ones, reducing cooperation or helpfulness. The essay reviews evidence from behavioral economics and psychology (e.g., dictator games, prisoner's dilemma, effects of fines and rewards) to support this mechanism. It recommends that institutional designers consider how incentives and frames interact, as poorly designed incentives can backfire by crowding out desirable motivations. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance on the importance of framing and motivation crowding for predicting the effects of punishment or incentives in social dilemmas. It suggests that introducing punishment or monetary incentives can sometimes reduce cooperation or pro-social behavior, especially if these incentives shift the frame from normative/moral to gain/self-interest. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze game design dimensions, it cannot be used directly to predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design features. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for negative or counterintuitive effects of punishment/reward mechanisms, especially when they alter the perceived frame of the game.

- source: Morris, A., & Cushman, F. (2018). A COMMON FRAMEWORK FOR THEORIES OF NORM COMPLIANCE. *Social Philosophy & Policy*.

  findings: The paper synthesizes and formalizes four major theories of norm compliance—planning (folk theory), habit, internalization, and choice set construction—within a reinforcement learning framework. It argues that norm compliance can arise from explicit planning over expected rewards (including punishment/reward), from habits formed by past reinforcement, from internalized intrinsic rewards for norm compliance, or from the exclusion of norm-violating actions from consideration. The paper reviews evidence from economic games (including public goods and ultimatum games) to illustrate the persistence of norm compliance even in the absence of external enforcement, but does not report new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. The framework highlights that the mechanisms by which punishment or sanctions affect behavior may differ (e.g., via planning, habit, or internalization), and that these mechanisms have different implications for the persistence and malleability of norm compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it is useful for understanding the possible cognitive mechanisms by which punishment, reward, and norm salience may influence cooperation and compliance. The framework suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether compliance is driven by planning (sensitive to incentives), habit (sensitive to past reinforcement), or internalization (less sensitive to external incentives). For prediction tasks, this implies that the design dimensions that affect the salience, frequency, and perceived legitimacy of punishment or reward may interact with these underlying mechanisms, but the paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence for such effects.

Buyer power over price/payments is weakly positively associated with willingness to cooperate, while buyer power over quality is negatively associated. Punishment is discussed as a theoretical solution to second-order collective action problems, but is not empirically measured or tested.

- source: Berger, J. (2021). Social Tipping Interventions Can Promote the Diffusion or Decay of Sustainable Consumption Norms in the Field. Evidence from a Quasi-Experimental Intervention Study. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
