  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing regular normative feedback about the prevalence of sustainable behavior (use of mugs) can trigger rapid norm change in the field, either increasing or decreasing sustainable behavior depending on the initial prevalence. In one cafeteria with a high initial rate of sustainable choices, the intervention led to a sharp increase in sustainable behavior, consistent with a social tipping dynamic. In another with a low initial rate, the same intervention led to a sharp decline (a 'boomerang effect'). Control conditions (appeal only or no intervention) showed little or no persistent change. The study does not report on efficiency, payoffs, or group welfare, and the outcome is strictly behavioral (proportion of sustainable choices).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The intervention is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The outcome is behavioral (norm adoption), not payoff-based. However, the study is relevant for understanding how feedback and norm interventions can shift group behavior in social dilemma contexts, and it highlights the importance of initial conditions for intervention success. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers only indirect, contextual evidence about norm change dynamics, not about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.

- source: Ohnuma, S., Hirose, Y., Karasawa, K., Yorifuji, K., & Sugiura, J. (2005). Why do residents accept a demanding rule?: Fairness and social benefit as determinants of approval of a recycling system. *Japanese Psychological Research*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that citizens' approval of a demanding, sanction-free recycling system is primarily determined by their perception of social benefit and procedural fairness. Preference for introducing strict rules (penalties, surveillance) is mainly driven by perceived outcome unfairness (i.e., the sense that some people are not complying and are not penalized). The system succeeded in reducing waste, but the paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff in a way directly comparable to public goods games. The findings suggest that emphasizing social benefit and procedural fairness can increase approval and cooperation even in the absence of incentives or sanctions, and that perceived unfairness can drive support for introducing punitive measures.

It suggests that in real-world social dilemma settings, approval and willingness to cooperate with demanding rules can be high even without punishment, if social benefit and procedural fairness are perceived as high. Preference for punishment or sanctions arises mainly from perceived unfairness in outcomes. However, because the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and does not manipulate or observe actual punishment regimes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the psychological determinants of support for punishment and cooperation in adjacent, real-world collective action problems.

- source: André, J. B., & Morin, O. (2011). Questioning the cultural evolution of altruism. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that models of cultural evolution can, in principle, allow for the spread of biologically altruistic behaviors (including costly punishment) among nonkin, but only because of maladaptive features of social learning such as conformist or payoff-biased imitation. These mechanisms do not change the fundamental selective pressures on altruism; rather, they introduce constraints or errors that allow maladaptive behaviors to persist. The paper critiques the literature for sometimes conflating mutualistic cooperation (which can be adaptive) with genuine altruism (which is maladaptive by definition), and for overstating the explanatory power of cultural group selection. The authors argue that the prevalence of genuine altruism in human societies is likely limited and always reflects some form of maladaptation, whether cognitive or cultural.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence relevant to predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer theoretical caution: the presence of punishment or helping behaviors in cultural evolutionary models does not guarantee group-level efficiency or welfare gains, as these behaviors may be maladaptive and persist only due to constraints in learning or imitation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the mere presence of punishment mechanisms (especially when costly) does not ensure improved efficiency, and that claims about the benefits of punishment should be scrutinized for underlying assumptions about adaptation and learning. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its direct value for quantitative prediction is limited.

- source: Bravo, G., & Tamburino, L. (2008). The evolution of trust in non-simultaneous exchange situations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The simulations show that in the Trust Game, average efficiency (measured as average payoff per interaction) is low when agents lack information about others' past behavior or when reputation effects are weak. Introducing strong reputation effects or increasing the spread of information about past behavior leads to a sharp increase in efficiency, with the system moving from a low-efficiency equilibrium (average payoff near 1) to a high-efficiency equilibrium (average payoff above 2, up to ~2.6 out of a maximum of 3). Direct reciprocity (repeated interaction with the same partner) can also increase efficiency, especially when information is incomplete. The transition between low and high efficiency is abrupt, with thin thresholds in the relevant parameters. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, 'punishment' is implemented as the refusal to trust low-reputation agents, which is individually rational and costless in this setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in Trust Game-like environments, the availability and reliability of information about others' past behavior (i.e., reputation mechanisms) are critical moderators of the effect of indirect reciprocity on efficiency. When reputation effects are strong and information is widely available, efficiency increases sharply. Direct reciprocity (repeated partners) can also boost efficiency, especially when information is incomplete. However, the model does not include explicit costly punishment or sanctioning mechanisms as in public goods games with punishment. Therefore, while the findings are highly informative about the role of information, reputation, and reciprocity in driving efficiency, they are only weakly informative about the effects of costly punishment per se. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper is most useful as a source of indirect evidence about the importance of information and reputation, rather than direct evidence about punishment.

- source: Shimura, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2018). Large group size promotes the evolution of cooperation in the mutual-aid game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in the mutual-aid game (an 'all for one' group helping structure), larger group size and more repeated interactions (rounds) both promote the evolution and stability of cooperation, as measured by the expected payoff of conditional cooperators relative to defectors. This is in contrast to the standard public goods game, where larger groups typically undermine cooperation. The effect is robust to implementation and perception errors, and is strongest under certain reputation assessment rules. The paper does not include punishment or costly sanctioning mechanisms; all incentives are mediated by reputation and conditional cooperation. The findings are directly about efficiency (payoff) outcomes, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in mutual-aid games with indirect reciprocity and reputation-based conditional cooperation (but no explicit punishment), increasing group size and number of rounds both increase the likelihood that cooperation (and thus high efficiency) will evolve and persist. The results are not directly transferable to standard public goods games with punishment, but they suggest that the structure of the game (e.g., 'all for one' vs 'all for all') and the presence of reputation-based mechanisms can strongly moderate the effect of group size and repetition on efficiency. Since punishment is not present, the paper does not inform the effect of enabling punishment per se, but it does provide a baseline for efficiency in a related class of games without punishment.

  findings: The study finds that allowing for fraternal (other-regarding) player types in spatial social dilemmas leads to the coexistence of egoist and fraternal strategies, often in spatially ordered patterns. The presence of fraternal players reduces the parameter region where only defectors survive, thus supporting higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and personality diversity can promote cooperation and mitigate the tragedy of the commons, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.

The findings suggest that, in environments where player types or preferences can vary, the introduction of pro-social types can have a positive effect on efficiency, analogous to the effect of some institutional interventions. For direct prediction of punishment effects in PGGs, this paper is only weakly informative, but it supports the general principle that mechanisms reducing pure self-interest can improve efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that prevention-focused individuals are more concerned about their reputation and, when exposed to subtle cues that reputation is at stake (watching eyes), increase their prosocial behavior (donations, dictator game giving). This effect is robust to controls for neuroticism and stress. Promotion focus does not show a similar effect. - source: Camera, G., Kim, J., & Arjona, D. R. (2025). Choice flexibility and long-run cooperation. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in indefinitely repeated helping games with groups of strangers, expanding the action set to allow partial cooperation (rather than only all-or-nothing) increases both cooperation and realized efficiency, but only when the payoff structure makes partial cooperation sufficiently rewarding. In the control (Baseline, binary choice), efficiency among strangers is much lower than in fixed pairs. Introducing flexible choices (Linear and Low treatments) raises efficiency among strangers by up to 50%, closing much of the gap with fixed pairs. The effect is not present in the High treatment (where partial cooperation is less rewarding) or in fixed pairs (where efficiency is already high). The mechanism appears to be that flexibility allows for trust-building and coordination on cooperation norms among strangers, shifting the distribution of player types toward more cooperative strategies. No punishment or sanctioning is present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in indefinitely repeated helping games without punishment, increasing the flexibility of contribution choices (moving from all-or-nothing to continuous/partial) can substantially increase efficiency among strangers, especially when partial cooperation is sufficiently rewarded. The effect is not observed in small, fixed pairs where efficiency is already high. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games that manipulate the all-or-nothing dimension, the payoff structure for partial cooperation, and group size. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency in control games (without punishment) as a function of action set flexibility and payoff structure.

- source: Andreoni, J., Kuhn, M. A., & Samuelson, L. (2019). Building rational cooperation on their own: Learning to start small. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a twice-played prisoners' dilemma, allowing players to endogenously choose the allocation of stakes between rounds leads to higher joint payoffs, especially as players learn to 'start small' (allocate more to the second round). The payoff-maximizing allocation is similar whether chosen endogenously or exogenously, but endogenous choice yields even higher payoffs, likely due to signaling effects. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design, and the results are not directly about public goods games but are adjacent in their relevance to cooperation and efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. However, it is highly informative about how endogenous structuring of game parameters (here, stake allocation) can enhance efficiency and cooperation, especially via signaling. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does suggest that endogenous institution formation can yield higher efficiency than exogenous imposition, a principle that may generalize to other game design features.

- source: Laird, R. A. (2018). Sequential interactions-in which one player plays first and another responds-promote cooperation in evolutionary-dynamical simulations of single-shot Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Sequential interactions in single-shot Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games allow for the emergence of conditional strategies, which can increase the average payoff of the population compared to simultaneous play. In the spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, sequential play greatly expands the parameter range (cost-benefit ratio) over which cooperation and higher payoffs are possible. In the Snowdrift game, sequential play leads to high average payoffs across the full parameter range. The effect is due to the structure of the game and the possibility of conditional responses, not to punishment, reward, or repeated interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it is highly informative about the effect of sequential versus simultaneous play in two-player social dilemmas (Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift), showing that sequential play can increase efficiency by enabling conditional strategies. Since the paper does not include punishment or sanctions, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding the role of game structure and timing in cooperation and efficiency.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Direct reciprocity on graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation via direct reciprocity is affected by the structure of the population (graph degree k) and the update rule. For birth-death and pairwise comparison updating, structured populations make it harder for cooperation to be evolutionarily stable, but easier for cooperators to be advantageous. For death-birth and imitation updating, structured populations always facilitate cooperation. The critical benefit-to-cost ratio for cooperation to be favored depends on the probability of repeated interaction (w) and the number of neighbors (k). Errors in strategy execution, which increase with the number of neighbors, make cooperation harder to sustain unless the network is sparse (small k). The paper does not consider punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it provides theoretical results on how network structure and repeated interaction affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoffs in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means it cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Its results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated dyadic games on networks, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2016). Imperfect information facilitates the evolution of reciprocity. *Mathematical Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of imperfect information on the evolution of reciprocity (and thus on group efficiency/payoff) depends on the structure of payoffs. When payoffs are linear, imperfect information always makes reciprocity (cooperation) less likely to evolve, reducing group efficiency. However, when payoffs are nonlinear (synergetic effects), imperfect information can sometimes make reciprocity more likely to evolve, increasing group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical, based on evolutionary stability conditions for tit-for-tat (TFT) versus always-defect (ALLD) strategies in the iterated prisoner's dilemma with mistakes and imperfect information. The results do not generalize directly to public goods games or to settings with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information structure and payoff nonlinearity can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in repeated dyadic games. However, it does not address public goods games, punishment, or sanctions, and thus is only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main lesson is that the effect of information and error structure on efficiency can be non-monotonic and depend on payoff nonlinearity, but this does not directly inform the effect of punishment in multi-player public goods games.

- source: Gros, C. (2022). Collective strategy condensation towards class-separated societies. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that adding an envy-based psychological component to agents' utility functions in a competitive resource allocation game leads to a phase transition: for low envy, agents play diverse pure and mixed strategies, but for high envy, a large fraction of agents adopt the same mixed strategy, forming a lower class. This transition is accompanied by a substantial drop in mean income (efficiency), indicating that envy is detrimental to group welfare. The results are robust to variations in model details and to heterogeneity in envy levels. The paper also estimates, using ultimatum game data, that real-world levels of envy are likely high enough to induce this class-stratified, low-efficiency regime.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that adding a psychological penalty for relative disadvantage (envy) to a competitive resource allocation game reduces group efficiency/welfare, especially beyond a critical threshold. While the game is not a public goods game or a punishment game, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where psychological or social comparison motives are present. However, the paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or public goods provision directly, so its predictive value is limited to adjacent cases where social comparison or envy-like terms are present in the utility function. The main actionable insight is that increasing the weight of social comparison (envy) reduces efficiency, even when the underlying game structure is otherwise unchanged.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the population, as encoded by the interaction and replacement graphs and their overlap, critically determines when cooperation can evolve in the Prisoner's Dilemma. For death-birth and imitation updating, cooperation can be favored if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold determined by the network structure. The results are derived for both finite and infinite populations, and are validated by numerical simulations. The paper does not consider punishment, rewards, or explicit public goods games, but provides general insights into how network structure affects the efficiency and prevalence of cooperation.

However, it offers theoretical results on how network structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoff in social dilemmas. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in games without punishment, the results on the threshold for cooperation (b/c > h g / l) may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency in structured populations. The absence of punishment or reward mechanisms means the paper cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Hilbe, C. (2011). Local Replicator Dynamics: A Simple Link Between Deterministic and Stochastic Models of Evolutionary Game Theory. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals interact and update strategies in finite groups (local replicator dynamics), the evolutionary outcome can be captured by a replicator equation with a modified payoff matrix. This approach allows the use of deterministic replicator dynamics to predict outcomes in finite populations, including the emergence of cooperation, risk aversion, and even the dominance of strategies that are not optimal in the classical sense. The model shows that group size has a significant effect: smaller groups can lead to the spread of spiteful or dominated strategies, while larger groups approximate the classical infinite-population results. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games directly, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how group size and local updating affect efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in general evolutionary games, but it does not include punishment mechanisms or analyze public goods games with or without punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. However, its theoretical insights on group size and local updating may be contextually informative for understanding the broader dynamics of efficiency in group-structured games.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2006). The replicator equation on graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that evolutionary game dynamics on regular graphs can be described by a replicator equation with a transformed payoff matrix, which incorporates local competition effects. For the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games, spatial structure (graph topology) can promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the update rule and game parameters, thereby affecting the equilibrium group payoff and efficiency. The framework is general and applies to any n-strategy game, but does not address punishment, reward, or explicit public goods games. The results are most relevant for understanding how population structure alters the efficiency and stability of cooperative outcomes in evolutionary games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical foundation for predicting how spatial structure (regular graphs) and update rules affect efficiency and group payoff in evolutionary games. However, it does not address punishment, reward, or public goods games directly, nor does it provide empirical estimates. For the downstream prediction task, the main value is in understanding that population structure can be mathematically mapped to a transformation of the payoff matrix, which in turn alters equilibrium efficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Kuwabaaa, K., Willer, R., Macy, M. W., Mashima, R., Terri, S., & Yamagishi, T. (2007). Culture, identity, and structure in social exchange: A web-based trust experiment in the United States and Japan. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that Japanese participants are more likely to form durable, committed exchange relations and are more trustworthy within those relations, while Americans are more likely to trust and interact with strangers. However, there is no significant difference in the amount entrusted (trust) between Americans and Japanese. The observed higher trustworthiness among Japanese is explained by their greater tendency to form committed relationships, not by culture per se. When controlling for relational commitment (mutuality, risk, provocation), nationality effects on trustworthiness disappear. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group welfare outcomes. Total earnings are reported but not the focus of analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The game is a repeated dyadic trust game, not a public goods game or close variant. The findings are relevant for understanding how relational structure and partner choice affect trust and trustworthiness, but not for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The only payoff-related outcomes are total earnings and commitment concentration, which are not analyzed in relation to any punishment treatment. Thus, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Charness, G., Du, N. H., & Yang, C. L. (2011). Trust and trustworthiness reputations in an investment game. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both direct-reciprocity (history of return) and indirect-reciprocity (history of trust) reputation systems significantly increase trust rates in a repeated investment game compared to a baseline with no reputation. Trustworthiness rates increase substantially only with direct-reciprocity reputation. The rate at which both trust and trustworthiness occur (i.e., both players cooperate in a pair) is much higher with direct-reciprocity reputation, but also significantly higher with indirect-reciprocity reputation than in the baseline. Average payoffs are higher in both reputation treatments, and in the indirect-reciprocity treatment, individuals who trust more tend to earn more on average. The results suggest that reputation systems can improve social outcomes even without explicit punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation systems—specifically, providing information about past trust or trustworthiness—can increase trust and improve efficiency-related outcomes in investment-game-like environments, even in the absence of punishment. While the design is not a public goods game, the investment game is a closely related social dilemma. The findings suggest that information-based interventions (reputation) can substitute for or complement punishment in promoting cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results imply that enabling reputation mechanisms (even without punishment) can yield large efficiency gains over baseline, especially when the reputation is for trustworthiness. However, the effect is weaker for indirect-reciprocity (trusting) reputation, as trustworthiness does not increase as much. The absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but they are informative about the power of information and reputation as alternative mechanisms.

- source: Barmettler, F., Fehr, E., & Zehnder, C. (2012). Big experimenter is watching you! Anonymity and prosocial behavior in the laboratory. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that ceteris-paribus variation in experimenter-subject anonymity (i.e., whether the experimenter can link decisions to subject identities) does not significantly affect prosocial behavior in the dictator, ultimatum, or trust games. Average transfers, offers, and repayments are very similar between single- and double-anonymous conditions, and none of the differences are statistically significant. The results are robust to alternative statistical specifications. The study concludes that experimenter-subject anonymity is not a major driver of prosocial behavior in these laboratory games. However, the study does not include public goods games or any form of punishment or sanctioning, so its direct relevance to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-game-like environments is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, provide informative context about the robustness of prosocial behavior to experimenter-subject anonymity in two-player allocation and trust games. Since no punishment, sanctioning, or public goods structure is present, the findings are only indirectly relevant. The main implication is that experimenter scrutiny (as manipulated here) is unlikely to confound observed prosocial behavior in lab settings, but this does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in multi-player public goods games.

- source: Huang, L. B., DeScioli, P., & Murad, Z. (2021). Pulling for the Team: Competition Between Political Partisans. *Evolutionary Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In deterministic contest games where participants can spend money to increase their team's chance of winning a prize, people exert significantly less effort when in teams (3-on-3 or best-of-3) than when competing individually (1-on-1), with about a 20% reduction in effort. This pattern holds both for arbitrarily assigned teams and for teams based on political partisanship, even under strong partisan cues and during a highly salient election period. The results support theories of shirking and free-riding in group settings and challenge the idea that group identity or partisanship alone is sufficient to mobilize greater costly effort. No evidence is provided about the effects of punishment, communication, or other mechanisms that might increase group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, group/team settings in contest-like environments lead to lower total effort (and thus lower group payoff/efficiency) compared to individual settings. The findings are adjacent to public goods games: the contest game is not a standard PGG, but the group incentive structure and the opportunity for free-riding are similar. The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is lower in group settings. However, the paper does not test punishment, so it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that group identity or partisanship alone is not sufficient to overcome shirking. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in the absence of punishment, group efficiency will be lower than in individual settings, and that adding group identity cues is unlikely to increase efficiency without additional mechanisms.

- source: Wakano, J. Y. (2007). Evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games with common resource dynamics. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured, anonymous, and obligate public goods games with resource regeneration and diffusion, cooperative strategies (farmers) can outcompete exploiters under certain parameter regimes, even when cooperation is costly (strong altruism). The mechanism is that cooperators can increase their population density and locally exhaust the benefits of cooperation, preventing exploiters from free-riding. However, the evolution of cooperation does not always improve the environmental resource level; sometimes, higher population density of cooperators leads to a degraded environment. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as an adjacent theoretical model showing that spatial structure and resource dynamics can enable cooperation without punishment, but it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency in the sense required for the prediction task. Its findings may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, but it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of benevolence (and thus group-level efficiency or welfare) in an open society depends on the interplay between insiders' tolerance, the assimilation rate of outsiders, and the network structure of interactions. As the fraction of outsiders increases, benevolence (measured by the fitness ratio R) generally decreases. If insiders' tolerance is too low or assimilation is too slow, the system can experience a sudden breakdown of cooperation (herd behavior), leading to antagonism and loss of mutual benefit. If tolerance and assimilation are balanced, a stable mutualistic equilibrium can be maintained. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it does provide indirect insight into how the withdrawal of benevolence (analogous to defection or exclusion) can destabilize cooperation and reduce group welfare. The model's outcomes are determined by tolerance and assimilation parameters, not by punishment or reward. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Haucap, J., Heldman, C., & Rau, H. A. (2024). Gender and cooperation in the presence of negative externalities. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that the presence of a negative externality (mutual cooperation harms a passive third party) reduces cooperation rates, especially among women. Women are less likely to cooperate when their cooperation harms others, and this is linked to higher guilt/shame aversion. Social information about the partner (gender, age, semesters studied) does not affect women's cooperation rates but reduces cooperation among men, especially when matched with a woman. There is no evidence of punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and psychological correlates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is adjacent to the target because it studies a prisoner's dilemma with a negative externality, but there is no punishment or sanction mechanism, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. The findings may inform expectations about gender differences in cooperation when externalities are present, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is all-or-nothing, one-shot, with no communication, and no feedback beyond social information. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to contextual insights about cooperation under negative externalities and gender, not about punishment or efficiency.

- source: Bicchieri, C., Dimant, E., Gächter, S., & Nosenzo, D. (2022). Social proximity and the erosion of norm compliance. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that observation of peer behavior leads to a decline in norm compliance (more taking from charity) when peers are anonymous, but this decline is halted when social proximity is made salient. The effect is driven by an asymmetry: participants respond strongly to observed norm violations but not to compliance unless social proximity is present. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the outcomes are individual-level behavioral choices, not group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that social proximity can substitute for punishment in stabilizing norm compliance, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it is informative about the dynamics of norm compliance in repeated settings with and without social proximity, and highlights that social context can moderate the erosion of pro-social behavior in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline norm compliance dynamics in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Corgnet, B., Espín, A. M., & Hernán-González, R. (2015). The cognitive basis of social behavior: cognitive reflection overrides antisocial but not always prosocial motives. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two lab studies (US and Spain), individuals with higher CRT scores are more likely to make choices consistent with 'mild' altruism (increasing others' payoffs at low/no cost), while those with lower CRT scores are more likely to act out of egalitarian or spiteful motives. The studies use non-strategic, anonymous allocation tasks (not public goods or social dilemma games) and do not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are directly measured or reported. The findings suggest that cognitive reflection is associated with a specific pattern of social preferences, but do not inform about the effects of punishment or efficiency in public goods contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a public goods or social dilemma design. The findings are about individual cognitive style and social motives in non-strategic allocation games. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Diekmann, A., Przepiorka, W., & Rauhut, H. (2015). Lifting the veil of ignorance: An experiment on the contagiousness of norm violations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when subjects are informed about the extent of others' norm violations (cheating in a dice game), their own rate of norm violation increases in subsequent rounds, compared to a control group with no such information. This effect is robust to whether the information is about an external group or the current session. The effect is not due to punishment, as the design eliminates any possibility of sanctions. The effect is moderated by subjects' prior beliefs: underestimators of norm violations increase cheating when informed, while overestimators may decrease it. The study provides evidence for the contagiousness of norm violations via conditional norm compliance, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as there is no punishment, no group payoff, and no public good. The findings are relevant for understanding how information about norm violations can erode compliance, but not for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and outcomes are adjacent to the prediction task, offering insight into norm dynamics but not into payoff-based outcomes or the role of sanctions.

- source: Liuzzi, D., & Vié, A. (2022). Staring at the Abyss: a neurocognitive grounded agent-based model of collective-risk social dilemma under the threat of environmental disaster. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The model shows that environmental awareness and cooperative behavior (abstaining from polluting) are most likely to emerge when the system is near environmental collapse (self-organized criticality). More resilient environments (higher natural decay or recovery) paradoxically lead to less awareness and more pollution, as agents internalize relaxed constraints. Network structure (centralization, density, correlation) and cognitive module combinations modulate the spread of awareness and pollution, but the model does not include payoffs, efficiency, or punishment. The findings are relevant for understanding the emergence of cooperation in collective-risk dilemmas, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

It may provide indirect context for how awareness and network structure affect cooperation, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Jeong, H. C., Oh, S. Y., Allen, B., & Nowak, M. A. (2014). Optional games on cycles and complete graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to opt out of the game (become loners) relaxes the social dilemma and can promote the abundance of cooperation, especially as the number of loner strategies increases. The effect of optionality and spatial structure (cycles vs. complete graphs) is analyzed, with the main result being that more loner strategies (or higher mutational bias toward loners) make cooperation more likely to be more abundant than defection. There is no evidence of synergy between optionality and spatial structure. The main dependent variable is the abundance (frequency) of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment or sanctions as a mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its results are only adjacent to the prediction task, as they concern the effect of optional participation (loner strategies) and spatial structure on the abundance of cooperation, not on efficiency or payoff. It should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The paper proposes that intense human cooperation and resource sharing, conceptualized as a 'regular hexagonal pyramid' of interconnected individuals, is the most effective way to reduce CO2 emissions and achieve sustainability. It applies this model to scenarios for 2030, suggesting that targeting individual high emitters and allowing low emitters to increase emissions is a fair and effective approach. The paper is entirely theoretical and does not present data or experimental results. There is no analysis of efficiency, payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions.

- source: Simpson, B., & Willer, R. (2008). Altruism and indirect reciprocity: The interaction of person and situation in prosocial behavior. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiments show that prosocial behavior in dictator games is strongly moderated by both individual social value orientation and the presence of reputational incentives. Egoists increase giving when their actions are public and reputational benefits are possible, but give little in private. Altruists give at similar (high) levels in both public and private conditions. In indirect reciprocity decisions, participants reward others' prosociality more when it occurred in private (without reputational incentives) than in public, with egoists showing a stronger discounting effect. Altruists both give and reciprocate more overall. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctioning, or group cooperation dilemmas, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides informative evidence about the role of reputational incentives and individual heterogeneity in prosocial behavior, which may be relevant for understanding mechanisms in environments where reputation or indirect reciprocity is salient. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, as it does not manipulate or report on punishment, efficiency, or public goods game structures.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2004). Strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that, contrary to previous consensus, strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups if groups persist for multiple generations. This is due to increased positive assortment (measured by the regression coefficient between actors and recipients) that arises after one or more generations within groups, even without kin selection, homogeneous groups, or population growth. The models show that smaller group size, higher benefit-to-cost ratio, and intermediate numbers of generations within groups favor the evolution of altruism. Stochasticity in group formation and population regulation can further affect the likelihood of altruism evolving. However, the models do not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its relevance is limited to providing theoretical context about the evolution of cooperation/altruism in group-structured populations, especially regarding the effects of group persistence, assortment, and stochasticity. It does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.

- source: Garfield, Z. H., Ringen, E. J., Buckner, W., Medupe, D., Wrangham, R. W., & Glowacki, L. (2023). Norm violations and punishments across human societies. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that evidence for punishment of norm violations is common across societies, with physical, material, and execution punishments each present in about a third of societies, and reputational punishments somewhat less common. The presence of reputational punishment is associated with egalitarianism and the absence of food storage, while material punishment is associated with food storage and animal husbandry. Execution punishment is moderately associated with social stratification. There is little evidence for strong phylogenetic (cultural history) effects, suggesting that punishment systems adapt quickly to local socioecology. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, nor does it use experimental economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is relevant for understanding the cross-cultural diversity of punishment systems and their socioecological correlates, but does not report on payoff-based outcomes or experimental manipulations of punishment. Its findings may be contextually informative for understanding the types of punishment that might arise in different socioecological settings, but it does not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in controlled game environments.

- source: Sánchez-Pagés, S., & Vorsatz, M. (2007). An experimental study of truth-telling in a sender-receiver game. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that senders tell the truth more often than predicted by equilibrium in both the baseline and punishment treatments, especially among subjects with strong procedural justice concerns. When punishment is possible, receivers punish deceptive senders at a substantial rate, especially when they trusted and were deceived. The presence of punishment increases trust by receivers, but has only a modest effect on aggregate truth-telling. When the potential gain from lying increases, excessive truth-telling disappears in the baseline but persists in the punishment treatment among 'moral' subjects. The main outcomes are rates of truth-telling, trust, and punishment, with only secondary discussion of payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. The game is a sender-receiver deception game, not a PGG or close variant. While it shows that punishment can increase norm compliance (truth-telling) and trust, it does not report efficiency or welfare as primary outcomes, and the structure is not comparable to PGGs. The main value is as adjacent evidence that punishment can increase norm compliance and trust in strategic settings, but not as a source for quantitative efficiency predictions in PGG-like environments.

- source: Clempner, J. B. (2018). A continuous-time Markov Stackelberg security game approach for reasoning about real patrol strategies. *International Journal of Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper presents a novel continuous-time Markov Stackelberg game model for security resource allocation, providing a method to compute equilibrium strategies that minimize average cost for defenders (leaders) and attackers (followers). The approach is shown to be computationally tractable and more realistic for real-world patrolling scenarios than previous discrete-time models. A numerical example demonstrates the convergence of strategies and the ability of the defender to eventually capture the attacker, but all results are theoretical or simulation-based and not tied to public goods or punishment in the sense of PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its relevance is limited to the general modeling of adversarial resource allocation and efficiency/cost outcomes in security games. The game structure, outcomes, and mechanisms differ substantially from PGGs with or without punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except possibly as very weak, adjacent evidence about the optimization of efficiency in adversarial settings.

- source: Hakimov, R., & Kajackaite, A. (2024). Breaking bad: Malfunctioning control institutions erode good behavior in a cheating game. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the mere announcement of a control institution, even if it is not enforced, crowds out intrinsic motivation for honesty and leads to more cheating than a trust-based (no control) environment. Strong control (with actual monitoring and punishment) does not increase overall honesty compared to no control, due to offsetting effects on the extensive and intensive margins of lying. The most detrimental effect on honesty occurs when a strong institution is announced but not enforced. These effects are robust to comparisons with a 'weak' institution (very low probability of control), which does not produce the same negative effect as a malfunctioning institution. The findings are about individual-level honesty and do not address group efficiency or public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers adjacent evidence that the announcement of unenforced punishment or control can crowd out intrinsic motivation and increase rule-breaking behavior, even more than having no punishment institution. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like environments, this suggests that unenforced or malfunctioning punishment institutions may reduce cooperation or efficiency more than the absence of punishment, but this is an indirect inference. The results are not directly transferable to group payoff or efficiency outcomes.
